Estimates for maximal functions associated to hypersurfaces in ℝ 3 {\mathbb{R}}^{3} with height h < 2 : h<2: Part II A geometric conjecture and its proof for generic 2-surfaces Thanks: 2020 Mathematical Subject Classification. 42B25 Thanks: Key words and phrases. Maximal operator, hypersurface, oscillatory integral, Newton diagram Thanks: We acknowledge the support for this work by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft under DFG-Grant MU 761/11-2.
Stefan Buschenhenke, Isroil A. Ikromov, Detlef Müller

Abstract
. In this article, we continue the study of L p L^{p} -boundedness of the maximal operator ℳ S {\mathcal{M}}_{S} associated to averages along isotropic dilates of a given, smooth hypersurface S S of finite type in 3-dimensional Euclidean space which satisfies a natural transversality condition. An essentially complete answer to this problem had been given about ten years ago by the last named two authors in joint work with M. Kempe for the case where the height h h of the given surface is at least two. The case where h < 2 , h<2, and where S S is contained in a sufficiently small neighborhood of a point x 0 ∈ S x^{0}\in S at which both principal curvatures vanish, had been treated in the first article (Part I) of this series. Here we continue the study of the case h < 2 , h<2, by assuming that exactly one of the principal curvatures of S S does not vanish at x 0 . x^{0}. Such surfaces exhibit singularities of type 𝒜 {\mathcal{A}} in the sense of Arnol’d’s classification. We distinguish between two sub-types 𝒜 − {\mathcal{A}}^{-} and 𝒜 + . {\mathcal{A}}^{+}. Under the assumption that S S is analytic, denoting by p c p_{c} the minimal Lebesgue exponent such that ℳ S {\mathcal{M}}_{S} is L p L^{p} -bounded for p > p c , p>p_{c}, we show that for sub-type 𝒜 − {\mathcal{A}}^{-} we have p c = max ⁡ { 3 / 2 , h } , p_{c}=\max\{3/2,h\}, whereas for surfaces of sub-type 𝒜 + {\mathcal{A}}^{+} which do not belong to an exceptional subclass 𝒜 e , {\mathcal{A}}^{e}, we have p c = max ⁡ { 3 / 2 , p e , h } . p_{c}=\max\{3/2,p_{e},h\}. Here, p e := 2 ​ n e / ( n e + 1 ) , p_{e}:=2n_{e}/(n_{e}+1), where n e n_{e} is a new quantity, called the effective multiplicity, which can be determined from Newton polyhedra associated to the given surface S . S. Our conjecture is that p c = max ⁡ { 3 / 2 , p e , h } p_{c}=\max\{3/2,p_{e},h\} also for surfaces of class 𝒜 e . {\mathcal{A}}^{e}. We also state a conjecture on how the critical exponent p c p_{c} might be determined by means of a geometric measure theoretic condition, which measures in some way the order of contact of arbitrary ellipsoids with S , S, even for hypersurfaces in arbitrary dimension, and show that this conjecture holds indeed true for all classes of 2-hypersurfaces S S for which we have gained an essentially complete understanding of ℳ S {\mathcal{M}}_{S} so far. For surfaces of type 𝒜 e , {\mathcal{A}}^{e}, we show that the methods devised in this paper allow at least to prove that p c ≤ max ⁡ { 3 / 2 , 2 ​ n / ( n + 1 ) } . p_{c}\leq\max\{3/2,2n/(n+1)\}. Our results lead in particular to a proof of a conjecture by Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger for arbitrary analytic 2-surfaces. The study of the afore-mentioned conjecture for surfaces of type 𝒜 e , {\mathcal{A}}^{e}, which bears amazing connections to combinations of cone multipliers with Fourier integral operators, will be left to the third paper in this series.



1.
Introduction

Let
S
be a smooth hypersurface in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and let
0\neq\rho\in C_{0}^{\infty}(S)
be a smooth non-negative function with compact
support. Consider the associated averaging operators
A_{t},t>0,

given by

A_{t}f(x):=\int_{S}f(x-ty)\rho(y)\,d{\sigma}(y),

where
d{\sigma}
denotes the surface measure on
S.
The associated
maximal operator is given by

{\mathcal{M}}f(x)={\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}f(x):=\sup_{t>0}|A_{t}f(x)|,\quad(x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

If
S
is compact and
\rho\equiv 1,
we also write
{\mathcal{M}}=:{\mathcal{M}}_{S}.

We shall be interested in the question of
L^{p}
-boundedness of
{\mathcal{M}},
i.e., we would like to determine the range of all
p\geq 1
such that

(1.1)

\|{\mathcal{M}}f(x)\|_{p}\leq C_{p}\|f\|_{p}\quad\text{for all}\ f\in{\mathcal{S}}.

We therefore define the critical exponent

\displaystyle p_{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\inf\{p\geq 1:\eqref{Mb}\text{ holds true}\},

so that (
1.1
) holds true when
p>p_{c},
but fails to be true for
p<p_{c}
(what happens when
p=p_{c}
is not captured by this critical exponent).

Clearly, since
\rho
has compact support, this problem can be localized to considering the contributions to
{\mathcal{M}}
by small neighborhoods of points
x^{0}\in S.

Fixing such a point
x^{0}\in S,
and assuming that
\rho(x^{0})\neq 0
(in order to exclude mitigating effects through the vanishing of our density
\rho
at
x^{0}
), let us define the following local critical exponent associated to this point:

\displaystyle p_{c}(x^{0})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\inf\{p\geq 1:\text{there is a neighborhood }U\text{ of }x^{0}\text{ such that }

\displaystyle\qquad\eqref{Mb}\text{ holds true whenever }\text{\rm supp\,}\rho\subset U\}.

Note that by testing
{\mathcal{M}}
on the characteristic function of the
unit ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d},
it is easy to see that a necessary condition
for
{\mathcal{M}}_{S}
to be bounded on
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is that
p>d/(d-1),
so that
p_{c}\geq d/(d-1),

provided the transversality Assumption
1.1
below is satisfied.

In 1976, E. M. Stein
[
S76
]
proved that, conversely,
if
S
is the Euclidean unit sphere in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\ d\geq 3,
then the
corresponding spherical maximal operator is bounded on
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

for every
p>d/(d-1).
The analogous result in dimension
d=2
was
later proven by J. Bourgain
[
Bou85
]
. The key property of
spheres which allows to prove such results is the non-vanishing of
the Gaussian curvature on spheres. These results became the
starting point for intensive studies of various classes of maximal
operators associated to subvarieties. Stein’s monograph

[
S93
]
is an excellent reference to many of these
developments.

Here, we continue our study of this question for maximal functions
{\mathcal{M}}
associated to analytic 2-hypersurfaces in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.
As in the preceding article
[
IKM10
]
, and the first article
[
BDIM19
]
of this series, we shall work under the following transversality assumption on
S:

Assumption 1.1

(Transversality)
.

The affine tangent plane
x+T_{x}S
to
S
through
x
does not pass
through the origin for every
x\in S.
Equivalently,

x\notin T_{x}S
for every
x\in S,
so that
0\notin S
and
x
is
transversal to
S
for every point
x\in S.

Let us now fix a point
x^{0}\in S.
We recall that the transversality
assumption allows us to find a linear change of coordinates in

{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
so that in the new coordinates

S
can locally be represented as the graph of a function
\phi,
and that the norm of
{\mathcal{M}}
when acting on
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
is invariant under such a linear change of coordinates.
More precisely, after applying a suitable linear change of coordinates to
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
we may assume that

x^{0}=(0,0,1),
and that within a sufficiently small neighborhood
U
of
x^{0},

S
is given
as the graph

U\cap S=\{(x_{1},x_{2},1+\phi(x_{1},x_{2})):(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\Omega}\}

of a smooth function
1+\phi
defined on an open neighborhood
{\Omega}

of
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and satisfying the conditions

(1.2)

\phi(0,0)=0,\,\nabla\phi(0,0)=0.

Moreover, assuming now that
\text{\rm supp\,}\rho\subset U,
the measure
\mu=\rho d{\sigma}
is then explicitly given by

\int f\,d\mu=\int f(x,1+\phi(x))\eta(x)\,dx,

with a smooth, non-negative bump function
\eta\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\Omega}),
and we may write for
(y,y_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}

A_{t}f(y,y_{3})=f*\mu_{t}(y,y_{3})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(y-tx,y_{3}-t(1+\phi(x)))\eta(x)\,dx,

where
\mu_{t}
denotes the norm preserving scaling of the measure

\mu
given by
\int f\,d\mu_{t}=\int f(tx,t(1+\phi(x)))\eta(x)\,dx.

Let us briefly recall some basic notions concerning Newton polyhedra - for further ones we refer to
[
BDIM19
]
, and
[
IM16
]
.
We begin by looking at the Taylor series

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})\sim\sum_{{\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2}=0}^{\infty}c_{{\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2}}x_{1}^{{\alpha}_{1}}x_{2}^{{\alpha}_{2}}

of
\phi
centered at the origin.
The set

{\mathcal{T}}(\phi):=\{({\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}:c_{{\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2}}=\frac{1}{{\alpha}_{1}!{\alpha}_{2}!}\partial_{1}^{{\alpha}_{1}}\partial_{2}^{{\alpha}_{2}}\phi(0,0)\neq 0\}{\@index{\@indexphrase[T]{${\mathcal{T}}(\phi)$ (Taylor support)}}}

will be called the
Taylor support
of
\phi
at
(0,0).
We shall always assume that the function
\phi
is of
finite type
at every point, i.e., that the associated graph
S
of
\phi
is of finite type. Since we are also assuming that
\phi(0,0)=0
and
\nabla\phi(0,0)=0,
the finite type assumption at the origin just means that

{\mathcal{T}}(\phi)\neq\emptyset.

The

Newton polyhedron

{\mathcal{N}}(\phi)
of
\phi
at the origin is
defined to be the convex hull of the union of all the quadrants

({\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2})+{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{+}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2},
with
({\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2})\in{\mathcal{T}}(\phi).

The
height
of
S
at the
point
x^{0}
is defined by
h(x^{0},S):=h(\phi),
where
h(\phi)
is
the height of
\phi
in the sense of Varchenko (which can be
computed by means of Newton polyhedra attached to
\phi
with respect to local coordinate systems). For the notions of height, and adaptedness of coordinates, we refer to
[
V76
]
and
[
IM11a
]
. The
height is invariant under affine linear changes of coordinates in
the ambient space
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.

We recall what is known on this question so far.

•

If
h(x^{0},S)\geq 2,
and if the density
\rho
is supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0},
then the condition
p>h(x^{0},S)
is sufficient for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be
L^{p}
-bounded, and if
\rho(x^{0})\neq 0,
this result is sharp (with the possible exception of the endpoint
p=h(x^{0},S),
when
S
is non-analytic) (see
[
IKM10
]
, and also
[
IU
]
for some classes of hypersurfaces in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
). For an alternative approach to some of these results based on ”damping” techniques, see also

[
Gr12
]
,
[
Gr13
]
.

•

If
h(x^{0},S)<2,
and if either both principal curvatures of
S
vanish at
x^{0},
or both do not vanish, then under the same kind of assumptions on
\rho
as before the condition
p>\max\{3/2,h(x^{0},S\})
is sufficient, and necessary too, for (
1.1
) to hold (see
[
BDIM19
]
).

•

If
h(x^{0},S)<2,
and if exactly one of the two principal curvatures of
S
at
x^{0}
vanishes, then it follows from Theorem 3.1 of
[
BDIM19
]
that we may assume that, in a suitable linear coordinate system (recall here that our problem is invariant under linear changes of coordinates!)), the functions
\phi
is of the form

(1.3)

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=b(x_{1},x_{2})(x_{2}-\psi(x_{1}))^{2}+b_{0}(x_{1}),

where
b,b_{0}
and
\psi
are smooth functions, and
b(0,0)\neq 0.

Moreover, either
\psi
is flat at
0,
or
\psi(x_{1})=x_{1}^{m}\omega(x_{1})
, where
{\omega}
is smooth with
{\omega}(0)\neq 0
and
m\geq 2.
We shall view the case where
\psi
is flat as the case where formally
m=\infty.

Moreover, the function
b_{0}
is of finite type at the origin and thus can be written as

b_{0}(x_{1})=x_{1}^{n}\beta(x_{1}),

where
n\geq 3
is a positive integer and
\beta
is a smooth function with
\beta(0)\neq 0.
Note here that if
b_{0}
were flat at the origin, then we would have
h(x^{0},S)=2
(compare the subsequent discussion), so this case cannot arise here.

This means that
\phi
has a
singularity of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}

in the sense of Arnol’d’s classification of singularities (cf.
[
AGV88
]
), with finite
n\geq 3.

We remark that a representation of
\phi
in the normal form (
1.3
) is possible if and only if the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are linearly adapted in the sense of
[
IM16
]
. Throughout this paper, let us therefore assume that our coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are linearly adapted.

The case
n=3
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}
-type singularities had already been treated in
[
BDIM19
]
(compare Theorem 7.1). In this case, under the same kind of assumptions on
\rho
as before, the condition
p>3/2
is sufficient, and necessary too, for (
1.1
) to hold.

These results clearly imply that, for all these classes of surfaces addressed so far, we have that

(1.4)

p_{c}(x^{0})=\max\{3/2,h\},

with
h:=h(x^{0},S).

Let us also remark that matters change drastically when the transversality assumption fails, as has been shown by E. Zimmermann in his doctoral thesis
[
Z5
]
. Zimmermann studied the case where the hypersurface
S
passes through the origin and proved, among other things, that for analytic
S
and
\text{\rm supp\,}\rho
sufficiently small, the condition
p>2
is always sufficient for the
L^{p}
- boundedness of
{\mathcal{M}}_{S}.

Under Assumption 1.1, however, what remains to be understood are the maximal functions associated to functions
\phi
of the form (
1.3
) that exhibit singularities of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1},
with
n\geq 4.
Their study will indeed present the most difficult challenges among all cases. In view of our discussion in Theorem 3.1 of
[
BDIM19
]
we shall distinguish the following two subcases, assuming that
\phi
given by (
1.3
) has a singularity of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1},n\geq 4
:

To begin with, let us recall from
[
BDIM19
]
when the coordinates
x
are adapted to
\phi
(assuming that
\phi
is represented in the normal form (
1.3
)).

Case of adapted coordinates.
The coordinates
x=(x_{1},x_{2})
are adapted to
\phi
if and only if
n\leq 2m
(with the understanding that
m:=\infty
if
\psi
is flat at the origin).

In this case the principal weight associated to the principal edge of the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi)
is given by
{\kappa}:=(1/n,1/2),
and

d(\phi)=h(\phi)=\frac{2n}{n+2},

where
d(\phi)
and
h(\phi)
denote the Newton distance and the height of
\phi
in the sense of Varchenko (see

[
IM16
]
, also for further notions used throughout this article).

Case of non-adapted coordinates.
The coordinates
x=(x_{1},x_{2})
are not adapted to
\phi
if and only if
n>2m.

In this case, adapted coordinates are given by
(y_{1},y_{2}):=(x_{1},x_{2}-x_{1}^{m}{\omega}(x_{1}),
and in these coordinates
\phi
is given by

\phi^{a}(y_{1},y_{2})=b(y_{1},y_{2}+y_{1}^{m}{\omega}(y_{1}))y_{2}^{2}+b_{0}(y_{1}).

Here, the principal weight is given by
{\kappa}:=(1/(2m),1/2),
and Newton distance and height are given by

d(\phi)=\frac{2m}{m+1},\qquad h(\phi)=\frac{2n}{n+2}.

In particular we see that
h(\phi)\leq 3/2
if and only if
n\leq 6.
·

As it turns out, for our purposes a slightly different distinction, namely between the cases where
n<2m,
and where
n\geq 2m,
becomes more natural.

Functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
.
By definition, this will be the case where
n<2m.

Using the notation from
[
IM16
]
, notice that this class of functions can equivalently be characterized by the following property of their Newton polyhedra, without any recourse to the normal form (
1.3
) (which will indeed not be of any real use in the study of this case).

Property
({\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}):

The principal face
\pi(\phi)
of the Newton polyhedron of
\phi
is the line segment
[(0,2),(n,0)]
with endpoints
(0,2)
and
(n,0),
and these are the only points of the Taylor support
{\mathcal{T}}(\phi)
of
\phi
which are on this line segment, i.e., the principal part
\phi_{\rm pr}
of
\phi
is of the form
c_{1}x_{2}^{2}+c_{2}x_{1}^{n},
with
c_{1}\neq 0\neq c_{2}.

Note that when
n\geq 3,
property
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
in particular implies that the coordinates
x
are adapted to
\phi
(compare Proposition 1.2 in
[
IM16
]
), that (
1.2
) holds true, and that the Hessian matrix of
\phi
at the origin is of the form

H\phi(0,0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0\\
0&{\partial}^{2}_{2}\phi(0,0)\\
\end{array}\right),\text{ where }\ {\partial}^{2}_{2}\phi(0,0)\neq 0.

Functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{-}
.
This will be the remaining case where
n\geq 2m.
This class can again be characterized just by means of Newton polyhedra: either the coordinates
x
are not adapted to
\phi
(this is the case where
n>2m
), or the principal face
\pi(\phi)
is contained in the line segment
[(0,2),(n,0)],
and besides the point
(0,2)
there is a point
P
different from the endpoints
(0,2)
and
(n,0)
which is contained in the Taylor support
{\mathcal{T}}(\phi).
This is the case where
n=2m
in the normal form (
1.3
), and here we have
P=(m,1).
Note also that in this particular case the endpoint
(n,0)
may or may not be in
{\mathcal{T}}(\phi).

Remark.
It turns out that the study of the maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}
for
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}
-type singularities requires very refined information on the resolution of singularities. For this reason, we shall assume in our main theorems that
\phi
is analytic (by this we mean “real analytic”).

1.1.
Case of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{-}

It is interesting to note that case
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{-},
which includes all cases where the coordinates
x
are not adapted to
\phi,
turns out to be “easier” to handle than case
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
. Indeed, it will be settled in a complete way (at least for analytic surfaces) in this paper by the following result:

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
S
is the graph of
1+\phi,
and accordingly
x^{0}=(0,0,1),
where
\phi
is analytic and of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{-}
. Then, if the density
\rho
is supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0},
the condition (
1.4
), i.e.,
p>\max\{3/2,h(x^{0},S)\},
is still sufficient for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be
L^{p}
-bounded, and if
\rho(x^{0})\neq 0,
it is also necessary.
In particular, here (
1.4
) still holds true.

1.2.
Case of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}

The new feature arising in this case is that, in contrast to all previous situations, it will no longer just be the “order of contact” of
S
at the point
x^{0}
with small balls, or slightly thickened hyperplanes, which basically determines the range of Lebesgue exponents
p
for which (
1.1
) holds true, but possibly also in some sense the “order of contact” with slightly thickened lines. Some instances of this kind of phenomenon had already been observed in articles by Nagel, Seeger, Wainger
[
NSeW93
]
, and Iosevich and Sawyer
[
ISa96
]
,
[
ISa97
]
,
[
ISaSe99
]
.

In order to formulate our conjecture for this case, and the results that we are able to prove in this paper towards this conjecture, we need to introduce a number of further quantities. So, let us assume that
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}.

Besides the
height
, we are here interested in a further characteristic quantity of the surface, which we shall call the “effective multiplicity”.

In order to define this notion, in a first step we decompose

(1.5)

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi(0,x_{2})+\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}),

and consider the function
\phi_{\rm red}
and its associated Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red}).

We then define the number
n^{x}_{e}=n^{x}_{e}(\phi)
by assuming that
(n^{x}_{e},1)
is the point at which the horizontal line
t_{2}=1
intersects the boundary of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red})
(see Figure
1
).

A look at (
1.3
) will reveal that the point
(n,0)
is a vertex of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red}),
and we denote by
{\gamma}_{1}
the (non-horizontal) edge of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red})
which has
(n,0)
as the right endpoint.

Note that this edge can possibly also be vertical, for instance if
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{2}^{2}\pm x_{1}^{n}.
We then choose the weight

{\kappa}^{e}=({\kappa}^{e}_{1},{\kappa}^{e}_{2})=(1/n,{\kappa}^{e}_{2})
with
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}\geq 0
so that the edge
{\gamma}_{1}
lies on the line
L_{1}:=\{t_{1},t_{2}):{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1\}.
Then
(n^{x}_{e},1)\in L_{1}
, which implies that (see Figure
1
)

(1.6)

n^{x}_{e}=\frac{1-{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}{{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}=(1-{\kappa}^{e}_{2})n.

Note that clearly
n^{x}_{e}\leq n.

For later use, let us also note that
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2.

Indeed, when passing from the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi)
to
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red}),
at least all of the points of the Taylor support
{\mathcal{T}}(\phi)
of
\phi
are removed from the
t_{2}
-axis, in particular the point
(0,2),
and since by property (
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
) there is also no point of
{\mathcal{T}}(\phi)
on the line segment with endpoints
(0,2)
and
(n,0),
we see that the line
L_{1}
must intersect the
t_{2}
-axis above
2,
i.e.,
1/{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>2,
if
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0,
or must be the vertical line passing through the point
(n,0),
if
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0.

Figure 1.
Effective Multiplicity

Next, somewhat in the spirit of Varchenko’s algorithm for constructing adapted coordinates for
\phi
(see, e.g.,
[
V76
]
,
[
IM11a
]
), we shall allow for local coordinate changes (“non-linear shears”) of the form

(1.7)

y_{1}:=x_{1}-{\alpha}(x_{2}),\quad y_{2}:=x_{2},

where
{\alpha}
is smooth and vanishes at
x_{2}=0.

Given such a coordinate system
y=(y_{1},y_{2}),
we express
\phi
in these coordinates by putting

\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2}):=\phi(y_{1}+{\alpha}(y_{2}),y_{2}).

Indeed, as will be discussed in more detail in Section
3
, we can even allow for a more general class of local coordinate changes near the origin than in (
1.7
), of the form

(1.8)

x_{1}:={\varphi}(y_{1},y_{2}),\quad x_{2}=y_{2},

where
{\varphi}
is smooth, vanishes at the origin and
{\partial}_{1}{\varphi}(0,0)\neq 0.
Such changes of coordinates will be called
admissible
. Here, we then put

\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2}):=\phi({\varphi}(y_{1},y_{2}),y_{2}).

If we work in the category of analytic functions, we may and shall assume that the above changes of coordinates are analytic too, i.e., that
{\alpha}
respectively
{\varphi}
are analytic.

Note:
The coordinates
(y_{1},y_{2})
are also adapted to
\tilde{\phi}.

Indeed, since
1/2>1/n,
one easily checks that property (
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
) is preserved by these kind of coordinate changes (
1.8
).

We next decompose again
\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})=\tilde{\phi}(0,y_{2})+\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})
as we did for
\phi
in (
1.5
), and compute for
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}
as in (
1.6
) the corresponding number
n_{e}^{y}=(1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})n,
where
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
is the weight corresponding to the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
with right endpoint
(n,0)
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}).
Finally, we define

n_{e}=n_{e}(\phi):=\sup\limits_{y}n_{e}^{y},

where the supremum is taken over all analytic local coordinate systems
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
of the form (
1.8
). Note also that
n_{e}\leq n.

We shall show in Section
3
that this supremum is indeed a maximum, i.e., there exists a coordinate system
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
such that
n_{e}^{y}=n_{e}.
This coordinate system will even be of the form (
1.7
), so that it would indeed have sufficed to take the supremum over all coordinate systems (
1.7
) in the definition of
n_{e}.
Any such coordinate system
y
with
n^{y}=n_{e}
will be called
line-adapted
to
\phi
(not to be confused with the notion of “linearly adapted” coordinate systems introduced in
[
IM16
]
!), and the quantity
n_{e}
will be called the
effective multiplicity
of
\phi.
The latter notion is motivated by the following:

Given
n_{e},
an important exponent for the study of the maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}
associated to
\phi
will be given by

p_{e}:=\frac{2n_{e}}{n_{e}+1}.

For instance, every function which has a singularity of type
A_{n-1}
can be written in suitable local coordinates in the normal form

\pm x_{2}^{2}\pm x_{1}^{n},
which is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}.
For this normal form, we have
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
and
n_{e}=n^{x}_{e}=(1-{\kappa}^{e}_{2})n=n.

Thus, the exponent
p_{e}
associated to
\phi
before is formally the same as for the normal form
\pm x_{2}^{2}\pm x_{1}^{n_{e}}
- this motivates our notion of effective multiplicity.

We remark that indeed also for
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{n-1}
-singularities it is possible to define (in a less direct way) the notion of effective multiplicity (see Remark
10.2
), so that this notion makes sense for any singularity of type

{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}.
However, we shall not really need to make use of this observation.

Remark 1.3
.

If
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+},
with
n\geq 4,
then
p_{e}>h(\phi).
Moreover, we always have
n<2n_{e},
in particular
n_{e}>2,
and
p_{e}\geq 3/2
if and only if
n_{e}\geq 3.

Indeed, we have
h=d={2n}/(n+2),
and
p_{e}\geq{2n^{x}_{e}}/(n^{x}_{e}+1).
And, a look at the Newton polyhedra of
\phi
and
\phi_{\rm red}
(cf. Figure
1
) shows that the horizontal line
t_{2}=1
intersects the principal face of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi)
at the
t_{1}
-coordinate
n/2<n_{e}^{x}\leq n_{e}.
But, this implies that

{2n_{e}}/(n_{e}+1)>h.
The remaining statements are immediate.

Let us present some examples of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
-type singularities. The first two examples show that the original coordinates
x
may in general not be line-adapted, and that the inequality
n_{e}\leq n
can indeed be strict:

Example 1.4
.

Let
\phi(x_{1},x_{2}):=x_{2}^{2}+(x_{1}+x_{2}^{\ell})^{n},
where
\ell\geq 2.

Then
\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1}+x_{2}^{\ell})^{n}-x_{2}^{n\ell},
so that
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=1/n\ell,
if
c\neq 0,
hence

n_{e}^{x}=(1-\frac{1}{n\ell})n=n-\frac{1}{\ell}.

However, in the coordinates
y_{1}:=x_{1}+x_{2}^{\ell},y_{2}:=x_{2},
we have
\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{2}^{2}+y_{1}^{n},
hence
n^{y}_{e}=n,
so that
n_{e}=n.
In particular, in contrast to the coordinates
x,
the coordinates
y
are line-adapted to
\phi
(which follows easily from Proposition
3.1
), and we have
p_{e}={2n}/(n+1).

Example 1.5
.

Let
\phi(x_{1},x_{2}):=(1+x_{1}^{\alpha}x_{2}^{\beta})x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{n},\qquad{\alpha},{\beta}\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Here, if
{\alpha}\geq 1,
then
\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}^{\alpha}x_{2}^{\beta}x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{n},
and if
{\alpha}=0,
then
\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}^{n}.
Thus one computes that
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=(n-{\alpha})/{n({\beta}+2)},
if
1\leq{\alpha}<n,
and
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=n,
if
{\alpha}\geq n.
Hence, by (
1.6
),
n_{e}^{x}=(n({\beta}+1)+{\alpha})/({\beta}+2),
if
1\leq{\alpha}<n,
and
n_{e}^{x}=n,
if
{\alpha}=0,
or
{\alpha}\geq n.
Moreover, it is easily seen that the coordinates
x
are already line-adapted (which follows again from Proposition
3.1
), and thus we have
n_{e}=n_{e}^{x}<n
iff
1\leq{\alpha}<n.
In particular,

p_{e}=2\big(n({\beta}+1)+{\alpha}\big)/\big(n({\beta}+1)+{\alpha}+{\beta}+2\big),
if
1\leq{\alpha}<n,
and
p_{e}=2n/(n+1),
if
{\alpha}=0,
or
{\alpha}\geq n.

Example 1.6
.

Let
\phi(x_{1},x_{2}):=\frac{1}{1-x_{1}}x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{n}.

In contrast to Example
1.5
, the graph of this function is convex for
|x_{1}|<1
when
n
is even.
Since

1/(1-x_{1})=1+x_{1}+O(x_{1}^{2}),
as in Example
1.5
we have
n_{e}^{x}=(n+1)/{2},
and the coordinates
x
are already line-adapted, so that
n_{e}=(n+1)/{2}
and
p_{e}=2(n+1)/(n+3).

Finally, for functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
let us put

\tilde{p}_{c}:=\max\{3/2,p_{e},h\}=\max\{3/2,p_{e}\},

where
h:=h(\phi)=h(x^{0},S).
Note that in view of Remark
1.3
we have indeed that
\tilde{p}_{c}=\max\{3/2,p_{e}\}.

The following result will be proven in Section
2
:

Proposition 1.7
.

Assume that
S
is the graph of
1+\phi,
and accordingly
x^{0}=(0,0,1),
where
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
. Then, if
\rho(x^{0})\neq 0,
the condition
p\geq\tilde{p}_{c}
is necessary for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be
L^{p}
-bounded.

Our main conjecture on the boundedness of the maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}
for singularities of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
states that the condition stated before is essentially also sufficient:

Conjecture 1
.

Assume that
S
is the graph of
1+\phi,
and accordingly
x^{0}=(0,0,1),
where
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}.
Then, if the density
\rho
is supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0},
the condition
p>\tilde{p}_{c}
is sufficient for
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}
to be
L^{p}
-bounded, so that indeed
p_{c}(x^{0})=\tilde{p}_{c}.

We shall show that for a large class of functions
\phi
of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
this conjecture indeed holds true.
To describe this class, let us assume that
(y_{1},y_{2})
is a line-adapted coordinate system in which the function
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
is represented by
\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2}).

Assuming first that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0,
let us decompose the function
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})=\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})-\tilde{\phi}(0,y_{2})
into

(1.9)

\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})=p(y_{1},y_{2})+\tilde{\phi}_{\rm err}(y_{1},y_{2}),

with a
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial
p
of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree 1 consisting of at least two monomial terms, with Taylor support on the first non-horizontal edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
of

{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}),
which is compact and has right endpoint
(n,0)
(and a remainder term
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm err}
consisting of terms of higher
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree).

Assume next that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0.
Then
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is vertical, and in this case
p(y_{1},y_{2})
may no longer be a polynomial, but of the form
y_{1}^{n}G(y_{1},y_{2}),
with
G
analytic and
G(0,0)\neq 0.

We shall be able to handle in this paper all functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
, except for functions from a class that we shall denote by

{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}.
To facilitate notation, let us assume without loss of generality henceforth that
\beta(0)=1
(see (
1.3
)).

The class of functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
.

By definition, these are those functions
\phi
from
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
which satisfy the following assumptions:

Assume that
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
is any line-adapted coordinate system. Then either
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
or
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0,
and the
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial
p(y_{1},y_{2})
satisfies the following two conditions:

(A1)

{\partial}_{y_{1}}^{2}p(y_{1},y_{2})
consists of at least two distinct monomial terms, one of them of course being
n(n-1)y_{1}^{n-2}.

(A2)

If
{\partial}_{y_{1}}^{2}p(y_{1},y_{2})
does not vanish of maximal possible order
n-2
along a real, non-trivial root of
{\partial}_{y_{1}}^{2}p(y_{1},y_{2});

more precisely,
{\partial}_{y_{1}}^{2}p(y_{1},y_{2})
is not of the form

(1.10)

{\partial}_{y_{1}}^{2}p(y_{1},y_{2})=n(n-1)(y_{1}-cy_{2}^{a})^{n-2},

with
c\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and integer exponent
a\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}.

On the exceptional class
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
.

We remark that if (
1.10
)
is
satisfied, i.e., if
\phi
is of class
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e},
then
{\partial}_{y_{1}}p
must be of the form

{\partial}_{y_{1}}p(y_{1},y_{2})=n(y_{1}-cy_{2}^{a})^{n-1}+c_{1}y_{2}^{a(n-1)},
with
c_{1}\neq 0,
for if
c_{1}=0,
then the coordinates
y
could not have been line-adapted by Corollary
3.5
. Consequently, we would have

p(y_{1},y_{2})=(y_{1}-cy_{2}^{a})^{n}+c_{1}y_{1}y_{2}^{a(n-1)}+c_{0}y_{2}^{an},\qquad\text{with}\ c_{1}\neq 0.

Note that we could here perform a change of coordinates
u_{1}:=y_{1}-cy_{2}^{a},u_{2}=y_{2},
so that in the new coordinates
u=(u_{1},u_{2}),
we would have

p(y_{1},y_{2})=u_{1}^{n}+c_{1}u_{1}u_{2}^{a(n-1)}+(c_{0}+cc_{1})u_{2}^{an},

where
a(n-1)\geq 3,
if
n\geq 3,
so that we may write
a(n-1)=\beta+2,
with
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}.
Since the last term depends on
u_{2}
only, if then we look at the function
\tilde{\tilde{\phi}}(u_{1},u_{2})
which represents
\phi
in these coordinates, we see that
\tilde{\tilde{\phi}}_{\rm red}(u_{1},u_{2})
is of the form

\tilde{\tilde{\phi}}_{\rm red}(u_{1},u_{2})=u_{1}^{n}+c_{1}u_{1}u_{2}^{\beta+2}+\tilde{\tilde{\phi}}_{\rm err}(u_{1},u_{2}),

and by the discussion in Section
3
it is easy to see that the coordinates
u
are line-adapted too. Up to the error term, we see that this is now of the form described in Example
1.5
, with
{\alpha}=1.

On the other hand, if we assume that
(A1)
is not satisfied, then we see that
p(y_{1},y_{2})
must be of the form

p(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}^{n}+c_{1}y_{1}y_{2}^{k},

with
c_{1}\neq 0,
and since
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2,
it is easily seen that
k\geq 2,
so that again

(1.11)

p(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}^{n}+c_{1}y_{1}y_{2}^{\beta+2},\qquad\text{with}\ \beta\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Thus, we see that
\phi
is of exceptional type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
if and only if there is a line-adapted coordinate system in which
p(y)
is of the form (
1.11
).

If we look at the examples, we thus see that the functions
\phi
in Example
1.5

are of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
if and only if
{\alpha}=1.
Another example of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
is Example
1.6
, which is a variant of the case
{\alpha}=1,\beta=0
in Example
1.5
in which
\phi
is even convex whenever
n\geq 2
is an even number.

Example 1.8
.

Let
\phi(x_{1},x_{2}):=x_{2}^{2}+(x_{1}+x_{2}^{\ell})x_{2}^{k}+(x_{1}+x_{2}^{\ell})^{n},
where
k\geq 2,\ell\geq 1
and
\ell(n-1)<k.

Here one checks that
{\kappa}_{1}^{e}=1/n,{\kappa}_{2}^{e}=1/n\ell.
If we pass to the admissible coordinates
(y_{1},y_{2})
with
y_{1}:=x_{1}+x_{2}^{\ell},y_{2}:=x_{2},
then
\phi
is represented by
\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{2}^{2}+y_{1}y_{2}^{k}+y_{1}^{n},
with
\tilde{\kappa}_{1}^{e}=1/n,\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=(n-1)/nk.
And, one easily verifies by means of Proposition
3.1
that the coordinates
(y_{1},y_{2})
are line-adapted to
\phi,
whereas the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are not, since
1/{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}.

Moreover,
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}y_{2}^{k}+y_{1}^{n},
which shows that
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}.

Our main result on functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
in this paper is

Theorem 1.9
.

Assume that
S
is the graph of
1+\phi,
and accordingly
x^{0}=(0,0,1),
where
\phi
is analytic and of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
. Then, if the density
\rho
is supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0},
the condition
p>\max\{3/2,p_{e}\}
is sufficient for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be
L^{p}
-bounded. Moreover, if
\rho(x^{0})\neq 0,
it is also necessary.
I.e., in this case we have
p_{c}(x^{0})=\max\{3/2,p_{e}\}.

We shall turn to the remaining exceptional class of functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
in the third paper of this series. However,
in the last Section
14
, we shall at least show that the condition
p>\max\{3/2,2n/(n+1)\}
is sufficient for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be
L^{p}
-bounded. In combination with all other results, this will lead to a proof of a conjecture by Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger for arbitrary analytic 2-surfaces (compare Subsection
2.2
).

1.3.
A more general conjecture in arbitrary dimension

Let us come back to more general hypersurfaces
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and their associated maximal operators
{\mathcal{M}}
as discussed at the beginning of this section. Assuming that
S
satisfies the transversality Assumption
1.1
near a given point
x^{0}\in S
and is contained in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0}
(as in the 3-dimensional case) we may assume that, after applying a suitable linear change of coordinates in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d},
we have
x^{0}=(0,1)
with respect to the splitting
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}={\mathbb{R}}^{d-1}\times{\mathbb{R}},
and that
S
is the graph

S=\{(x^{\prime},1+\phi(x^{\prime})):x^{\prime}\in{\Omega}\}

of a smooth function
1+\phi
defined on an small open neighborhood
{\Omega}

of
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-1}
satisfying

\phi(0)=0,\,\nabla\phi(0)=0.

By localizing to a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0},
we may also assume that

(1.12)

{\Omega}={\Omega}_{\varepsilon}=\{z^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-1}:|z^{\prime}|<{\varepsilon}\}\quad\text{and}\quad|\nabla\phi(z^{\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{10}\quad\text{for all }z^{\prime}\in{\Omega},

where
0<{\varepsilon}<1
can be assumed to be small.

Let us also assume here that
\rho\geq c>0
on
S.
Then, without loss of generality, we may even assume that

(1.13)

A_{t}f(y)=\int_{\Omega}f(y^{\prime}-tx^{\prime},y_{d}-t(1+\phi(x^{\prime})))\,dx^{\prime}.

The following proposition, which yields necessary conditions of geometric measure theoretic type for the
L^{p}
- boundedness of the maximal operator

{\mathcal{M}}_{S},
will be proved in Section
2
under these assumptions.

Proposition 1.10
.

Let
T\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be a symmetric convex body of positive volume
|T|>0
. Assume further that (
1.12
) is satisfied, and let
z_{d}:{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be any
C^{1}
- function so that

(1.14)

z_{d}(0)=1,\quad\nabla z_{d}(0)=0,\quad\text{and}\quad|\nabla z_{d}(z^{\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{6}\quad\text{for all }\ z^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}.

Put
T(z^{\prime}):=(z^{\prime},z_{d}(z^{\prime}))+T
for
z^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{\varepsilon},
and

|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|:=\int_{{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}}\chi_{T(z^{\prime})}(x^{\prime},1+\phi(x^{\prime}))\,dx^{\prime}.

Then, for
1\leq p<\infty,
we have

\int_{{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|^{p}}{|T|}\,dz^{\prime}\leq 4(C_{d}{\varepsilon}^{d-1})^{-p}\|{\mathcal{M}}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}^{p}.

Remarks 1.11
.

(i) The same result would hold more generally for any star-shaped body
T,
but that fact does not seem to be relevant for our applications.

(ii) In view of John’s theorem, we may basically replace the class of all convex bodies
T
in this result by the class of all ellipsoids centered at the origin.

(iii) Our proof will show that we even have the estimate

\int_{{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|^{p}}{|T|}\,dz^{\prime}\leq 4(C_{d}{\varepsilon}^{d-1})^{-p}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm loc}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}^{p},

where

{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm loc}f(x):=\sup_{1/2\leq t\leq 1}|A_{t}f(x)|.

Under the assumptions (
1.12
) on
\phi,
let us put

p_{\rm geom}(x^{0}):=\inf p,

where the infimum is taken over all
p\geq 1
for which there is some
{\varepsilon}>0,
and a constant
C_{p,{\varepsilon}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\int_{{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|^{p}}{|T|}\,dz^{\prime}\leq C_{p,{\varepsilon}}

holds true for all ellipsoids
T
centered at the origin and all
C^{1}
-functions
z_{d}:{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying the assumptions (
1.14
).

As a corollary to Proposition
1.10
, we obtain

Corollary 1.12
.

If
p<p_{\rm geom}(x^{0}),
then the maximal operators
{\mathcal{M}}
is unbounded on
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),

so that in particular
p_{\rm geom}(x^{0})\leq p_{c}(x^{0}).

Conjecture 2
.

If
p>p_{\rm geom}(x^{0}),
then the maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}
is bounded on
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),
provided
\rho
is supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0},
so that indeed
p_{c}(x^{0})=p_{\rm geom}(x^{0}).

Our discussion in Section
2
will reveal that this conjecture holds indeed true in dimension
d=3,
with the possible exception of hypersurfaces of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{e},
for which, however, it is equivalent to Conjecture
1
.

1.4.
A guide through the organization of the paper and the proofs of our main results

Section
2
will be devoted to the proof of Proposition
1.10
, from which we shall also deduce the necessity of the conditions in our main Theorems
1.2
and
1.9
. Moreover, we shall compare our results with results and a conjecture by
Iosevich, Sawyer and Seeger from their article
[
ISaSe99
]
.

In preparation of the proof of Theorem
1.9
, in Sections
3
and
4
we shall study functions
\phi=\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}
and their Legendre transforms
\breve{\phi}=\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})
with respect to the second variable
x_{2}.
The latter will be crucial in order to obtain very precise information on the partial Fourier transform of the measure
\mu
with respect to
x_{2}.
In particular, we shall show in Section
3
how to construct line-adapted coordinates for
\phi,
and give a necessary and sufficient condition for a given coordinate system to be line-adapted. And, in Section
4
, we shall show that the classes
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}_{n-1}
are invariant under this Legendre transform, and that the principal parts
\phi_{\rm red}
of
\phi
and
\breve{\phi}_{\rm red}
of
\breve{\phi}
are “essentially” the same, a fact which will be crucial in order to be able to transfer the conditions
(A1),(A2)
on
\phi_{\rm red}
into the same conditions on
\breve{\phi}_{\rm red}.

Next, in Section
5
, we shall provide some auxiliary results which will be used frequently later on. First, we recall a well-know version of a van der Corput type estimate for one-dimensional oscillatory integrals. Then, in Lemma
5.2
, we prove an identity for two-dimensional oscillatory integrals which has the flavour of a stationary phase identity and which will become crucial later on whenever we shall have to deal with the most challenging oscillatory integrals arising in our later studies. Next, we shall recall a lemma from our preceding paper
[
BDIM19
]
which will be applied whenever we shall prove
L^{p}
- estimates for
p
close to
1
for maximal operators associated to “microlocalized” pieces of our measure
\mu.
Finally, we shall perform some preliminary reductions which will allow to spectrally localize the measure
\mu
to frequencies
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
where

|\xi_{3}|\sim{\lambda}\gg 1\text{ and }|s_{1}|,|s_{2}|\ll 1,\text{ with }s_{1}:=\frac{\xi_{1}}{\xi_{3}},s_{2}:=\frac{\xi_{2}}{\xi_{3}},s_{3}:=\frac{\xi_{3}}{{\lambda}}.

Here,
{\lambda}
is a dyadic number, and the resulting measure will be denoted by
\mu^{\lambda}.

The Sections
6
to
9
will be devoted to the proof of the sufficiency of the conditions in Theorem
1.2
.
We begin by applying the method of stationary phase in the coordinate
x_{2};
in particular, we determine the Legendre transform

\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})
(cf. (
6.2
)) and show that the Fourier transform of
\mu^{\lambda}
can be written as

\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)={\lambda}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+1\big)}J({\lambda},s)\,\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3}),

where
J({\lambda},s)
denotes the one-dimensional oscillatory integral

J({\lambda},s):=\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime})}\eta(x_{1},s_{2})\,dx_{1},

with phase
\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime}):=\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1},
where
s^{\prime}:=(s_{1},s_{2}).

Quite important for us will also be the two-dimensional oscillatory integral

F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}):=\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime})-s_{1}y_{1})}\eta(x_{1},s_{2})\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{0}(s_{1})\,dx_{1}ds_{1},

which will allow us to write

\mu^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})={\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}\int\Big(\int F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-s_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{0}(s_{2})ds_{2}\Big)\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3},

where we have put
\Gamma:=(0,0,1).

Our estimates for the maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}
associated to the measure
\mu^{\lambda}
will be based on an interpolation between
L^{2}
- estimates and
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates for
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small. In view of Plancherel’s theorem, for these
L^{2}
- estimates, we shall need to estimate the oscillatory integrals
J({\lambda},s),
whereas for our
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
-estimates, pointwise bounds on
\mu^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})
will be required in order to be able to apply Lemma
5.4
.

As for the oscillatory integral
J({\lambda},s),
which strongly depends on the parameter
s_{2},
the strategy will be to fix a certain dyadic level of
s_{2},
say where
s_{2}\sim 2^{-k}\ll 1,
and then try to decompose the support of
\eta
in
x_{1}
into intervals over which the second derivative

\Phi(x_{1},s_{2}):={\partial}_{x_{1}}^{2}\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime})={\partial}_{x_{1}}^{2}\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})

will essentially be of a certain level size for these values of
s_{2},
so that for these parts of the oscillatory integral we can apply van der Corput’s estimate of order
2.
Clearly, in order to perform this, we shall need to understand the null set
\Phi(x_{1},s_{2})=0.
It is well-known that this set is the union of finitely many curves, either of the form
x_{1}=0,
or
x_{1}=r(s_{2}),
with non-trivial “real roots”
r(s_{2})
which can be expanded as Puiseux series in
s_{2}
– these facts will be re-called in Subsection
6.2
, as well as their connections with the two-dimensional Newton-polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
associated to
\Phi.
We are here following ideas from
[
PS97
]
. These roots come in clusters, the first type of cluster being determined by the leading exponent of the roots (we also have to consider complex roots!). These in return are in one to one correspondence with the compact edges of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi):
if
a=a_{l}
is the leading exponent of all roots in a given cluster, then there is a unique compact edge
{\gamma}={\gamma}_{l}
such that
a
is just the modulus of the slope of this edge.

Moreover, each compact edge
{\gamma}_{l}
comes with a certain (typically) anisotropic scaling structure, which allows to decompose our
(x_{1},s_{2})
- domain into certain domains
D_{l}
which are invariant under the scalings associated to
{\gamma}_{l},
and transition domains
E_{l}
in between these homogeneous domain (see Figure
3
). This decomposition will be explained in Section
7
. Now, on each transition domain, it turns out that we do have a good resolution of singularities of
\Phi;
indeed, by (
7.5
),

\Phi(x_{1},s_{2})=V(x_{1},s_{2})\,s_{2}^{A_{l}}x_{1}^{B_{l}},\qquad(x_{1},s_{2})\in E_{l},

where
|V(x_{1},s_{2})|\sim 1,
and where
(B_{l},A_{l})
is a vertex of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
associated to
E_{l}.

Thus, if we assume that
x_{1}\sim 2^{j},
then we see that
|\Phi(x_{1},s_{2})|\sim 2^{-kA_{l}-jB_{l}},
so that we can apply van der Corput’s estimate to the corresponding part
J_{j,k}({\lambda},s)
of
J({\lambda},s)
and get an appropriate
L^{2}
- estimate for the associated maximal operator

{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{j,k}.

However, a considerably bigger challenge comes with the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{j,k}.
Here, we need to understand the original phase

\Phi_{0}
and the associated functions
F_{j,k}
and
\mu^{\lambda}_{j,k}.
We show in (
7.6
) that, for suitable functions
g
and
h,

\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime})=\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1}=U(x_{1},s_{2})s_{2}^{A_{l}}x_{1}^{B_{l}+2}+x_{1}(s_{1}+g(s_{2}))+h(s_{2}),

where
|U(z_{1},s_{2})|\sim 1,
and one problem will be to gain a suitable control on these functions
g
and
h
(which is not very difficult in this first step of our resolution algorithm, but becomes more involved later on) in order to later also be able to control the integration in
s_{2}
in our integral formula for
\mu^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma}).

Once the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates are established, we can interpolate in order to obtain
L^{p}
-estimates for the maximal operators

{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{j,k}.
In order to show that these estimates can indeed be summed over all dyadic
{\lambda}\gg 1
and all indices
j,k
associated to the domain
E_{l},
we
also need the important “Geometric Lemma”
7.3
and its corollary, Lemma
7.4
, which will allow us to relate information from the Newton polyhedron to the exponents which appear in our
L^{p}
-estimates.

We are then left with the contributions by the homogeneous domains
D_{l},
which will contain all real “root curves” of the associated cluster. Given any leading term
cs_{2}^{a}
of some real root in the cluster, we can then essentially localize to a narrow, homogeneous neighborhood of the curve
x_{1}=cs_{2}^{a},
which will contain all “root curves” with this leading term.

In order to study the contribution by this narrow domain, we perform a change of coordinates, by putting
x_{1}-cs_{2}^{a}=:\tilde{z},

and express everything in these new coordinates
(\tilde{z},s_{2})
in place of
(x_{1},s_{2}).
In particular, we can then express the function
\Phi(x_{1},s_{2})
as a function
\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2}).

All this will be done in Section
7
.

In a second step of our resolution of singularities algorithm, we shall then consider the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi}),
and try to iterate the procedure from the first step, with
\Phi
replaced by
\tilde{\Phi}.
This will be performed in Section
8
. The
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates for the corresponding transition domains
\tilde{E}_{l}
will become even more involved in this Step 2. There are many subtleties arising in this process, let us just highlight one: Lemma
7.4
requires that the first compact edge of the Newton polyhedron has a left endpoint
(B_{1},A_{1})
with
A_{1}\geq 1.
This is immediate in Step 1, but no longer in Step 2, where this condition may fail. But, it turns out that the cases where this happens to fail can still be handled, since we do have a good resolution of singularities in these cases (see Subsection
8.1
).

Proceeding in this way with our algorithm, in order to analyze the contributions by the new homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l},
we have again to perform changes of coordinates by subtracting second order terms of the Puiseux series expansions of roots from
\tilde{z},
in order to arrive at Step 3, and so forth. All this will be explained in Section
9
. Iterating this procedure, we shall, step by step, narrow down our considerations to smaller and smaller homogeneous domains containing only root curves from sub-clusters of higher and higher order, which, after a finite number of steps, will only contain one, real root
r(s_{2}),
but possibly with multiplicity. However, in this case one can obtain a good resolution of singularity near this root, and can then essentially apply the same techniques that we had used for transition domains also to such kind of homogeneous domains.

The Sections
10
to
13
will be covering the proof of the sufficiency of the conditions in Theorem
1.9
.
By and large, we shall be able to follow the proof of Theorem
1.2
and shall therefore only highlight those parts of the proof which will require different or modified arguments. Major differences are caused by the change of coordinates (
1.7
) which leads to line-adapted coordinates. Moreover, a crucial new tool will be the “Multiplicity Lemma“
11.1
, which, under our assumptions
(A1),(A2),
implies that in Step 2 of our resolution algorithm, the case where
A_{1}<1
can only arise when all non-trivial real roots have multiplicity
1,
so that we can then argue in a similar way as for Theorem
1.2
.
Another difference is the way how we control the functions
g
and
h
above; this, however, turns out to be even easier here than in the case of singularities of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{n-1},
due to the fact that here
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2.

Finally, in Section
14
, we shall indicate how the proof of Theorem
1.9
can be modified to give a proof of the Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger conjecture for surfaces of class
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}_{n-1}.

Conventions:
Throughout this article, we shall use the “variable constant” notation, i.e., many constants appearing in the course of our arguments, often denoted by
C,
will typically have different values at different lines. Moreover, we shall use symbols such as
\sim,\lesssim
or
\ll
in order to avoid writing down constants, as explained in
[
IM16
, Chapter 1]
.
By
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
we shall denote smooth cut-off functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
with typically small compact supports,
where
\chi_{1}
vanishes near the origin and is identically one near
1,
whereas
\chi_{0}
is identically
1
on a small neighborhood of the origin.
These cut-off functions may also vary from line to line, and may in some instances, where several of such functions of different variables appear within the same formula, even designate different functions.

Also, if we speak of the
slope
of a line such as a supporting line to a Newton polyhedron, then we shall actually mean the modulus of the slope.

Acknowledgement:
The authors wish to thank Spyros Dendrinos for helpful annotations on the paper and in particular for suggesting Example
1.6
.

2.
Necessary conditions

Let us first prove Proposition
1.10
.

Proof of Proposition
1.10
.
Recall that
{\Omega}={\Omega}_{\varepsilon}=\{z^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-1}:|z^{\prime}|<{\varepsilon}\},
where
0<{\varepsilon}<1,
and let

B:=\{t(z^{\prime},z_{d}(z^{\prime})):z^{\prime}\in\Omega,1/2\leq t\leq 1\}.

If
y\in B
, then
y=t(z^{\prime},z_{d}(z^{\prime}))=tz
, for suitable
t=t(y),z^{\prime}=z^{\prime}(y)
and
1/2\leq t\leq 1
. Then, by (
1.13
), for such
y,t,z
,

\displaystyle A_{t}\chi_{T}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\chi_{T}(y^{\prime}-tx^{\prime},y_{d}-t(1+\phi(x^{\prime})))\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\chi_{\frac{1}{t}(y+T)}(x^{\prime},1+\phi(x^{\prime}))dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{d}{\varepsilon}^{d-1}|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|,

since
T
is symmetric and convex, which implies that
\frac{1}{t}(y+T)\supset T(z^{\prime})
. Thus,

|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|\leq\frac{1}{C_{d}{\varepsilon}^{d-1}}{\mathcal{M}}\chi_{T}(tz)

for

1/2\leq t\leq 1
. But,
F:(t,z^{\prime})\mapsto(tz^{\prime},tz_{d}(z^{\prime}))=tz
is a
C^{1}
- change of coordinates, with Jacobian
J_{F}
satisfying

|J_{F}(t,z^{\prime})|=t^{d-1}|z_{d}(z^{\prime})-z^{\prime}\cdot\nabla z_{d}(z^{\prime})|,

so that, by (
1.14
),
1/2^{d}\leq|J_{F}(t,z^{\prime})|\leq 2.

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|^{p}dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(C_{d}{\varepsilon}^{d-1})^{-p}\int_{\Omega}\int_{1/2}^{1}{\mathcal{M}}\chi_{T}(tz^{\prime},tz_{d}(z^{\prime})))^{p}dtdz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4(C_{d}{\varepsilon}^{d-1})^{-p}\int_{B}{\mathcal{M}}\chi_{T}(y)^{p}dy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4(C_{d}{\varepsilon}^{d-1})^{-p}\|{\mathcal{M}}\|^{p}_{p\to p}|T|.

Q.E.D.

In applications one would choose the function
z_{d}(z^{\prime})
so that
|T(z^{\prime})\cap S|
becomes essentially as large as possible.

2.1.
The three dimensional case

Let us now turn our attention to the
d=3
case. We will be looking at
T
of the form
T=[-{\delta}_{1},{\delta}_{1}]\times[-{\delta}_{2},{\delta}_{2}]\times[-{\delta}_{3},{\delta}_{3}]
with
{\delta}_{i}\leq 1.
Let

I:=\{i\in\{1,2\}:{\delta}_{i}\sim 1\}.

We will be taking
{\delta}_{i}\ll 1
for
i\not\in I
. Also let
k:=|I|
and, given
z^{\prime}=(z_{1},z_{2})\in{\Omega},
let us consider
(z_{1}^{0},z_{2}^{0}),
where
z_{i}^{0}:=0,
if
i\in I,
and
z_{i}^{0}:=z_{i},
if
i\not\in I
. It is then plausible that a good choice for the function
z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2})
might be

(2.1)

z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2}):=1+\phi(z_{1}^{0},z_{2}^{0}).

We deduce necessary conditions for the
L^{p}
-boundedness of
{\mathcal{M}}
based on the following three examples, where the cases
k=0
and
k=2
can indeed by treated by this kind of choice of
z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2}).
In the case
k=1,
we will first have to apply suitable changes of coordinates of the form (
1.7
) before making this kind of choice.

Example 2.1
.

The case
k=0
.

Here
I=\emptyset
, i.e.,
{\delta}_{i}\ll 1,i=1,2,3
, and we choose
z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2}):=1+\phi(z_{1},z_{2})
. Then if we choose
{\delta}_{1}={\delta}_{2}={\delta}_{3}={\delta}\ll 1
, meaning that
T
is a
\delta
-cube, we have
|T(z_{1},z_{2})\cap S|\sim{\delta}^{2}
for all
(z_{1},z_{2})\in{\Omega}
, which implies that for all
{\delta}\ll 1,

\|{\mathcal{M}}\|^{p}_{p\to p}\gtrsim\int_{\Omega}{\delta}^{2p-3}dz_{1}dz_{2}\sim{\delta}^{2p-3},

giving the necessary condition
p\geq 3/2

{\mathcal{M}}
to be bounded on
L^{p}.

Example 2.2
.

The case
k=2
.

Here we choose
I=\{1,2\}
, hence only
{\delta}_{3}={\delta}\ll 1,
and thus
T
is essentially a
{\delta}
-slab. We choose
(z_{1}^{0},z_{2}^{0})=(0,0)
, so
z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2})=1+\phi(0,0)=1
. Then, the condition
|1+\phi(z_{1},z_{2})-z_{3}|=|\phi(z_{1},z_{2})|<{\delta}
implies that
|T(z_{1},z_{2})\cap S|\sim{\delta}^{1/h}
for all
(z_{1},z_{2})\in{\Omega}
(i.e.,
|(z_{1},z_{2})|<{\varepsilon}
), provided
{\varepsilon}>0
is chosen sufficiently small. Here,
h=h(x^{0})
denotes the height of
\phi
. This in turn implies that

\|{\mathcal{M}}\|^{p}_{p\to p}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}{\delta}^{p/h-1}dz_{1}dz_{2}\sim{\delta}^{p/h-1},

giving the necessary condition
p\geq h
for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be bounded on
L^{p}.

Note that the same computations show that necessarily

(2.2)

p_{\rm geom}\geq\max\{3/2,h\}.

Since we have seen in the Introduction that (
1.4
) holds true for all classes of 2-hypersurfaces, with the exception of hypersurfaces of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+},
and since by Corollary
1.12
we have
p_{\rm geom}\leq p_{c},
(
2.2
) shows that indeed
p_{\rm geom}=p_{c}
for these classes of surfaces.

Example 2.3
.

The case
k=1
.

We can here focus on the remaining class of functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}.
Following the discussion in Section
1
just before Examples
1.4
and
1.5
, we may perform any local coordinate change as in (
1.7
) and denote by
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
the new coordinate system.

Recall the decomposition
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})=\tilde{\phi}(0,y_{2})+\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})
. Note that
\tilde{\phi}(0,y_{2})=y_{2}^{2}\rho(y_{2})
for some analytic function
\rho
at the origin. Denote by
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
the first edge of the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})
of

\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}
with left endpoint
(n,0)
(which must intersect the line
t_{2}=1
), and by
\tilde{L}_{1}:=\{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1\}
the line supporting
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}.
Then

\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}n_{y}^{e}+\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=1,
hence (cf. (
1.6
))

n_{e}^{y}=(1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}.

Assume first that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0,
so that
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is a compact edge.

We choose
I=\{1\}
, so that

T=[-1,1]\times[-{\delta}_{2},{\delta}_{2}]\times[-{\delta},{\delta}],

and then choose
z_{3}
according to (
2.1
), but with respect to the coordinates
(y_{1},y_{2})
. Noting that
y_{2}=x_{2},
this means that we put

z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2}):=1+\tilde{\phi}(0,z_{2})=1+z_{2}^{2}\rho(z_{2}).

Assume that the point
(x_{1},x_{2},1+\phi(x_{1},x_{2}))
on
S
lies in
T(z_{1},z_{2}).
Then

|x_{1}-z_{1}|\leq 1,\ |x_{2}-z_{2}|\leq{\delta}_{2},\ |1+\phi(x_{1},x_{2})-z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2})|\leq{\delta}.

But, we have

(2.3)

\displaystyle|1+\phi(x_{1},x_{2})-z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|1+\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})-z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|y_{2}^{2}\rho(y_{2})-z_{2}^{2}\rho(z_{2})|+|\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})|.

Note next that since
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}
agrees with a
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial of degree
1,
up to some error term of higher

\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree, we see that

|\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})|\lesssim|y_{1}|^{1/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}+|y_{2}|^{1/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}},

provided
{\varepsilon}>0
is sufficiently small in our definition of
{\Omega}.

We also recall that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}=1/n.

Thus, to make sure that
|\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})|\leq{\delta}/2,
it suffices to assume that

|x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2})|=|y_{1}|\leq c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad|x_{2}|=|y_{2}|\leq 2c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}},

where
0<c<1
is sufficiently small.
Let us therefore assume in what follows that
|z_{2}|\leq c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}
and
|x_{2}|\leq 2c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}.

Then also

|y_{2}^{2}\rho(y_{2})-z_{2}^{2}\rho(z_{2})|\lesssim|x_{2}-z_{2}|(|x_{2}|+|z_{2}|)\leq{\delta}_{2}4c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}.

We therefore choose
{\delta}_{2}:={\delta}^{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}
in the above definition of
T,
i.e., we consider

T=T_{\delta}:=[-1,1]\times[-{\delta}^{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}},{\delta}^{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}]\times[-{\delta},{\delta}].

Then, (
2.3
) and the subsequent estimates show that

|1+\phi(x_{1},x_{2})-z_{3}(z_{1},z_{2})|\leq\delta,

if
c
is sufficiently small. To summarize: if we define the set

A_{\delta}(z_{1},z_{2}):=\{(x_{1},x_{2}):|x_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{2},|x_{2}|\leq 2c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}},\,|x_{2}-z_{2}|\leq c{\delta}^{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}},|x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2})|\leq c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}\},

then if
|z_{1}|\leq 1/2,\,|z_{2}|\leq c{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}
and
(x_{1},x_{2})\in A_{\delta}(z_{1},z_{2}),
we have
(x_{1},x_{2},1+\phi(x_{1},x_{2}))\in T_{\delta}(z_{1},z_{2}),
so that

|T_{\delta}(z_{1},z_{2})\cap S|\geq|A_{\delta}(z_{1},z_{2})|\gtrsim{\delta}^{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}={\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}+1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}},

provided
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}\leq 1/2.
But, since
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})\subset{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}),
and property
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}
holds true also for
\tilde{\phi},
we must indeed have
1/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}\geq 2.

Thus, by Proposition
1.10
, for any sufficiently small
{\delta}>0,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{M}}\|^{p}_{p\to p}

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\int_{|z_{1}|\leq 1,|z_{2}|\leq{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}}\frac{|T_{\delta}(z_{1},z_{2})\cap S|^{p}}{|T|}dz_{1}dz_{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle{\delta}^{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}\frac{{\delta}^{(\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}+1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})p}}{{\delta}^{2-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta}^{(\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}+1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})p-2(1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})},

so we must have that

p\geq 2\frac{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}+1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}=\frac{2n^{y}_{e}}{n^{y}_{e}+1}.

Note that the right-hand side is increasing in
n_{e}^{y}.

Assume finally that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0.
Then, for any
M\in{\mathbb{N}},M\gg 1,
let us change coordinates to
y_{1}^{(M)}:=y_{1}+y_{2}^{M},y_{2}^{(M)}:=y_{2},
and consider
\phi^{(M)}_{\rm red}
representing
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}
in these new coordinates. Then, for
M
sufficiently large, the edge of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi^{(M)}_{\rm red})
passing through the line
t_{2}=1
will be compact, and it is easily seen
that
n_{e}^{y^{(M)}}\to n_{e}^{y}
as
M\to\infty.
Thus, by what we have already shown for the case where
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is compact, also in this case we find that necessarily

p\geq 2n_{e}^{y}/(n_{e}^{y}+1).

Our estimates imply the necessary condition
p\geq p_{e}
for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be bounded on
L^{p}.

In combination, these three examples show that the condition

p\geq\tilde{p}_{c}=\max\{3/2,p_{e},h\}

is necessary in Case
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
, which proves Proposition
1.7
. In combination with Theorem
1.9
, we see as well that indeed

p_{\rm geom}(x^{0})=p_{c}(x^{0})=\max\{3/2,p_{e},h\},

if
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+},
but not of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e},
and Conjecture
1
would even imply that it is true for all functions of type

{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}.

2.2.
A comparison with a conjecture by Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger

A. Iosevich, E. Sawyer and A. Seeger conjectured in
[
ISaSe99
]
(Remark 1.6 (iii)) that, at least for convex surfaces, a
sufficient
condition for the
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
- boundedness of the maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}
of Subsection
1.3
is the following:

For every
l=0,\dots,d-1
and for every affine
l
-plane
E_{l}
through
x^{0}
the function
x\mapsto\text{\rm dist\,}(x,E_{l})^{-1}
belongs to
L^{(d-l)/p}(S)
, that is

(2.4)

\int_{S}\text{\rm dist\,}(x,E_{l})^{-(d-l)/p}d\sigma(x)<\infty.

Let us compare this conjecture with the results described in the Introduction for the case
d=3,
so that we have to look at the condition (
2.4
) for
l=0,1,2
.

If
l=0,
then obviously we have
\text{\rm dist\,}(x,E_{0})\sim|x-x^{0}|,
so that
\text{\rm dist\,}(x,E_{0})^{-1}\in L^{3/p}(S)
for every
p>3/2
.

The case
l=2
had already been discussed in
[
IKM10
]
, where it been shown that (
2.4
) is equivalent to the condition

p>h(\phi)=h
.

Finally, if
l=1,
let us here concentrate on the case of singularities of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1};
for all other types analogous considerations apply as well.
So let us assume that
\phi
is given by the normal form (
1.3
), and that
x^{0}=(0,0,1).
Then the maximal possible restriction on
p
exerted by (
2.4
) appears when the line
E_{1}
points in the direction of the
x_{1}
- axis and passes through
x^{0}.
Then

\text{\rm dist\,}(x,E_{1})\sim|x_{2}|+|b(x_{1},x_{2})(x_{2}-x_{1}^{m}\omega(x_{1}))^{2}+x_{1}^{n}\beta(x_{1})|\sim|x_{2}|+|x_{1}|^{n},

at least when
n
is finite, and then (
2.4
) holds if and only if
p>{2n}/(n+1).
Note that here
2n/(n+1)>2n/(n+2)=h.

The case where
b_{0}(x_{1})
in (
1.3
)
is flat at the origin, where formally
n=\infty,
works in a similar way.

Thus, since here
h=2n/(n+2),
the conditions given by (
2.4
) require that

p>\max\Big\{\frac{3}{2},h,\frac{2n}{n+1}\Big\}=\max\Big\{\frac{3}{2},\frac{2n}{n+1}\Big\}.

Now, by Theorem
1.2
, we know that for singularities of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{-},
the condition
p>\max\{3/2,h\}=\max\{3/2,2n/(n+2)\}
does suffice, which is a weaker condition, unless
n\leq 3.
We remark that the corresponding surfaces cannot be convex, if the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are not adapted to
\phi.

On the other hand, for singularities of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}
we always have
n_{e}\leq n,
so that
p_{e}\leq{2n}/(n+1),
and by Theorem
1.9
for type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
the condition

p>\max\Big\{\frac{3}{2},p_{e}\Big\}=\max\Big\{\frac{3}{2},\frac{2n_{e}}{n_{e}+1}\Big\}

is sufficient. Since there are many cases in which we indeed have strict inequality
p_{e}<{2n}/(n+1),

we see that the Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger conjecture does not exactly identify the critical exponent
p_{c}
in general, even for convex surfaces.

A concrete example of this type is Example
1.5
, i.e.,
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=(1+x_{1}^{\alpha}x_{2}^{\beta})x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{n},
with
2\leq{\alpha}<n
and
n\geq 5.
We had seen that here
n_{e}=(n({\beta}+1)+{\alpha})/({\beta}+2)<n,
so that
p_{e}<2n/(n+1),
and one checks easily that
n_{e}>3,
so that by Remark
1.3
we have
p_{e}>3/2.
Theorem
1.9
thus shows that the maximal operator is
L^{p}
- bounded in the wider range
p>p_{e}
than
p>2n/(n+1).

Another example is the following variant of Example
1.6
, which is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}.

Example 2.4
.

Let
\phi(x_{1},x_{2}):=\frac{1}{1-x_{1}^{2}}x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{n},
with even
n\geq 4.

The graph of this function is even convex for
|x_{1}|<1,
provided
n
is even.
Since

1/(1-x_{1}^{2})=1+x_{1}^{2}+O(x_{1}^{4}),
one finds that
n_{e}^{x}=(n+2)/{2},
and the coordinates
x
are already line-adapted, so that
n_{e}=(n+2)/{2}\geq 3,
hence
p_{e}\geq 3/2.
Thus, we see that
p_{e}=2(n+2)/(n+4)<2n/(n+1),
and by Theorem
1.9
,
{\mathcal{M}}
is bounded on the wider range
p>p_{e}
than
p>2n/(n+1).

We also recall that we shall give a proof of the conjecture by Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger for analytic singularities of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
in Section
14
.

Thus, our discussion in the Introduction and the main theorems of this paper show that our results give a proof of the conjecture by Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger in dimension
d=3
(at least for analytic, but not necessarily convex surfaces
S
).

3.
Existence of line-adapted coordinates for functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}

Assume again that
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}.

Our first result will give some necessary conditions for the given coordinate system
x
not to be line-adapted to
\phi;
it bears some analogies with Theorem 3.3 of
[
IM11a
]
, in which necessary conditions were given for the given coordinate system
x
not to be adapted to
\phi
in the sense of Varchenko. Our proof also basically follows ideas from that paper, but is somewhat simpler.

Recall from the Introduction the decomposition
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi(0,x_{2})+\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}).
Recall also that the point
(n,0)
is a vertex of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red}),
and that we denote by
{\gamma}_{1}
the (non-horizontal) edge of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red})
which has
(n,0)
as right endpoint. Moreover, the weight

{\kappa}^{e}=({\kappa}^{e}_{1},{\kappa}^{e}_{2})=(1/n,{\kappa}^{e}_{2})
had been chosen so that the edge
{\gamma}_{1}
lies on the line
L:=\{t_{1},t_{2}):{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1\}.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\phi
by of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1},
and assume that the given coordinates
x
are
not line-adapted to
\phi.
Then all of the following conditions hold true:

(a)

The edge
{\gamma}_{1}
is compact, i.e.,
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0.

(b)

{\kappa}^{e}_{1}/{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=1/({\kappa}^{e}_{2}n)\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(c)

If we denote by
\phi_{e_{1}}:=(\phi_{\rm red})_{{\kappa}^{e}}
the
{\kappa}^{e}
-principal part of
\phi_{\rm red},
then
{\partial}_{1}\phi_{e_{1}}
has a (unique) root of multiplicity
n-1
on the unit circle
S^{1},
and this root does not lie on a coordinate axis.

Proof.

If the coordinates
x
are not line-adapted to
\phi,
then there is an admissible system of coordinates
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
such that
n_{e}^{x}<n_{e}^{y}.
Assume it is given by
x_{1}={\varphi}(y_{1},y_{2}),x_{2}=y_{2},
and let
\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2}):=\phi({\varphi}(y_{1},y_{2}),y_{2})
represent
\phi
in the coordinates
y.
Since
{\partial}_{1}{\varphi}(0,0)\not=0,
the implicit function theorem and a Taylor expansion show that we may write
{\varphi}(y_{1},y_{2})
as
{\varphi}(y_{1},y_{2})=(y_{1}-\psi(y_{2}))u(y_{1},\psi(y_{2})),
with smooth functions
\psi
and
u,
where
u(0,0)\neq 0
and
\psi(0)=0.
After scaling, we may assume for simplicity that
u(0,0)=1.

Obviously, if
\psi
were a flat function at the origin, then the change of coordinates would not change the Newton polyhedra of
\phi
and
\phi_{\rm red},
so this case cannot arise here.

Thus,
\psi
is of some finite type
k\geq 1,
so that
\psi(y_{2})=y_{2}^{k}{\omega}(y_{2}),
with a smooth function
{\omega}
such that
{\omega}(0)\neq 0.

Part (a) is obvious, for if
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
then
n_{e}^{x}=n
is already maximal possible.

To prove the other claims, let us first assume that
k>{\kappa}^{e}_{1}/{\kappa}^{e}_{2}.
As in
[
IM11a
]
(Section 3 or Lemma 2.1 in that paper) we easily see that

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\phi_{\rm red})_{{\kappa}^{e}}({\varphi}_{{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2}),y_{2})-(\phi_{\rm red})_{{\kappa}^{e}}({\varphi}_{{\kappa}^{e}}(0,y_{2}),y_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\ \mbox{terms of higher ${\kappa}^{e}$-degree,}

so that
(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})_{{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2})=\phi_{e_{1}}({\varphi}_{{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2}),y_{2})-\phi_{e_{1}}({\varphi}_{{\kappa}^{e}}(0,y_{2}),y_{2}).
Since
y_{1}
has
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
{\kappa}^{e}_{1},
and
y_{2}^{k}
has
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
k{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>{\kappa}^{e}_{1},
we see that
{\varphi}_{{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1},
so that

(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})_{{\kappa}^{e}}=\phi_{e_{1}}.
This would mean that the change of coordinates would not change the edge
{\gamma}_{1},
contradicting our assumption
n_{e}^{x}<n_{e}^{y}.
Thus, we must have
k\leq{\kappa}^{e}_{1}/{\kappa}^{e}_{2}.

Assume next that
k<{\kappa}^{e}_{1}/{\kappa}^{e}_{2}.
Then let us consider the weight
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}:=({\kappa}^{e}_{1},{{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}/k).
Note that
1/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/{\kappa}^{e}_{2},
which means that the line
\tilde{L}:=\{(t_{1},t_{2}):\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1\}
is less steep then
L.
In particular,

\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}\alpha_{1}+\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}\alpha_{2}\geq 1
for every
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red}).
Moreover, similarly as before,

(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2})=(\phi_{\rm red})_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}({\varphi}_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2}),y_{2})-(\phi_{\rm red})_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}({\varphi}_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}(0,y_{2}),y_{2}).
But, clearly

{\varphi}_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}-cy_{2}^{k},
with
c:={\omega}(0)\neq 0,
and
(\phi_{\rm red})_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}=x_{1}^{n}\beta(0),
with
\beta(0)\neq 0.
Therefore

(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})_{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2})=(y_{1}-cy_{2}^{k})^{n}\beta(0)-(-cy_{2}^{k})^{n}\beta(0),
which shows that the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
associated to
\tilde{\phi}
lies on the line
\tilde{L}.
But this in return would imply that
n_{e}^{x}>n_{e}^{y}.

We thus see that necessarily
k={\kappa}^{e}_{1}/{\kappa}^{e}_{2},
which proves (b).

To prove (c), recall that
{\kappa}^{e}_{1}/{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=k\geq 1
is a natural number, so that in particular
{\kappa}^{e}_{1}\geq{\kappa}^{e}_{2}.
Since
\phi_{e_{1}}
is a
{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial of
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree 1, arguing as before, we see that the change to the coordinates
y
leads to the corresponding function

(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})_{{\kappa}^{e}}(y_{1},y_{2})=\phi_{e_{1}}(y_{1}-cy_{2}^{k},y_{2})-\phi_{e_{1}}(-cy_{2}^{k},y_{2}),

which is again
{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous of degree 1. However, only when the Taylor support of the latter function consists just of the single point
(n,0),
then in the
y
coordinates the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
corresponding to
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}
can be steeper than the edge
{\gamma}_{1}
(this is equivalent to the condition
n_{e}^{x}<n_{e}^{y}
) - otherwise it would have the same slope as
{\gamma}_{1}
. This means that necessarily

\phi_{e_{1}}(y_{1}-cy_{2}^{k},y_{2})-\phi_{e_{1}}(-cy_{2}^{k},y_{2})=ay_{1}^{n}

for some constant
a\neq 0,
i.e., that

\phi_{e_{1}}(x_{1},x_{2})=a(x_{1}+cx_{2}^{k})^{n}+\phi_{e_{1}}(-cx_{2}^{k},x_{2}).

This implies that
{\partial}_{1}\phi_{e_{1}}(x_{1},x_{2})=an(x_{1}+cx_{2}^{k})^{n-1},
so that
{\partial}_{1}\phi_{e_{1}}
has indeed a (unique) root of multiplicity
n-1

on the unit circle, and since
c\neq 0,
it does not lie on a coordinate axis.

Q.E.D.

Corollary 3.2
.

If
(y_{1},y_{2})
is any admissible coordinate system for
\phi
in which (
1.11
) holds true, then these coordinates are already line-adapted to
\phi.

Proof.

In this case,
\phi_{e_{1}}=p
is of the form (
1.11
), and it is easily seen that then condition (c) in Proposition
3.1

cannot be satisfied.

Q.E.D.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\phi
by of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1},
and assume that the given coordinates
x
are
not line-adapted to
\phi.
Then there exists a local line-adapted coordinate system
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
of the form (
1.7
), i.e.,

y_{1}=x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}),y_{2}=x_{2},
where we can choose for
{\alpha}(x_{2})
the unique smooth local solution to the equation
{\partial}_{1}^{n-1}\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=0
in
x_{1}.

Remark 3.4
.

Since
{\alpha}(0)=0,
we may locally write
{\alpha}(x_{2})=x_{2}^{l}\rho(x_{2}),
where
l
is a natural number
l\geq 1,

\rho
is smooth, and where we may assume that
\rho(0)\neq 0,
if
{\alpha}
is of finite type
l
at the origin. If
{\alpha}
is flat at the origin, then we may choose
l
arbitrarily large.

Without loss of generality, we may even assume that
l\geq 2,
for if
{\alpha}
is of finite type
l=1,
we may first apply the linear change of coordinates

y_{1}=x_{1}-\rho(0)x_{2},y_{2}=x_{2},
which is harmless as the estimation of our maximal operators is invariant under linear coordinate changes, and then apply in a second step a change of coordinates as before, but now with
l\geq 2.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.
In view of Proposition
3.1
it will suffice to show that the conditions (a) – (c) in that proposition already suffice to derive the existence of such an adapted coordinate system.

Since
{\partial}_{1}^{n-1}\phi(0,0)=0
but
{\partial}_{1}^{n}\phi(0,0)\neq 0,
the implicit function theorem guarantees locally the existence of a unique smooth solution
x_{1}={\alpha}(x_{2})
to the equation
{\partial}_{1}^{n-1}\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=0.
Applying a Taylor expansion of
\phi
around
x_{1}={\alpha}(x_{2}),
we see that

(3.1)

\displaystyle\phi(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{n}(x_{1},x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{n}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle B_{n-2}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{n-2}+\dots+B_{1}(x_{2})(x_{1}-{\alpha}(x_{2}))+B_{0}(x_{2}),

where the functions
B_{j}
are smooth and
B_{n}(0,0)=\beta\neq 0,B_{j}(0)=0
for
0=1,\dots,n-2.

Let
\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})
henceforth represent
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
in the new coordinates
y_{1}:=x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}),y_{2}:=x_{2}.

Since
{\partial}_{1}\phi_{\rm red}={\partial}_{1}\phi,
(
3.1
) implies that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{1}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{n-1}(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}),x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{n-1}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle A_{n-2}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{n-3}+\dots+A_{1}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))+A_{0}(x_{2}),

where the functions
A_{j}
are smooth and
A_{n-1}(0,0)\neq 0.
Recall also from (a) – (c) in Proposition
3.1
that
{\kappa}_{1}/{\kappa}_{2}=:k\in{\mathbb{N}}
is a positive integer. Moreover, either
{\alpha}
is flat at the origin, or of some finite type
\ell,
so that
\alpha(x_{2})=x_{2}^{\ell}{\omega}(x_{2}),
with
{\omega}(0)\neq 0.

But, since

{\partial}_{1}^{n-1}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})=D(x_{1},x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2})),

where
D(0,0)\neq 0,
and since the
{\kappa}
-homogeneous part of
{\partial}_{1}^{n-1}\phi_{\rm red}
has
{\kappa}
-degree
1-(n-1){\kappa}_{1}=1/n={\kappa}_{1},
we see that we must have
\ell\geq k,
since otherwise the homogeneity degree
{\kappa}_{2}\ell=({\ell}/k){\kappa}_{1}
of the leading term of
{\alpha}
would be strictly less than
{\kappa}_{1},
and the same would apply to
{\partial}_{1}^{n-1}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}).

Consider next the
{\kappa}
-principal part of the function

A_{n-1}(y_{1},y_{2})y_{1}^{n-1}+A_{n-2}(y_{2})y_{1}^{n-3}+\dots+A_{1}(y_{2})y_{1}+A_{0}(y_{2}),

which is of the form

A_{n-1}(0,0)y_{1}^{n-1}+a_{n-3}y_{2}^{l_{n-3}}y_{1}^{n-3}+\dots+a_{1}y_{2}^{l_{1}}y_{1}+a_{0}y_{2}^{l_{0}},

with real coefficients
a_{j}
and integer exponents
l_{j}>0,j=0,\dots,n-3.

As in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we then see that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{1}(\phi_{\rm red})_{\kappa}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{n-1}(0,0)(x_{1}-{\alpha}_{\kappa}(x_{2}))^{n-1}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle a_{n-3}y_{2}^{l_{n-3}}(x_{1}-{\alpha}_{\kappa}(x_{2}))^{n-3}+\dots+a_{1}x_{2}^{l_{1}}(x_{1}-{\alpha}_{\kappa}(x_{2}))+a_{0}x_{2}^{l_{0}},

where
{\alpha}_{\kappa}
denotes the
{\kappa}
-principal part of
{\alpha}.
By condition (c), this polynomial must have a root of order
n-1
away from the coordinate axes. Clearly this is only possible if all coefficients
a_{j},j=0,\dots n-3,
are
0.
But then
{\partial}_{1}(\phi_{\rm red})_{\kappa}=A_{n-1}(0,0)(x_{1}-{\alpha}_{\kappa}(x_{2}))^{n-1},
so that in the coordinates
y
we have
{\partial}_{1}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})_{\kappa}=A_{n-1}(0,0)y_{1}^{n-1}
plus terms of higher
{\kappa}
-degree. This shows that in the new coordinates
y,
the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
of the Newton polyhedron of
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}
which passes through
(n,0)
is steeper than
{\gamma}_{1},
so that
n_{e}^{y}>n_{e}^{x}.
We shall denote by
\tilde{\kappa}=(1/n,\tilde{\kappa}_{2})
the weight associated to
\tilde{\gamma}_{1},
which then satisfies
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}<{\kappa}_{2}.

Note that this shows in particular that the conditions (a) – (c) imply that the original coordinates
x
were not line-adapted.

Let us finally show that the coordinates
y
are indeed line-adapted to
\phi.
This is obvious if
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}=0,
for then
n^{y}_{e}=n
is maximal possible. Let us therefore assume that
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}>0.

By (
3.1
),
\tilde{\phi}(y)
can be written as

\tilde{\phi}(y)=\tilde{B}_{n}(y_{1},y_{2})y_{1}^{n}+B_{n-2}(y_{2})y_{1}^{n-2}+\dots+B_{1}(y_{2})y_{1}+B_{0}(y_{2}),

with smooth functions
B_{j},
where
\tilde{B}_{n}(0,0)\neq 0.
As before, this implies that

{\partial}_{1}\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y)={\partial}_{1}\tilde{\phi}(y)=\tilde{A}_{n-1}(y_{1},y_{2})y_{1}^{n-1}+A_{n-2}(y_{2})y_{1}^{n-3}+\dots+A_{1}(y_{2})y_{1}+A_{0}(y_{2}),

where
\tilde{A}_{n-1}(0,0)\neq 0.
Then, as before, we see that the
\tilde{\kappa}
-homogeneous part of this function is of the form

\displaystyle{\partial}_{1}(\phi_{\rm red})_{\tilde{\kappa}}(y_{1},y_{2})=\tilde{A}_{n-1}(0,0)y_{1}^{n-1}+\tilde{a}_{n-3}y_{2}^{\tilde{l}_{n-3}}y_{1}^{n-3}+\dots+\tilde{a}_{1}y_{2}^{\tilde{l}_{1}}y_{1}+\tilde{a}_{0}y_{2}^{\tilde{l}_{0}},

and not all coefficients
\tilde{a}_{j},j=0,\dots n-3,
can vanish. Since this function, as a polynomial in
y_{1},
contains no term with
y_{1}
- exponent
n-2,
it cannot have any root of order
