n-1.
Thus, by Proposition
3.1
, the coordinates
y
must be line-adapted to
\phi.

Q.E.D.

Our proof shows that Proposition
3.1
can even be strengthened:

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\phi
be of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}.
Then the given coordinates
x
are
not line-adapted to
\phi
if and only if all of the conditions (a)–(c) in Proposition
3.1
hold true.

4.
On the Legendre transform in
x_{2}
of functions of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}

Let
\phi
be a function of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}.
Recall that we were left with the case
n\geq 4,
on which we shall concentrate here.

Even though for the arguments to follow this would not be really necessary, we may assume that
\phi
is given in the normal form (
1.3
), i.e.,

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=b(x_{1},x_{2})(x_{2}-x_{1}^{m}\omega(x_{1}))^{2}+x_{1}^{n}\beta(x_{1}).

We shall later apply the method of stationary phase to the partial Fourier transform of the corresponding measure
\mu
defined in Section
1
, so we shall need to look for the critical point in
x_{2}
of the phase

\Phi(x_{1},x_{2},s_{2}):=\phi(x_{1},x_{2})+s_{2}x_{2},

i.e., for the solution to the equation
{\partial}_{2}\Phi(x_{1},x_{2},s_{2})=0
with
x_{2}(0,0)=0
. Note that such a solution will locally exist as a smooth function, in view of the implicit function theorem, since
\Phi(0,x_{2},0)=b(0,x_{2})x_{2}^{2}.

We then denote by

\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2}):=\Phi(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}),s_{2})

the
Legendre transform
of
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
in
x_{2}
(note that the Legendre transform
\L:\phi\mapsto\breve{\phi}
is often defined with the opposite sign, which ensures that it is an involution, i.e.,
\L\circ\L=\rm{id}
, but our definition is better adapted to the Fourier transform in
x_{2}
and the usage of the method of stationary phase).

We shall see that the function
\breve{\phi},
as a function of the two variables
x_{1}
and
s_{2},
will also be of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1},
so that we can define its associated effective multiplicity
n_{e}(\breve{\phi})
too, and we have

Theorem 4.1
.

If the function
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1},
then so is its Legendre transform
\breve{\phi},
and both have the same effective multiplicity, i.e.,
n_{e}(\breve{\phi})=n_{e}(\phi).

Moreover, if
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}_{n-1},
then so is its Legendre transform
\breve{\phi}.

In order to prepare the proof of this theorem, recall from the Introduction the decomposition

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi(0,x_{2})+\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}),

and denote again by
e_{1}
the (non-horizontal) edge of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm red})
which has
(n,0)
as right endpoint. Recall also the weight
{\kappa}^{e}=({\kappa}^{e}_{1},{\kappa}^{e}_{2})=(1/n,{\kappa}^{e}_{2})
which is chosen so that the edge
e_{1}
lies on the line
L_{1}:=\{t_{1},t_{2}):{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1\}.

Recall also that
0\leq{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2.
We shall distinguish two cases:

a) If
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0,
i.e., if the line
L_{1}
is non-vertical, then the edge
e_{1}
is compact, and we denote by
p=(\phi_{\rm red})_{\kappa}^{e}
the
{\kappa}^{e}
-principal part of
\phi_{\rm red}
(cf.
[
IM16
]
), i.e., the sum of all terms in the Taylor series expansion of
\phi_{\rm red}
corresponding to points on
e_{1}.
Then
p
is
{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous of degree
1,
i.e., if we define the dilations
{\delta}_{r},\,r\geq 0,
associated to
{\kappa}^{e}
by

{\delta}_{r}(x_{1},x_{2}):=(r^{{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}x_{1},r^{{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}x_{2}),
then
p({\delta}_{r}(x_{1},x_{2}))=rp(x_{1},x_{2}).
Thus we may further decompose

(4.1)

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi(0,x_{2})+p(x_{1},x_{2})+\phi_{\rm err}(x_{1},x_{2}),

where
\phi_{\rm err}(x_{1},x_{2})

consists of terms of higher
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree.
By this we mean that there is some
{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\phi_{\rm err}({\delta}_{r}\cdot)=O(r^{1+{\varepsilon}})
for every
r>0,
and the same applies to any
C^{k}
-norm of
\phi_{\rm err}({\delta}_{r}\cdot)
over
{\Omega}.

Indeed, since
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi_{\rm err})\subset\{(t_{1},t_{2}):{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}>1\},
where
{\kappa}^{e}_{1},{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0,
this can indeed easily be seen by means of a Taylor approximation to sufficiently high degree.

b) If
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
i.e., if
L
is the line
t_{1}=n,
then a Taylor expansion in
x_{1}
shows that we may write

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi(0,x_{2})+x_{1}f_{1}(x_{2})+\cdots x_{1}^{n-1}f_{n-1}(x_{2})+x_{1}^{n}b_{n}(x_{2})+\phi_{\rm err}(x_{1},x_{2}),

where the functions
f_{1}(x_{2}),\dots f_{n-1}(x_{2})
are flat,
b_{n}(0)=\beta(0)\neq 0,
and where
\phi_{\rm err}(x_{1},x_{2})
consists of terms of higher
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree (actually with
{\varepsilon}=1
).

We will first consider the case a), in which the following lemma will be crucial. Case b) will simply be reduced to case a) later.

The next result will be proved in the category of (real) analytic functions, but the proof works as well in the category of smooth functions.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that
{\kappa}^{e}=({\kappa}^{e}_{1},{\kappa}^{e}_{2})
is a weight such that
0<{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2
and
{\kappa}^{e}_{1}>0.
Let
\phi
be an analytic function which can be written in the form

\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{2}^{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{2}^{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+p(x_{1},x_{2})+\phi_{\rm err}(x_{1},x_{2}),

with an analytic function
b_{1}
satisfying
b_{1}(0)\neq 0
and an analytic function
\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})
such that
\phi_{\rm red}(0,x_{2})\equiv 0.
We assume that
\phi_{\rm red}
decomposes into a non-zero
{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial
p
of
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
1
such that
p(0,x_{2})=0,

and a remainder term
\phi_{\rm err}
consisting of terms of higher
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree. Then the Legendre transform
\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})
of the function
\phi
with respect to
x_{2}
can be written in the form

\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},cs_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+p(x_{1},cs_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(x_{1},cs_{2}),

where
B
is an analytic function with
B(0)=-\frac{1}{4b_{1}(0)}\neq 0
,
c:=-\frac{1}{2b_{1}(0)}
,
and
\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}
is again an analytic remainder term consisting of terms of higher
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree. Moreover, also

\breve{\phi}_{1}(0,cs_{2})\equiv 0.

Proof.

We begin by studying the equation

\partial_{2}(x_{2}^{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+s_{2}x_{2})=2x_{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+x_{2}^{2}{\partial}_{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+s_{2}=0.

Obviously the equation has an analytic solution
x_{2}(s_{2})
by the implicit function theorem, however, we need more information. For this reason, we first write

x_{2}=ws_{2}
. Then it suffices to solve the equation

2wb_{1}(ws_{2})+s_{2}w^{2}{\partial}_{2}b_{1}(ws_{2})+1=0,

which is possible by the implicit function theorem.
Let
w_{0}(s_{2})
be the unique analytic solution to this equation. Note that
w_{0}(0)=-1/{2b_{1}(0)}
. Then we expand the function
x_{2}^{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+s_{2}x_{2}
in
x_{2}
around the point
s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})
:

(4.2)

\displaystyle x_{2}^{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+s_{2}x_{2}=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+(x_{2}-s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))^{2}B_{2}(x_{2},s_{2}),

where
B(s_{2}):=w_{0}(s_{2})\{1+w_{0}(s_{2})b_{1}(s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\}
and
B_{2}
are analytic functions, with
B(0)=-1/{4b_{1}(0)}
and
B_{2}(x_{2},0)=b_{1}(x_{2})
.

We also expand

\displaystyle\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))+(x_{2}-s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})){\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))

\displaystyle+(x_{2}-s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))^{2}G(x_{1},x_{2},s_{2}),

where
G(x_{1},x_{2},s_{2})
is an analytic function with
G(0,0,0)={\partial}_{2}^{2}\phi_{\rm red}(0,0)=0,
since
\kappa^{e}_{2}<1/2
(note, e.g, that
{\partial}_{2}^{2}p
is
{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous of degree
1-2{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0
).

For the Legendre transform, we seek a solution
x_{2}^{c}=x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})
to the equation

{\partial}_{2}\phi(x_{1},x_{2}^{c})+s_{2}=0.

According to (
4.2
) and the subsequent expansion of
\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}),
this equation can be
re-written in the form

(x_{2}-s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))G_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},s_{2})+{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))=0,

where
G_{1}
is an analytic function with
G_{1}(0,0,0)=2(G(0,0,0)+B_{2}(0,0))=2b_{1}(0)
.

By a similar scaling argument as before, we see that the solution to the last equation is of the form

(4.3)

\displaystyle x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})+{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\,g(x_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{2}w_{0}(0)+s_{2}^{2}\tilde{w}_{0}(s_{2})+{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\,g(x_{1},s_{2}),

where
g(x_{1},s_{2})
is an analytic function (to be determined) with
g(0,0)=-1/{2b_{1}(0,0)}
.

Then we look at

(4.4)

\displaystyle\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))+s_{2}x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})^{2}b_{1}(x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))+s_{2}x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})\Big)+\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})).

It is easy to see by (
4.3
) and a Taylor expansion in
x_{2}
around the point
s_{2}w_{0}(0)
of

\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(0)+s_{2}^{2}\tilde{w}_{0}(s_{2})+{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\,g(x_{1},s_{2}))

that the term
\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))=p(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))+\phi_{\rm err}(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))
is equal to the
{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial
p(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(0))
plus a remainder term consisting of terms of higher
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree. Here we use indeed that
0<{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2.

To deal with what happens if we plug
x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})
into
x_{2}^{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+s_{2}x_{2},
we use (
4.2
) and (
4.3
):

(4.5)

\displaystyle(x_{2}^{2}b_{1}(x_{2})+s_{2}x_{2})|_{x_{2}=x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},x_{2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+\Big({\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\,g(x_{1},s_{2})\Big)^{2}B_{2}(x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},x_{2}),s_{2}).

The first term
s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})
is just what we expect. For the second term, observe
that
{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))g(x_{1},s_{2})=\{{\partial}_{2}p(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))+{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm err}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\}g(x_{1},s_{2})
is a sum of monomials of
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
1-{\kappa}^{e}_{2}
and error terms of even higher
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree. But then

(x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},x_{2})-s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))^{2}
consists of terms of
{\kappa}^{e}
-degree at least
2-2\kappa^{e}_{2}>1
since
\kappa^{e}_{2}<1/2
. In other words, the second term is a remainder term too.

Our discussion also shows that
\breve{\phi}_{1}(0,cs_{2})\equiv 0.

Q.E.D.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
4.1
. Again, we shall work in the category of analytic functions, but the proof works as well in the category of smooth functions.

Assume that the function
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}.
We begin by showing that its Legendre transform
\breve{\phi}
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}
too.

Suppose first that we are in case a), i.e., that
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0.
This applies in particular if the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are not line-adapted (recall from Proposition
3.1
that the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are line-adapted if
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0
).
Here, our claims follow directly from Lemma
4.2
.

Next consider case b) where
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0.
Then clearly
n_{e}(\phi)=n,
and
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}_{n-1}.

Given any sufficiently small
{\kappa}^{\prime}_{2}>0,
we then decompose
\phi
in the form (
4.1
), but now with respect to the weight

{\kappa}^{\prime}:=(1/n,{\kappa}^{\prime}_{2}),
i.e., we choose for the polynomial
p
the
{\kappa}^{\prime}
-principal part
p_{{\kappa}^{\prime}}
of
\phi_{\rm red},
which is
{\kappa}^{\prime}
-homogeneous of degree
1
. Then clearly we have
p_{{\kappa}^{\prime}}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}^{n}b_{n}(0)=x_{1}^{n}\beta(0).
We may thus apply Lemma
4.2
with
{\kappa}^{\prime}
in place of
{\kappa}^{e}
and find that
\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+p_{{\kappa}^{\prime}}(x_{1},cs_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(x_{1},s_{2}),
which implies that
j/n+{\kappa}_{2}^{\prime}k\geq 1
for every point
(j,k)
in the Taylor support of
\breve{\phi}_{\rm red}.
Letting
{\kappa}^{\prime}_{2}
tend to
0,
we see that
j/n\geq 1,
so that
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}_{\rm red})
is contained in the region where
t_{1}\geq n.
This implies that again
\breve{\phi}
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1},
and that
n_{e}(\breve{\phi})=n=n_{e}(\phi),
so that
\breve{\phi}
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}_{n-1}
too.

In the second step of the proof, we show that
n_{e}(\breve{\phi})=n_{e}(\phi).
In the case where the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are line-adapted to
\phi,
this is again immediate from Lemma
4.2
when
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0,
and for the case
{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0
we have already verified this in the first step of the proof.

There remains the case where the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
are not line-adapted to
\phi.

As shown in the proof of Proposition
3.3
then there exists an analytic function
\alpha(x_{2})
with
\alpha(0)=0

such that
\phi_{\rm red}
can be written as

(4.6)

\displaystyle\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{n}(x_{1},x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{n}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle B_{n-2}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{n-2}+\dots+B_{1}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2})),

where the functions
B_{j}
are smooth and
B_{n}(0,0)=\beta(0)\neq 0,B_{j}(0)=0
for
j=1,\dots,n-2,
and so that the coordinates
y=(y_{1},y_{2}):=(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}),x_{2})
are line-adapted. In these coordinates,
\phi_{\rm red}
is given by

(4.7)

\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})=B_{n}(y_{1}+{\alpha}(y_{2}),y_{2})y_{1}^{n}+B_{n-2}(y_{2})y_{1}^{n-2}+\dots+B_{1}(y_{2})y_{1}.

We denote by
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
the edge of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red})
which has
(n,0)
as right endpoint, and choose the weight
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}=(\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1},\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})=(1/n,\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})
so that
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is contained in the line
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1.

Note that if we just work with smooth functions, then
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0
if and only if all of the functions
B_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n-2,
are flat, but in the category of analytic functions they even do vanish. In any case we see that if
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
then
n_{e}^{y}=n,
so that
n_{e}(\phi)=n.

Let us first consider the case where
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0.
As before, we decompose

(4.8)

\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})=\tilde{p}(y_{1},y_{2})+\tilde{\phi}_{\rm err}(y_{1},y_{2}),

where
\tilde{p}
is a
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial of degree
1
, and
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm err}
consists of terms of higher
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree. Next, following the proof of Lemma
4.2
, we apply (
4.4
) to decompose
\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})
as a sum of two terms. We first look at the term

\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})),
which according to (
4.3
) we can write as

(4.9)

\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))=\phi_{\rm red}\big(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})+{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\,g(x_{1},s_{2})\big).

Let us assume without loss of generality that
g(0,0)=1.
Then it will suffice to understand
\phi_{\rm red}\big(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})+{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\big),
since the passage from
g=1
to our more general function
g
will only add terms of higher
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
- degree.

Let us put
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}):={\alpha}(s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})),
and introduce the new coordinate
z_{1}:=x_{1}-\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}).
We will show that in the coordinates
(z_{1},s_{2})
,
\breve{\phi}
is line-adapted.

We apply a Taylor expansion in the second variable of the right-hand side in (
4.9
) (with
g=1
) around the point
s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}).
Then, according to (
4.6
), the leading term is given by

\phi_{\rm red}\big(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))=\tilde{B}_{n}(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{n}+\tilde{B}_{n-2}(s_{2})z_{1}^{n-2}+\dots+\tilde{B}_{1}(s_{2})z_{1},

where
\tilde{B}_{n}(z_{1},s_{2}):=B_{n}(z_{1}+\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}),s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))
and
\tilde{B}_{j}(s_{2}):=B_{j}(s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))
for
j=1,\dots,n-2.

A comparison with (
4.7
) then shows that
\phi_{\rm red}\big(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))=\tilde{p}(z_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(0))
plus terms of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
- degree
>1.

The second term in this Taylor expansion will be given by

\big({\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})\big)^{2}.

Looking at (
4.6
),
{\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2})
is a sum of terms
B_{j}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{j},j=1,\dots,n-2,
and
B_{n}(x_{1},x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{n}.

And, for
j=1,\dots,n-2,

{\partial}_{2}\big(B_{j}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{j}\big)={\partial}_{2}B_{j}(x_{2})\,(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{j}-j{\alpha}^{\prime}(x_{2})B_{j}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{j-1},

so that

{\partial}_{2}\big(B_{j}(x_{2})(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}))^{j}\big)|_{x_{2}=s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})}={\partial}_{2}B_{j}(s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))\,z_{1}^{j}-j{\alpha}^{\prime}(s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))B_{j}(s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2}))z_{1}^{j-1};

a similar reasoning holds for
j=n.
A comparison with (
4.7
) then shows that all of these summands consist, in the coordinates
(z_{1},s_{2}),
of terms of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
\geq 1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2},
or
\geq 1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}.
Since
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2
and also
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}=1/n<1/2,
this shows that
\big({\partial}_{2}\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})\big)^{2}
consists of error terms of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
>1.

A similar reasoning applies also to the higher order terms in the afore mentioned Taylor expansion, so that we see that

\phi_{\rm red}(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))=\tilde{p}(z_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(0))
plus terms of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
- degree
>1.

For the first term in (
4.4
), we use again (
4.5
). The first term
s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})
is exactly what we expect, and the second term consists of error terms of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
>1,
as we have just seen.

Combining everything, we see that in the coordinates
(z_{1},s_{2}),
in which
\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})
is represented by the function
\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}(z_{1},s_{2}),
we have

(4.10)

\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}(z_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+\tilde{p}(z_{1},s_{2}w_{0}(0))\ +\text{ terms of $\tilde{\kappa}^{e}$ - degree $>1.$ }

This shows that the coordinates
(z_{1},s_{2})
are line-adapted to
\breve{\phi},
and that
n_{e}(\breve{\phi})=n_{e}(\phi).
It also shows that if
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}_{n-1},
then so is
\breve{\phi}.

Assume finally that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
so that
n_{e}(\phi)=n.
We thus have to show that also
n_{e}(\breve{\phi})=n.
To this end, we can apply the same kind of trick that we used before to handle case b). For any sufficiently small
\tilde{\kappa}^{\prime}_{2}>0,
we decompose
\breve{\phi}
is an (
4.8
), but now with respect to the weight
\tilde{\kappa}^{\prime}:=(1/n,\tilde{\kappa}^{\prime}_{2}).
Then clearly
\tilde{p}(y_{1},y_{2})=B_{n}(0,0)y_{1}^{n}
(note that the functions
B_{j}(y_{2}),j=1,\dots,n-2,
in (
4.7
) are now all flat!). Thus, arguing as before, we see that
j/n+\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{\prime}k\geq 1
for every point
(j,k)
in the Taylor support of
\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}_{\rm red}.
Letting
\tilde{\kappa}^{\prime}_{2}
tend to
0,
we see that
j/n\geq 1,
so that
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}_{\rm red})
is contained in the region where
t_{1}\geq n.

Q.E.D.

5.
Preparatory steps in the proofs of Theorems
1.2
and
1.9

5.1.
Auxiliary results

Let us first recall a classical version of van der Corput’s lemma (cf. Corollary to Proposition 2 in
[
S93
, VIII]
).

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose
\phi
is smooth and real-valued in
(u,v)
such that
|\phi^{(m)}(x)|\geq 1
for all
x\in(u,v),
where
m\geq 2.
Then, for every smooth complex amplitude
a
on
[u,v]
we have that

\Big|\int_{u}^{v}e^{i{\lambda}\phi(x)}a(x)\,dx\Big|\leq c_{m}{\lambda}^{-\frac{1}{m}}\Big[|a(v)|+\int_{u}^{v}|a^{\prime}(x)|\,dx\Big].

We shall also need the following lemma, which is closely related to
[
IM16
, Lemma 5.6]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Consider the oscillatory integral

I(u,\zeta):=\iint e^{iN\big(\phi(x,\zeta)+\eta(u-x)\big)}a(x,\eta,u,\zeta)\,d\eta dx,

where
a
is a smooth, compactly supported amplitude function on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p},

\phi
is a real-valued smooth amplitude function defined on a neighborhood of the projection of the support of
a
to the
(x,\zeta)
-variables, and
N\gg 1.
We also assume that we have uniform bounds on the partial derivatives of
a
and
\phi
of any order.
Then

I(u,\zeta)=N^{-1}e^{iN\phi(u,\zeta)}b(u,\zeta,N^{-1}),

where
b
is a smooth amplitude with compact support whose partial derivatives can be controlled by means of the uniform bounds that we have postulated on
a
and
\phi.

Remarks 5.3
.

i) Note that the phase function
\phi(u,\zeta)
is just the value of the function
(x,\eta)\mapsto\phi(x,\zeta)+\eta(u-x)
at its unique critical point
(x^{c},\eta^{c}):=(u,{\partial}_{x}\phi(u,\zeta)).
Thus, at least formally, the result is a consequence of the method of stationary phase, but our subsequent direct argument is simpler and leads faster to the claimed estimates.

ii) It will become important later on to recall that the critical value, i.e., the value of a function at its critical point, is invariant under coordinate changes in the argument of the function.

Proof.

Denoting by
{\mathcal{F}}_{\eta}
the partial Fourier transform in
\eta,
we have

\displaystyle I(u,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int e^{iN\phi(x,\zeta)}{\mathcal{F}}_{\eta}a(x,N(x-u),u,\zeta)\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-1}\int e^{iN\phi(u+tN^{-1},\zeta)}{\mathcal{F}}_{\eta}a(u+tN^{-1},t,u,\zeta)\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-1}e^{iN\phi(u,\zeta)}b(u,\zeta,N^{-1}),

where

b(u,\zeta,N^{-1}):=\int e^{i\psi(u,tN^{-1},\zeta)}{\mathcal{F}}_{\eta}a(u+tN^{-1},t,u,\zeta)\,dt,

where by Taylor

\psi(u,tN^{-1},\zeta):=t\int\limits_{0}^{1}{\partial}_{x}\phi(u+stN^{-1},\zeta)\,ds.

Our claim now follows easily since
{\mathcal{F}}_{\eta}a(u+tN^{-1},t,u,\zeta)
is rapidly decaying in
t.

Q.E.D.

Another important tool for us will be the following result (cf.
[
BDIM19
, Proposition 4.2]
):
If
A
is a bounded Lebesgue measurable subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n},
then we denote by
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
the corresponding maximal operator

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}f(x):=\sup_{t>0}\int_{A}|f(x+ty)|\,dy,\qquad f\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}).

In particular, if
A=B_{1}(0)
is the Euclidean unit ball, then
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is the Hardy-Littlewood maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm HL}.

Denote further by
\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\to S^{n-1}
the
spherical projection
onto the unit sphere
S^{n-1},
given by
\pi(x):=x/|x|,
and by
|\pi(A)|
the
n-1
-dimensional volume of this set with respect to the surface measure on the sphere.

Lemma 5.4
.

Assume that
A
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
contained in an annulus where
|x|\sim 1.

Then, for
1<p\leq\infty,
we have that

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{A}f\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}\leq C_{p}|\pi(A)|.

5.2.
Dyadic frequency decomposition

To prove our main theorems, we shall follow in the first steps the discussion of
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
- type singularities in Subsection 7.1 of
[
BDIM19
]
.

Recall that the maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}_{S}
is associated to the averaging
operators of convolution with dilates of a measure
\mu,

whose Fourier transform at
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
is given by

(5.1)

\hat{\mu}(\xi):=\int e^{-i(\xi_{1}x_{1}+\xi_{2}x_{2}+\xi_{3}(1+\phi(x_{1},x_{2})))}\eta(x_{1},x_{2})\,dx_{1}dx_{2}.

In order to estimate this maximal operator, we shall perform a non-homogeneous
dyadic frequency decomposition with respect to the last variable
\xi_{3}.
Since the low frequency part is easily controlled by the Hardy-Littlewood
maximal operator, we shall subsequently concentrate on the regions where
\pm\xi_{3}\sim{\lambda},
with
{\lambda}\gg 1
a sufficiently large dyadic number.

To be more specific, let us choose suitable smooth cut-off functions
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}

of sufficiently small compact supports, where
\chi_{1}
vanishes near the origin and is identically one near
1,
whereas
\chi_{0}
is identically
1
on a small neighborhood of the origin. Then, for a fixed dyadic
{\lambda}\gg 1,
we shall concentrate on the contribution by
\chi_{1}\left({\xi_{3}}/{\lambda}\right)\hat{\mu}(\xi).
We shall later see that the estimates that we shall obtain for the corresponding maximal operators will sum over all such dyadic numbers
{\lambda}.

As a further reduction, let us decompose these contributions into

\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi):=\chi_{0}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\lambda},\,\frac{\xi_{2}}{\lambda}\right)\chi_{1}\left(\frac{\xi_{3}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{\mu}(\xi),

and

\left(1-\chi_{0}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\lambda},\,\frac{\xi_{2}}{\lambda}\right)\right)\chi_{1}\left(\frac{\xi_{3}}{\lambda}\right)\hat{\mu}(\xi).

Note that if we choose the support of the amplitude
\eta
in (
5.1
) sufficiently small, then
integrations by parts easily show that the latter term is of
order
O({\lambda}^{-N})
for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
as
{\lambda}\to+\infty,
so
that the corresponding contribution to the maximal operators is
under control.

It therefore suffices to control the contribution by
\mu^{\lambda}.

As in
[
IM16
]
and
[
BDIM19
]
, we write

\xi_{3}={\lambda}s_{3},\quad\xi_{1}={\lambda}s_{3}s_{1},\quad\xi_{2}={\lambda}s_{3}s_{2},

and put
s^{\prime}:=(s_{1},s_{2}),s:=(s^{\prime},s_{3}).
Then, choosing the supports of
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
sufficiently small, we have

|s_{3}|\sim 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad|s^{\prime}|\ll 1

on the support of
\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}
. Hence

(5.2)

\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)=e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{1}x_{1}+s_{2}x_{2}+\phi(x))}\eta(x)\,dx.

6.
First steps in the proof of Theorem
1.2

In this section, we assume that
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{-},
with
n\geq 4.
Recall that then

2m\leq n\quad\text{and }\quad h=\frac{2n}{n+2}.

6.1.
Stationary phase in
x_{2}
and identities for
\mu^{\lambda}

We first apply the method of stationary phase to the
x_{2}
- integration in (
5.2
).
Recall that for
\phi
of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{-}
we have
2m\leq n,
so that both
m
and
n
are finite. We recall that the coordinates
(x_{1},y_{2})
with
y_{2}:=x_{2}-\psi(x_{1})
are adapted to
\phi.

In these coordinates,
\phi
is of the form
\phi^{a}(x_{1},y_{2})=b^{a}(x_{1},y_{2})y_{2}^{2}+b_{0}(x_{1}),
where we had written
b_{0}(x_{1})=x_{1}^{n}{\beta}(x_{1})
and
\psi(x_{1})=x_{1}^{m}{\omega}(x_{1}),
with
{\beta}(0)\neq 0
and
{\omega}(0)\neq 0.
Changing coordinates in (
5.2
), we see that

\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)=e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{1}x_{1}+s_{2}\psi(x_{1})+s_{2}y_{2}+\phi^{a}(x_{1},y_{2})}\eta(x_{1},y_{2}+\psi(x_{1}))\,dy_{2}dx_{1}.

Applying the method of stationary phase to the
y_{2}
-integration, this leads to

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-is_{3}{\lambda}}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\big[{\lambda}^{-1/2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{1}x_{1}+s_{2}\psi(x_{1})+\phi^{a}(x_{1},y_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})))}\tilde{\eta}(x_{1},s_{2})\,dx_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt+r({\lambda},s)\big],

with a slightly modified cut-off function
\chi_{1},

where
\tilde{\eta}
is another smooth bump function supported in a
sufficiently small neighborhood of the origin,
r({\lambda},s)
is a
remainder term of order

r({\lambda},s)=O({\lambda}^{-\frac{3}{2}})\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\lambda}\to+\infty,

and
y_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})
denotes the unique (non-degenerate)
critical point of the phase
\phi^{a}
with respect to
y_{2}.
Then
\phi^{a}(x_{1},y_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))=\breve{\phi}^{a}(x_{1},s_{2})
is the Legendre transform of
\phi^{a}.

Applying a similar scaling argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.2
, we see that locally near the origin there exists a unique smooth function
w(x_{1},s_{2})
with
w(0,0)=-1/b^{a}(0,0)
such that
y_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}w(x_{1},s_{2}).
This implies that
\breve{\phi}^{a}(x_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}\breve{b}^{a}(x_{1},s_{2})+x_{1}^{n}{\beta}(x_{1}),
where

\breve{b}^{a}
is smooth, with
\breve{b}^{a}(0,0)=-1/4b^{a}(0,0)\neq 0.
Taylor expansion around
x_{1}=0
then shows that
\breve{\phi}^{a}(x_{1},s_{2})
can be written as

(6.1)

\breve{\phi}^{a}(x_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+s_{2}^{2}x_{1}q(x_{1},s_{2})+x_{1}^{n}{\beta}(x_{1}),

with a smooth functions
B
and
q,
where
B(0)\neq 0
.

The contribution of the error term
r({\lambda},s)
to
\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}},
which we denote by
\mu^{\lambda}_{\rm error},

and the corresponding maximal operator
{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{\rm error}
are easily estimated, and we
shall henceforth ignore it.

Indeed, let us briefly sketch the argument: since
\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}_{\rm error}}=O({\lambda}^{-\frac{3}{2}}),

by applying the usual arguments to control the maximal operator on
L^{2},
which are based on almost orthogonality and a variant of Sobolev’s embedding theorem (as for instance in the proof of
[
BDIM19
, Proposition 4.1]
), we obtain that

\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{\rm error}\|_{L^{2}\mapsto L^{2}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\lambda}^{-\frac{3}{2}}=C{\lambda}^{-1}.

On the other hand, we find that
|\mu^{\lambda}_{\rm error}(y+\Gamma)|\lesssim{\lambda}^{3/2}
(compare (
6.5
), where the exponent
5/2=3-1/2
has to be replaced by
3-3/2=3/2
), and by Lemma
5.4
this implies that for every
{\varepsilon}>0
,

\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{\rm error}\|_{L^{1+{\varepsilon}}\mapsto L^{1+{\varepsilon}}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}{\lambda}^{-1}.

Interpolation between these estimates leads to

\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{\rm error}\|_{L^{p}\mapsto L^{p}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}(\frac{2}{p}-1)+{\varepsilon}}{\lambda}^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{5}{p}-\frac{7}{2}+{\varepsilon}}

for every
{\varepsilon}>0.
However, we are assuming that
p>3/2,
so that
5/p-7/2<-1/6,
which shows that the estimates can be summed over all dyadic
{\lambda}
s.

Putting

(6.2)

\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2}):=x_{1}^{n}\beta(x_{1})+s_{2}x_{1}^{m}\omega(x_{1})+s_{2}^{2}x_{1}q(x_{1},s_{2}),

we thus see that finally may assume that

(6.3)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(s_{1}x_{1}+s_{2}x_{1}^{n}{\omega}(x_{1})+\breve{\phi}^{a}(x_{1},s_{2})+1\big)}\,\eta(x_{1},s_{2})dx_{1}\,\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+1\big)}J({\lambda},s)\,\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3}),

where we have put

(6.4)

J({\lambda},s):=\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1})}\eta(x_{1},s_{2})\,dx_{1}.

In order to defray the notation, we have simply dropped the tilde from
\eta.

Note also that, by Fourier inversion and a change from the coordinates
\xi
to
s,
we have that (with slightly modified functions
\chi_{0},\chi_{1}
)

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mu^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}\int J({\lambda},s)e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}-s_{2}y_{2}-y_{3}\big)}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}\int\Big(\int F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-s_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{0}(s_{2})ds_{2}\Big)\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3},

where we have put
\Gamma:=(0,0,1),
and

(6.6)

\displaystyle F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int J({\lambda},s)e^{i{\lambda}s_{3}s_{1}y_{1}}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{0}(s_{1})\,ds_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1}-s_{1}y_{1})}\eta(x_{1},s_{2})\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{0}(s_{1})\,dx_{1}ds_{1}.

Quite important for us will thus be the function

(6.7)

\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime}):=\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1},

which allows to re-write

\displaystyle J({\lambda},s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime})}\eta(x_{1},s_{2})\,dx_{1},

\displaystyle F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime})-s_{1}y_{1})}\eta(x_{1},s_{2})\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{0}(s_{1})\,dx_{1}ds_{1}.

We should like to mention that the variable
s_{3},
which is of size
s_{3}\sim 1,
will be irrelevant for our analysis, so that we hall basically consider it to be frozen.

In view our definition of the oscillatory integral
J({\lambda},s)
and van der Corput’s lemma, what will be quite important for our analysis will indeed be the second derivative of
\Phi_{0}
with respect to the variable
x_{1},
i.e., the function

(6.8)

\Phi(x_{1},s_{2}):={\partial}_{x_{1}}^{2}\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime})={\partial}_{x_{1}}^{2}\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2}),

which depends only on the integration variable
x_{1}
in
J({\lambda},s),
and the “parameter”
s_{2}.
In order to understand the oscillatory integrals
J({\lambda},s)
and
F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}),
it turns out that we need to understand the singularities of the function

\Phi(x_{1},s_{2})
as a function of
x_{1}
and
s_{2},
and to this end we shall follow ideas from
[
PS97
]
,
[
PSS99
]
,
[
IKM10
]
,
[
IM11a
]
,
[
IM16
]
and related papers, by looking at Newton polyhedra associated to
\Phi
and the related Puiseux series expansions of the roots of
\Phi.

Remark 6.1
.

In its definition, as well as in the proofs of our main Theorems
1.2
and
1.9
, we shall make use of certain local coordinate changes of the form (
1.7
), in which the roles of the variables
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are interchanged, compared to the changes of coordinates that had been used to pass to adapted coordinates in the monograph
[
IM16
]
and related papers, such as
[
IM11a
]
.

Thus, when comparing with Newton polyhedra in these articles, one should imaging the coordinate axis to be interchanged compared to the present situation.

This should be taken into account when comparing the discussions in the next subsection with the discussions in these papers.

6.2.
Description of the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
of
\Phi
in terms of the roots.

In the course of our arguments, by applying an algorithm for a resolution of singularities, we shall have to change coordinates from
x_{1}
to
z_{\iota}
in the step
\iota.
Therefore let us begin by writing
z_{1}:=x_{1}
in Step 1 of this algorithm, where we work in our original coordinate
x_{1},
so that

\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})=\Phi(x_{1},s_{2})={\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2}).

Since
\Phi
is real-analytic and real valued, the Weierstraß preparation theorem allows to write

\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})=U_{0}(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{\nu_{1}}s_{2}^{\nu_{2}}\Psi(z_{1},s_{2})

near the origin, where
\Psi(x_{1},s_{2})
is a pseudo-polynomial of the form

\Psi(z_{1},s_{2})=z_{1}^{N}+a_{1}(s_{2})z_{1}^{N-1}+\dots+a_{N}(s_{2}),\qquad(\nu_{1}+N=n-2),

and
U_{0},\,a_{1},\dots,a_{N}
are real-analytic functions satisfying

U_{0}(0,0)\neq 0,\,a_{j}(0)=0
. By (
6.2
), we here have
\nu_{2}=0.

Observe that the Newton polyhedron of
\Phi
is the same as that of
z_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\Psi(z_{1},s_{2}).
We shall also assume without loss of generality that
a_{N}
is a non-trivial function, so that the roots
r(s_{2})
of the equation
\Psi(z_{1},s_{2})=0,
considered as a polynomial in
z_{1},
are all non-trivial.

It is well-known that these roots can be expressed in a small neighborhood of
0
as Puiseux series

r(s_{2})=c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}+c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}+\cdots+c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}}+\cdots,

where

c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}{\beta}}\neq c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}{\gamma}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\beta}\neq{\gamma},

a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}>a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p-1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-2}},

with strictly positive exponents
a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}>0
and non-zero complex coefficients
c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}\neq 0,
and where we have kept enough terms to distinguish between all the non-identical roots of
\Psi.

By the
cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right],

we shall designate all the roots
r(s_{2})
, counted with their multiplicities,
which satisfy

r(s_{2})-\Big(c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}+c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}+\cdots+c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}}\Big)=O(s_{2}^{b})\

for some exponent
b>a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}
. The corresponding function

c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}+c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}+\cdots+c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}}

will be called the
leading jet
of the sub-cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right].

We
also introduce the sub-clusters

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p-1}&\cdot\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p-1}&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right],

by

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p-1}&\cdot\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p-1}&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right]:=\bigcup\limits_{{\alpha}_{p}}\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right].

Each index
{\alpha}_{p}
or
l_{p}
varies in some finite range which we shall not specify here. We finally put

N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right]:=\mbox{number of roots in}\,\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right],

N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p-1}&\cdot\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p-1}&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right]:=\mbox{number of roots in}\,\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p-1}&\cdot\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p-1}&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right]

The number

N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right]

will be called the
multiplicity of the sub-cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right].

Let
a_{1}<\dots<a_{l}<\dots<a_{L}
be the distinct leading exponents of all the
roots of
\Psi.
Each exponent
a_{l}
corresponds to the cluster
\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right],
so that the set of all roots of
\Psi
can be divided as
\bigcup\limits_{l=1}^{L}\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]
.
Then we may write

(6.9)

\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})=U_{0}(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\prod_{l=1}^{L}\Phi\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right](z_{1},s_{2}),

where

\Phi\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right](z_{1},s_{2}):=\prod_{r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}(z_{1}-r(s_{2})).

We introduce the following quantities:

(6.10)

B_{l}=B\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]:=\nu_{1}+\sum_{\mu\geq l+1}N\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
\mu\end{matrix}\right],\quad A_{l}=A\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]:=\sum_{\mu\leq l}a_{\mu}N\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
\mu\end{matrix}\right].

Notice that
B_{l}
is just the number of all roots with leading exponent strictly greater than
a_{l}
(where we here interpret the trivial roots corresponding to the factor
(z_{1}-0)^{\nu_{1}}
in our representation of
\Phi
as roots with exponent
+\infty
),
and that

B_{0}>B_{1}>\dots>B_{L},\qquad A_{0}<A_{1}\dots<A_{L}.

If
N_{{\rm all}}
denotes the total number of all roots of
\Phi
away from the axis
s_{2}=0,
including the trivial ones and counted with their multiplicities, then we can also write

(6.11)

B_{l}=N_{\rm all}-\sum_{\mu\leq l}N\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
\mu\end{matrix}\right].

Then the vertices of the Newton diagram
{\mathcal{N}}_{d}(\Phi)
of
\Phi
are the points

(B_{l},A_{l})),\ l=0,\dots,L,
and the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
is the convex hull
of the set
\cup((B_{l},A_{l})+{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2})
(compare Observation 1 in
[
PS97
]
).

Figure 2.
Edges

Notice also that

A_{l}+a_{l}B_{l}=A_{l-1}+a_{l}B_{l-1},

so that, if we put
\Delta A_{l}:=A_{l}-A_{l-1},\Delta B_{l}:=B_{l}-B_{l-1},
then

a_{l}=\frac{\Delta A_{l}}{-\Delta B_{l}}

is just the (modulus) of the slope of the edge
{\gamma}_{l}:=[(B_{l},A_{l}),(B_{l-1},A_{l-1})]
(compare Figure
2
).

Let
L_{l}:=\{(t_{1},t_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}:{\kappa}^{l}_{1}t_{1}+{\kappa}^{l}_{2}t_{2}=1\}
denote the line supporting this edge
{\gamma}_{l}.

It is easy to see that it is given by

\displaystyle{\kappa}^{l}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta A_{l}}{A_{l}\Delta B_{l}-B_{l}\Delta A_{l}}=\frac{a_{l}}{A_{l}+a_{l}B_{l}},

\displaystyle{\kappa}^{l}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta B_{l}}{A_{l}\Delta B_{l}-B_{l}\Delta A_{l}}=\frac{1}{A_{l}+a_{l}B_{l}}.

This defines a weight
{\kappa}^{l}:=({\kappa}_{1}^{l},{\kappa}_{2}^{l})
associated to the edge
{\gamma}_{l},
and we see that

\frac{{\kappa}^{l}_{1}}{{\kappa}^{l}_{2}}=a_{l}.

Note that

a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{l}<\dots<a_{L}.

Finally, fix
l,
and let us determine the
{\kappa}^{l}
-principal part
\Phi_{{\kappa}^{l}}
of
\Phi
corresponding to the supporting line
L_{l}.
To this end, observe that
\Phi
has the same
{\kappa}^{l}
-principal part as the function

U_{0}(0,0)z_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\prod_{{\alpha},\mu}\Big(z_{1}-c^{\alpha}_{\mu}s_{2}^{a_{\mu}}\Big)^{N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}\\
\mu\end{matrix}\right]}.

Moreover, the
{\kappa}^{l}
-principal part of
z_{1}-c^{\alpha}_{\mu}s_{2}^{a_{\mu}}
is given by
-c^{\alpha}_{\mu}s_{2}^{a_{\mu}},
if
\mu<l,
and by
z_{1}
if
\mu>l.
This implies that

(6.12)

\Phi_{{\kappa}^{l}}(z_{1},s_{2})=c_{l}\,s_{2}^{A_{l-1}}z_{1}^{B_{l}}\prod_{\alpha}\Big(z_{1}-c^{\alpha}_{l}s_{2}^{a_{l}}\Big)^{N{\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}}.

In view of this identity, we shall say that the edge
{\gamma}_{l}
is
associated
to the cluster of roots
{\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}.

We collect these results in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

The vertices of the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
of
\Phi
are the points

(B_{l},A_{l}),

l=0,\dots,L,
with
B_{j},A_{j}
given by (
6.10
), and its compact edges are the intervals

{\gamma}_{l}:=[(B_{l},A_{l}),(B_{l-1},A_{l-1})],

l=1,\dots,L.
Moreover the
{\kappa}^{l}
-principal part of
\Phi
corresponding to the supporting line
L_{l}
through the edge
{\gamma}_{l}
is given by (
6.12
).

Based on the information given in this subsection and following ideas from
[
PS97
]
, as well as from
[
IKM10
]
or
[
IM16
]
, we shall apply a
resolution (of singularity) algorithm
which will allow us to reduce considerations step by step to suitable neighborhoods of smaller sub-clusters of roots until one ends up with a neighborhood a sub-cluster containing only one single root (with multiplicity)

7.
Analysis in Step 1 of the resolution algorithm

We begin by working in our original coordinate
x_{1},
and recall that we want to denote
x_{1}
by
z_{1}.
Recall also that we are restring attention to the upper half-plane
s_{2}>0.

We shall decompose this half-plane into
{\kappa}^{l}
-
homogeneous domains

D_{l}=D_{l}^{(1)}
of the form

D_{l}:=\{(z_{1},s_{2}):2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l}}\leq|z_{1}|\leq 2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{l}}\},\quad l=1,\dots,L,

and
transition domains

\displaystyle E_{0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(z_{1},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{1}}<|z_{1}|\},

\displaystyle E_{l}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(z_{1},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{l+1}}<|z_{1}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l}}\},\quad l=1,\dots,L-1,

\displaystyle E_{L}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(z_{1},s_{2}):|z_{1}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{L}}\}

between two such domains of different type of homogeneity.
Here,
M\gg 1
is an integer which will have to be chosen sufficiently large later on.

Figure 3.
Domain Decomposition

To localize to domains of type
D_{l}
in a smooth way, we put more precisely

\rho_{l}(z_{1},s_{2}):=\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{z_{1}}{2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{l}}}\Big)-\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{z_{1}}{2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l}}}\Big),\quad l=1,\dots,L,

and replace
J({\lambda},s)
in (
6.4
) by

J^{\rho_{l}}({\lambda},s):=\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}z_{1})}\eta(z_{1},s_{2})\rho_{l}(z_{1},s_{2})\,dz_{1}.

The corresponding functions such as
\mu^{\lambda}
and
F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}),
and the corresponding maximal operator will be designated by means an extra upper index
\rho_{l},
such as

\mu^{\rho_{l},{\lambda}},

F^{\rho_{l}}({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\rho_{l}}.

Similarly, in order to localize to domains of type
E_{l},
we put, for
l=1,\dots,L-1,

\displaystyle\tau_{l}(z_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{z_{1}}{2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l}}}\Big)\,-\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{z_{1}}{2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{l+1}}}\Big),

and define
\tau_{0}
and
\tau_{L}
by means of the obvious modifications of this definition. The corresponding functions and the corresponding maximal operator will be denoted by
\mu^{\tau_{l},{\lambda}},F^{\tau_{l}}({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}),{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau_{l}},
… .

Recall that we may assume that
|s_{2}|<{\varepsilon},
for any given
{\varepsilon}>0.
It is then easy to see that, once we have chosen
M,
by assuming that
{\varepsilon}>0
is chosen sufficiently small, then
2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l}}>2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{l+1}}
for every
l.

Then the functions
\rho_{l},l=1,\dots L,
jointly with the functions
\tau_{l},l=0,\dots L,
form a partition of unity consisting of non-negative functions.

Finally note that it suffices to suitably control all the maximal operators defined in this way in order to prove the first statement in Theorem
1.2
.

7.1.
Dyadic localization in
z_{1}
and
s_{2}

Let us localize to

|z_{1}|\sim 2^{-j},\ s_{2}\sim 2^{-k},

where we may assume that
j,k
are sufficiently large integers
\gg 1.
To this end, we shall define scaled coordinates
z,{\sigma}_{2}
by writing

z_{1}=2^{-j}z,\ s_{2}=2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},
so that

|z|\sim 1,\ s_{2}\sim 1.

Accordingly, let

\displaystyle J_{j,k}({\lambda},s)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\chi_{1}(2^{k}s_{2})\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}z_{1})}\eta(z_{1},s_{2})\chi_{1}(2^{j}z_{1})\,dz_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,2^{-j}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(2^{-j}z,s_{2})+2^{-j}s_{1}z)}\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})\,dz,

and

J^{\tau_{l}}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)=\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,2^{-j}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(2^{-j}z,s_{2})+2^{-j}s_{1}z)}\chi_{1}(z)\,(\tau_{l}\eta)(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})\,dz.

The corresponding functions such as
\mu^{\lambda}
and
F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}),
and the corresponding maximal operator will be designated by means an extra lower index
j,k,
such as

\mu^{{\lambda}}_{j,k},F_{j,k}({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}),{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k},
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\rho_{l}}_{j,k}.

Convention:
In order to defray the notation, we shall use the shorthand notation

\eta dz=\eta(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})\,dz
in these integrals, as well as in many similar situations later on, where a smooth amplitude factor
\eta
depending on integration variables (here the variable
z
) and small parameters (here
2^{-j}
and
2^{-k}
) in such a way that we have uniform estimates for derivatives of these amplitude factors. The concrete meaning of
\eta
will be allowed to be different at every instance where this symbol is used.

Thus, for instance we shall write

J^{\tau_{l}}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)=\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,2^{-j}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(2^{-j}z,s_{2})+2^{-j}s_{1}z)}\chi_{1}(z)\,\tau_{l}(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})\,\eta dz.

Similarly, we write

J^{\rho_{l}}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)=\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,2^{-j}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(2^{-j}z,s_{2})+2^{-j}s_{1}z)}\chi_{1}(z)\,\rho_{l}(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})\,\eta dz.

For the contribution by the transition domain
E_{l},
i.e., for
J^{\tau_{l}},
it is easily seen that we may assume that
J^{\tau_{l}}_{j,k}\equiv 0,
unless

(7.1)

\displaystyle j\geq ka_{l}+M/2,\qquad\text{if}\quad l=1,\dots L,

(7.2)

\displaystyle j\leq ka_{l+1}-M/2,\qquad\text{if}\quad l=0,\dots L-1.

Similarly, for the contribution by the homogeneous domain
D_{l},1\leq l\leq L,
i.e., for
J^{\rho_{l}},
we may assume that
J^{\rho_{l}}_{j,k}\equiv 0,
unless

(7.3)

|j-ka_{l}|\leq M/2.

We thus see that the factors
\tau_{l}(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
respectively
\rho_{l}(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
can be absorbed into a modified factor
\eta
in the definitions of
J^{\tau_{l}}_{j,k},
respectively
J^{\rho_{l}}_{j,k},
so that, with a slight abuse of notation, we may and shall henceforth assume that

\displaystyle J^{\tau_{l}}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J^{\rho_{l}}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)=J_{j,k}({\lambda},s)=\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,2^{-j}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(2^{-j}z,s_{2})+2^{-j}s_{1}z)}\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dz

for the range of
j
s and
k
s described by (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), respectively (
7.3
).

7.2.
Resolution of singularity on the transition domain
E_{l}

It is well-known that on
E_{l},
we may write

(7.4)

\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})=V(z_{1},s_{2})\,s_{2}^{A_{l}}z_{1}^{B_{l}},\qquad(z_{1},s_{2})\in E_{l},

where the function
V
is analytic away from the coordinate axes and
|V(z_{1},s_{2})|\sim 1
(compare
[
PS97
]
). Actually, we shall need more precise information on the function
V:
by (
6.9
), we may write

\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})=U_{0}(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{L}\prod_{r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right]}(z_{1}-r(s_{2})).

Now, if
l^{\prime}\geq l+1,
then
a_{l^{\prime}}\geq a_{l+1},
and since
a_{l^{\prime}}
is the leading exponent of any root
r
in the cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right],

we may factor

z_{1}-r(s_{2})=z_{1}\big(1-\frac{r(s_{2})}{z_{1}}\big),\quad\text{where}\ \frac{r(s_{2})}{z_{1}}=O(2^{-M/4}).

Similarly, if
l^{\prime}\leq l,
then we may factor

z_{1}-r(s_{2})=s_{2}^{a_{l^{\prime}}}\big(-\frac{r(s_{2})}{s_{2}^{a_{l^{\prime}}}}+\frac{z_{1}}{s_{2}^{a_{l^{\prime}}}}\big),\quad\text{where}\ \frac{r(s_{2})}{s_{2}^{a_{l^{\prime}}}}=c^{\alpha}_{l^{\prime}}\neq 0,\text{ and }\frac{z_{1}}{s_{2}^{a_{l^{\prime}}}}=O(2^{-M/4}).

In combination with (
6.10
), this implies (
7.4
), with

V(z_{1},s_{2}):=U_{0}(z_{1},s_{2})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{l}\prod_{r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right]}\big(-\frac{r(s_{2})}{s_{2}^{a_{l^{\prime}}}}+\frac{z_{1}}{s_{2}^{a_{l^{\prime}}}}\big)\prod_{l^{\prime}=l+1}^{L}\prod_{r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right]}\big(1-\frac{r(s_{2})}{z_{1}}\big).

This implies in particular that, as a function of
z_{1},

V(z_{1},s_{2})
admits a convergent Laurent expansion of the form

(7.5)

V(z_{1},s_{2})=\sum\limits_{k=-B_{l}}^{\infty}z_{1}^{k}\rho_{k}(s_{2}),

where the
\rho_{k}(s_{2})
are fractionally analytic in
s_{2}.
Moreover, on
E_{l},
we may assume that
|\rho_{0}(s_{2})|\sim 1
and

\sum\limits_{0\neq k=-B_{l}}^{\infty}|z_{1}^{k}\rho_{k}(s_{2})|\ll 1.

In order derive information from (
7.4
) on the function
\Phi_{0}
in (
6.7
), the following simple lemma will be useful:

Lemma 7.1
.

Given
B\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a smooth function
V(z)
on an interval
I
not containing
0,
then there is a smooth function
U(z)
on this interval such hat

\frac{d}{dz}\big(U(z)z^{B+1}\big)=V(z)z^{B}.

Proof.

We can choose

U(z):=z^{-(B+1)}\int z^{B}V(z)\,dz,

where the integral can designate any primitive of the integrand.

Q.E.D.

Applying this to the term of order
k
in (
7.5
), with
B=B_{l}
and
B_{l}+k\geq 0,
we see that if we define

V_{1}(z_{1},s_{2}):=\sum\limits_{k=-B_{l}}^{\infty}z_{1}^{k}\frac{\rho_{k}(s_{2})}{B_{l}+k+1},

then we find that

{\partial}_{z_{1}}\big(V_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{B_{l}+1}\big)=V(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{B_{l}},

and iterating this once more, we obtain

{\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}\big(U(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{B_{l}+2}\big)=V(z_{1},s_{2})z_{1}^{B_{l}},

if we set

U(z_{1},s_{2}):=\sum\limits_{k=-B_{l}}^{\infty}z_{1}^{k}\frac{\rho_{k}(s_{2})}{(B_{l}+k+1)(B_{l}+k+2)}.

Consequently,

{\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}\big(U(z_{1},s_{2})s_{2}^{A_{l}}z_{1}^{B_{l}+2}\big)=\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})={\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2}).

Clearly,
U(z_{1},s_{2})
has similar properties as
V(z_{1},s_{2});
in particular,
|U(z_{1},s_{2})|\sim 1.

Note also that the function
U(z_{1},s_{2})s_{2}^{A_{l}}z_{1}^{B_{l}+2}
is analytic in
z_{1}
and vanishes of second order at
z_{1}=0.

Consequently, we see that

\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})=U(z_{1},s_{2})s_{2}^{A_{l}}z_{1}^{B_{l}+2}+z_{1}g(s_{2})+h(s_{2}),

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}h(s_{2})&=\breve{\phi}_{1}(0,s_{2})\equiv 0,\\
g(s_{2})&={\partial}_{z_{1}}\breve{\phi}_{1}(0,s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}q(0,s_{2})=:s_{2}^{2}q_{0}(s_{2}).\end{split}

Thus, we find that, on
E_{l},

(7.6)

\Phi_{0}(z_{1},s^{\prime}):=\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}z_{1}=U(z_{1},s_{2})s_{2}^{A_{l}}z_{1}^{B_{l}+2}+z_{1}(s_{1}+s^{2}q_{0}(s_{2})).

Let us finally again apply our changes of coordinates
z_{1}=2^{-j}z,\ s_{2}=2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}.
Then, if we put
A:=A_{l},B:=B_{l}
and

d_{j,k}:=2^{-kA-j(B+2)},

assuming that (
7.1
), (
7.2
) hold true, we have that

(7.7)

\Phi_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2}):=\Phi_{0}(z_{1},s^{\prime})=U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2})\,d_{j,k}{\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+2^{-j}z\big(s_{1}+2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}g^{s}({\sigma}_{2})\big),

with
U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}):=U(2^{-j}z,2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
and
g^{s}({\sigma}_{2}):=q_{0}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}).

Note that
|U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2})|\sim 1,
whereas derivatives of
U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2})
can be assumed to be very small, and recall that

|z|\sim 1,\sigma_{2}\sim 1,|s_{1}|\ll 1.

Note also that

(7.8)

d_{j,k}\leq 2^{-2j}\ll 2^{-j}.

7.3.
L^{2}
- estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l}

We begin by observing that since
d_{j,k}\ll 2^{-j},
by choosing
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
properly we can always find a critical point
of
\Phi_{0}^{s}
in (
7.7
) w.r. to
z.

Thus, in view of Plancherel’s theorem, the best possible estimate of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
can be achieved by means of an application of van der Corput’s estimate of order
2
to

J_{j,k}({\lambda},s)=\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,2^{-j}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\Phi_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})}\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dz.

This leads to

|J_{j,k}({\lambda},s)|\lesssim 2^{-j}({\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-1/2},

if
{\lambda}d_{j,k}\geq 1.
If
{\lambda}d_{j,k}\leq 1,
the trivial estimate

|J_{j,k}({\lambda},s)|\lesssim 2^{-j}

holds true. Since, by (
6.3
),
|\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}_{j,k}}|\lesssim{\lambda}^{-1/2}|J_{j,k}({\lambda},s)|,
the usual arguments show that this implies the following
L^{2}
- estimate:

(7.9)

\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{j,k}\|_{2\to 2}\lesssim 2^{-j}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark 7.2
.

Throughout Section
7
, we shall assume that
{\lambda}2^{-j}\geq 1,
i.e.,
j\lesssim\log{\lambda}.
The case where
{\lambda}2^{-j}\geq 1
will be discussed in Subsection
8.7
of Section
8
.

7.4.
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l},l=1,\dots,L,
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

In view of Lemma
5.4
, the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates require pointwise bounds for
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}.
By (
6.5
), we have

\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})={\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-k}\int\int F_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})d{\sigma}_{2}\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3},

where

\displaystyle F_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-j}\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\Phi_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1})}\chi_{0}(s_{1})\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dzds_{1}.

Let us put
Y_{1}:=2^{j}y_{1},
and use (
7.6
) to write the phase function in the latter integral as

(7.10)

\Phi_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}=2^{-j}\big[U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2})\,2^{j}d_{j,k}{\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+z2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}g^{s}({\sigma}_{2})+s_{1}(z-Y_{1})\big]

Let us first consider the contribution by the region where
|Y_{1}|\gg 1
and
|Y_{1}|\geq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{j},
where we assume that
0<{\delta}\ll 1.
Here,
{\lambda}2^{-j}|Y_{1}|\gg{\lambda}^{\delta},
and since
|z|\sim 1,
integrations by parts in
s_{1}
show that

F_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=O({\lambda}^{-N})
for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}},
and thus also
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})=O({\lambda}^{-N})
for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}},
which shows that this leads just to a small error term.

Consider next the contribution by the region where
1\ll|Y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{j}.
Then, by (
7.8
),

{\lambda}d_{j,k}\leq 2^{\frac{j}{1-{\delta}}}2^{-2j}\ll 1,

if we choose
{\delta}
sufficiently small. This shows that we can include the exponential factor corresponding to the term
U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2})\,d_{j,k}{\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}
of the phase
\Phi_{0}^{s}
into the amplitude and consequently ignore it. Let us then collect all summands of the complete phase in the oscillatory integral
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})
which depend on
{\sigma}_{2}:

(7.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&2^{-j}z2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}\tilde{g}({\sigma}_{2}))+2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}\\
&=2^{-2k}\big[{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-{\sigma}_{2}Y_{2}+2^{-j}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}z\tilde{g}({\sigma}_{2})\big],\end{split}

where we have set
Y_{2}:=2^{k}y_{2}.

Let us now decompose the region where
1\ll|Y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{j}
into the regions

\Delta_{\iota_{1}}:=\{y:|Y_{1}|\sim 2^{\iota_{1}}\},

where clearly we may assume that
1\ll\iota_{1}<j.
We further decompose
\Delta_{\iota_{1}}
into the subregions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\sim 2^{\iota_{1}},|Y_{2}|\sim 2^{\iota_{2}}\},\quad 1\ll\iota_{2}\leq k,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{1},0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\sim 2^{\iota_{1}},|Y_{2}|\lesssim 1\}.

Then

|\Delta_{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}|\lesssim 2^{\iota_{1}+\iota_{2}-j-k}.

The corresponding measures which are given by restricting
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}
to the corresponding sets and the associated maximal operators will be denoted
\mu_{k,j,\iota}^{\lambda}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}.

Now, clearly, if
y\in\Delta_{\iota_{1}},
then we may integrate by parts in
s_{1}.

Moreover, if
\iota_{2}\gg 1,
and if
{\lambda}2^{-2k+\iota_{2}}\geq 1,
then (
7.11
) shows that we can also integrate by parts in
{\sigma}_{2},
and altogether this leads to the following estimate for
\mu_{k,j,\iota}^{\lambda}:

|\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-j}2^{\iota_{1}})^{-1}({\lambda}2^{-2k}2^{\iota_{2}})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{k-\iota_{1}-\iota_{2}}.

Thus, Lemma
5.4
shows that, for every
{\varepsilon}>0,

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{-j}.

But, recall that we are assuming that
j\lesssim\log{\lambda},
and
note that
{\lambda}2^{-2k+\iota_{2}}\geq 1
implies that
{\lambda}2^{-k}\geq 1,
hence
\iota_{2}\leq k\lesssim\log{\lambda},

so that by summing over these
\iota
s
we will just pick up an extra factor
(\log{\lambda})^{2}
compared to previous estimate:

(7.12)

\sum\limits_{\iota_{1}\lesssim j,\iota_{2}\leq k}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j},

for every
{\varepsilon}>0.

Assume next that
\iota_{2}\gg 1
and
{\lambda}2^{-2k+\iota_{2}}<1.
Then
1\ll 2^{\iota_{2}}<2^{2k}{\lambda}^{-1},
and so we cannot gain anything from the integration in
{\sigma}_{2}
and arrive at the following estimate:

|\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-j}2^{\iota_{1}})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k-\iota_{1}},

hence
\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-j+\iota_{2}-2k}.

Thus, summing over the corresponding
\iota
s, we get

\sum\limits_{\iota_{1}\lesssim j,\,2^{\iota_{2}}<2^{2k}{\lambda}^{-1}}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\sum\limits_{\iota_{1}\lesssim j,\,2^{\iota_{2}}<2^{2k}{\lambda}^{-1}}{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-j+\iota_{2}-2k}<{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j},

which matches with (
7.12
).

Assume finally that
\iota_{2}=0.
Then, in place of an integration by parts in
{\sigma}_{2},
we apply van der Corput’s estimate of order 2 in
{\sigma}_{2},
which leads to

|\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-j}2^{\iota_{1}})^{-1}({\lambda}2^{-2k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}={\lambda}2^{-\iota_{1}},

hence

(7.13)

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,(\iota_{1},0)}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}.

Again, we can sum in
\iota_{1}\lesssim j,
and combining all our estimates we see that the contribution
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\gg 1}^{\lambda}

of the region where
|Y_{1}|\gg 1
to
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
can be estimated by

(7.14)

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\gg 1}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j}+{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k},

for every
{\varepsilon}>0.

What remains is the contribution
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}
of the region where
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1
to
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}.

A look at the phase (
7.10
) shows that we are now in the position to apply Lemma
5.2
to the oscillatory integral
F_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})
in the variables
(s_{1},z)
in place of
(\eta,x),
with
N:={\lambda}2^{-j}\geq 1.

Moreover, observe that
s_{1}
and
z
result from our original coordinates
s_{1}
and
z_{1}=x_{1}
by means of a smooth change of coordinates. Passing back to our original coordinate, according to Remarks
5.3
we therefore must evaluate the original phase
\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1}-s_{1}y_{1}
in (
6.6
) at
x_{1}=y_{1},
and thus get

(7.15)

F_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1}({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=({\lambda}2^{-j})^{-1}2^{-j}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},s_{2})}\eta(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}).

This implies that

\mu_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})={\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3}\big)}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,\eta d{\sigma}_{2}ds_{3}.

By (
6.2
), the complete phase is here given by

2^{-2k}\big[{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+y_{1}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}q(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{k}{\sigma}_{2}(y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1}))\big]+y_{1}^{n}\beta(y_{1})-y_{3}.

In order to estimate the result of the integration in
{\sigma}_{2},
we can argue now in a similar way as before and decompose the region where
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1
into the subregions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,2^{k}|y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})|\sim 2^{\iota_{2}}\},1\ll\iota_{2}\leq k,

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,2^{k}|y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})|\lesssim 1\}.

Then

|\Delta_{\iota_{2}}|\lesssim 2^{-j}2^{\iota_{2}-k}.

If
\iota_{2}\gg 1,
then we may integrate by parts in
{\sigma}_{2}
and obtain

|\mu_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,\iota_{2}}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}({\lambda}2^{-2k+\iota_{2}})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{k-\iota_{2}},

hence

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,\iota_{2}}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{-j}.

And, if
\iota_{2}=0,
then we apply van der Corput’s estimate of order 2 in
{\sigma}_{2},
which leads to

|\mu_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,\iota_{2}}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}({\lambda}2^{-2k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}={\lambda},

hence

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,\iota_{2}}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}2^{-k}2^{-j}.

These estimates match with (
7.14
), and thus altogether we find that, for every
{\varepsilon}>0,

(7.16)

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j}.

7.5.
L^{p}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l}
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

Interpolation between the
L^{2}
- estimate (
7.9
) and the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimate (
7.16
) leads to

(7.17)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle({\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}(2-\frac{2}{p})}2^{-j}+({\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}(2-\frac{2}{p})}2^{-j}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\,(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}\,2^{-j}

\displaystyle\quad+\,{\lambda}^{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\,(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\,2^{-j},

if
1<p<2.

•

Since
1/p-1/2>0,
we can first sum both terms over all dyadic
{\lambda}
s such that
{\lambda}d_{j,k}\leq 1,
provided we choose
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
sufficiently small.

•

If
{\lambda}d_{j,k}>1,
then the total exponent of
{\lambda}d_{j,k}
in the first summand is

\frac{2}{p}-1+\frac{1}{p}-1=\frac{3}{p}-2<0,

since we are assuming that
p>3/2.
Similarly, the total exponent of
{\lambda}d_{j,k}
in the second summand is

\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}-1=\frac{2}{p}-\frac{3}{2}<\frac{4}{3}-\frac{3}{2}<0.

Thus, again both terms can be summed over all dyadic
{\lambda}
s such that
{\lambda}d_{j,k}>1,
provided we choose
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
sufficiently small.

This easily shows that, for every
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0,

(7.18)

\sum\limits_{{\lambda}\gg 1}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}\lesssim(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}\,2^{-j}+(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}.

In order to also sum over the
j
s and
k
s associated to
E_{l},
it turns out that we shall have to make use of the condition
(
7.1
). To this end, we shall first derive a little lemma which will help to relate any edge of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
having the
vertex
(B,A):=(B_{l},A_{l})
associated to the transition domain
E_{l}
as an endpoint.

7.6.
A geometric lemma and
L^{p}
-estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau_{l}},l=1,\dots,L

Lemma 7.3
.

Given a weight
{\kappa}=({\kappa}_{1},{\kappa}_{2})
such that
{\kappa}_{1}>0,{\kappa}_{2}\geq 0,
and the associated line

L_{\kappa}:=\{(t_{1},t_{2}):{\kappa}_{1}t_{1}+{\kappa}_{2}t_{2}=1\},

assume that
(B,A)
is any point on
L_{\kappa}.
We define the number
n_{\kappa}-2
as the
t_{1}
-coordinate of the intersection of the line
L_{\kappa}
with the horizontal line
t_{2}=1
(compare Figure
4
). Then

n_{\kappa}=\frac{A-1}{a}+B+2,

where
a:={\kappa}_{1}/{\kappa}_{2}
is the modulus of the slope of the line
L_{\kappa}.

Proof.

The condition

{\kappa}_{1}B+{\kappa}_{2}A=1
is equivalent to
A/a+B=1/{\kappa}_{1},
hence to

\frac{A-1}{a}+B+2=\frac{1}{{\kappa}_{1}}-\frac{{\kappa}_{2}}{{\kappa}_{1}}+2=\frac{1-{\kappa}_{2}}{{\kappa}_{1}}+2=(n_{\kappa}-2)+2=n_{\kappa}.

Q.E.D.

Figure 4.
Meaning of
n_{\kappa}

The next lemma will allow us to show that the estimates in (
7.18
) can be summed over the relevant
j
s and
k
s. Since in Step 2 and higher we shall have to deal with functions
\Phi(z,s_{2})
which are analytic in the first variable
z,
but only fractionally analytic in the second variable, we shall state it more generally for Newton-Puiseux polyhedra.

So, assume that
\Phi
is such a function, with Newton-Puiseux polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi),
whose vertices are the points

(B_{l},A_{l}),l=0,\dots L,
where
(B_{0},A_{0})=(n-2,0),
somewhat as in Subsection
6.2
(compare Figure
2
). Note, however, that in contrast to the case of Newton polyhedra, where necessarily
A_{1}\geq 1,
we may possibly have
A_{1}<1.
Otherwise, we adapt the same notation as in Subsection
6.2
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume that
A_{1}\geq 1,
so that
A_{l}\geq 1
for every
l\geq 1.
Then

\frac{A_{l}-1}{a_{l}}+B_{l}+2\leq\frac{A_{1}-1}{a_{1}}+B_{1}+2=\frac{A_{0}-1}{a_{1}}+B_{0}+2

for
l=1,\dots,L.

The proof is geometrically evident in view of Lemma
7.3
(note that apparently the sequence
\{n_{{\kappa}^{l}}\}_{l}
is decreasing) and can easily by carried out by means of an induction over
l\geq 0.
It will therefore be omitted.

We are now in the position to show that the estimates in (
7.18
) can be summed over all relevant
j
s and
k
s associated to a given transition domain
E_{l},l=1,\dots,L,
provided
p>\max\{3/2,h\}.
Actually, we shall only need condition (
7.1
) here, i.e.,

j\geq ka_{l}+M/2.
Indeed, we shall prove that under these assumptions

(7.19)

\sum\limits_{j,k\gg 1:j\geq ka_{l}}\Big((d_{j,k}^{-1}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}+(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}\Big)<\infty,

provided we choose
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
sufficiently small.

In combination with estimate (
7.18
) this will clearly imply that for if
l=1,\dots,L,

(7.20)

\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau_{l}}\|_{p\to p}<\infty,

provided
p>\max\{3/2,h\}.

Now, from (
6.2
) and (
6.8
), we find that the first two vertices of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
are given by
(B_{0},A_{0})=(n-2,0)
and
(B_{1},A_{1})=(m-2,1),
and that
{\kappa}_{1}^{1}=1/(n-2)>0
and

a_{1}=\frac{{\kappa}_{1}^{1}}{{\kappa}^{1}_{2}}=\frac{1}{n-m}.

(compare also Figure
2
). Thus, Lemma
7.4
implies that

(7.21)

\frac{A_{l}-1}{a_{l}}+B_{l}+2\leq m

for
l=1,\dots,L.
Fix such an
l,
and put
A:=A_{l},B:=B_{l}
and
a=a_{l}>a_{1}=1/(n-m)>0.
Then

d_{j,k}^{-1}=2^{kA+j(B+2)}

and
k\leq j/a
in (
7.19
). Thus, we can re-write (
7.19
) as

(7.22)

\sum\limits_{j,k\gg 1:k\leq j/a}\Big((2^{k(A-1)+j(B+2)})^{\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}+(2^{kA+j(B+2)})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}\Big)<\infty.

Since the exponents
\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
and
\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
are strictly positive, we can first sum over all

k\leq j/a
and are left with showing that

\sum\limits_{j\gg 1}\Big(2^{j\big[(\frac{A-1}{a}+B+2)(\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-1\big]}+2^{j\big[\frac{A}{a}+B+2)(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-1\big]}\Big)<\infty.

But, by (
7.21
),

\frac{A-1}{a}+B+2\leq m\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2}{p}-1<\frac{2}{n}\leq\frac{1}{m},

since
1/p<1/h=1/2+1/n
and
n\geq 2m,
so that we may assume that the exponent of the first summand in (
7.22
) is strictly negative and we can sum in
j.

Similarly,

\frac{A}{a}+B+2\leq m+\frac{1}{a}\leq m+(n-m)=n\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{n},

so that we may assume that the exponent of the second summand in (
7.22
) is strictly negative too and we can again sum in
j.

This completes the proof of (
7.19
).

7.7.
L^{p}
-estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau_{0}}

Let us finally look at the case
l=0.
Recall that here only the condition (
7.2
), i.e.,
j\leq ka_{1}-M/2,
is available, and that now

d_{j,k}^{-1}=2^{kA_{0}+j(B_{0}+2)}=2^{jn}.

Thus, here we would like to show that

(7.23)

\sum\limits_{j,k\gg 1:k\geq j/a_{1}}\Big((2^{jn-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}+(2^{jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}\Big)<\infty.

In the first summand, we can first sum over all
k\geq j/a_{1}
and arrive at the sum

\sum\limits_{j\gg 1}2^{j\big[(n-\frac{1}{a_{1}})(\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-1\big]}.

But,

n-\frac{1}{a_{1}}=m,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2}{p}-1<\frac{2}{n}\leq\frac{1}{m},

so that we may assume that the exponent of the first summand in (
7.23
) is strictly negative by choosing
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
sufficiently small, and thus we can sum in
j.

The second summand is insufficient for a summation in
k,
unless, say,
k\lesssim j.
In the latter case, we can sum in
k\lesssim j
and arrive at the sum

(7.24)

\sum\limits_{j\gg 1}2^{jn(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\,2^{-j},

with any slightly bigger
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
than before. This series is convergent for
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
sufficiently small, since we are assuming that
p>h.

So, let us assume henceforth that, say,
k>jn.

The crucial observation is that the second term in (
7.16
) can be improved when
{\lambda}2^{-k}\leq 1,
and this will be needed for the case
l=0.

To be more precise, assume first that
{\lambda}2^{-k}>1,
i.e.,
k\lesssim\log{\lambda}.
Then we can keep the second term in (
7.17
), which is here given by

{\lambda}^{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}2^{-jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\lambda}2^{-jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\,(2^{jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\,2^{-j}.

Summing now first in
k\lesssim\log{\lambda},
we can estimate by the same kind of expression, but with a slightly bigger exponent
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0.

After summing in
{\lambda},
we are again left with the series in (
7.24
) and are done.

Assume next that
{\lambda}2^{-k}\leq 1.
Then the exponential factor of the oscillatory integral
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+\Gamma)
which collects all terms depending only on
{\sigma}_{2}
(compare (
7.11
)) is essentially non-oscillatory, and so we shall rather estimate for
y\in\Delta_{\iota_{1}}
by

|\mu_{j,k,\iota_{1}}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-j}2^{\iota_{1}})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k-\iota_{1}}.

Since
|\Delta_{\iota_{1}}|\lesssim 2^{\iota_{1}-j},
we obtain the improved estimate

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota_{1}}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-j-k}.

This shows that we can replace the second term in (
7.16
) by
{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k},
and finally, after interpolation with our
L^{2}
-estimate (
7.9
), we are led to summing

M({\lambda},j,k):=({\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}(1+{\lambda}2^{-jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}2^{-j}

over our
{\lambda}
s,
j
s and
k
s.

Assume first that
2^{k}\geq{\lambda}>2^{jn}.
Then

M({\lambda},j,k)\sim({\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}({\lambda}2^{-jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}2^{-j},

hence

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k:2^{k}\geq{\lambda}}M({\lambda},j,k)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}2^{-jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}2^{-j}=({\lambda}2^{-jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}2^{jn(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}2^{-j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\lambda}2^{-jn})^{\frac{2}{p}-\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}2^{j\big[n(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-1\big]}.

But, since we are assuming that
p>\max\{3/2,h\},
we have
2/p-3/2=-1/6<0,
and again
n(1/p-1/2)-1<0,

so that we can first sum in
{\lambda}>2^{jn},
and then in
j\gg 1,
provided
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
is sufficiently small.

We are thus left with the case where
{\lambda}\leq 2^{jn}
and
k>jn.
Then

M({\lambda},j,k)\sim({\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}2^{-j},

and thus

\sum\limits_{{\lambda}:{\lambda}\leq 2^{jn}}M({\lambda},j,k)\lesssim(2^{jn(\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon})}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}2^{-j}.

This implies

\sum\limits_{k:k\geq jn}\sum\limits_{{\lambda}:{\lambda}\leq 2^{jn}}M({\lambda},j,k)\lesssim(2^{jn(\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon})})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}2^{-j}.

and again we can finally also sum in
j,
since
p>h.

Consequently, estimate (
7.20
) holds true even for
l=0.

7.8.
On the contributions by the homogeneous domains
D_{l},l=1,\dots,L

Let us fix
l_{1}\in\{1,\dots,L\}.
We recall that

D_{l_{1}}:=\{(z_{1},s_{2}):2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\leq|z_{1}|\leq 2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\},

where we may assume that
\rho_{l_{1}}
is supported in
D_{l_{1}}.
We also recall from Subsection
7.1
that we may assume that

J^{\rho_{l_{1}}}({\lambda},s)=\sum\limits_{j,k:|j-ka_{l_{1}}|\leq M/2}J_{j,k}({\lambda},s).

We shall decompose
D_{l_{1}}
into a finite number of very narrow subregions. To this end, fix any point
c
in the compact interval
I:=[2^{-M},2^{M}],
and put

D_{l_{1}}^{c}:=\{(z_{1},s_{2}):|z_{1}-cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\},

where
{\varepsilon}>0
is supposed to be sufficiently small. More precisely, recall from Subsection
6.2
that every root
r(s_{2})
in the cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]

associated to the compact edge
{\gamma}_{l_{1}}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
admits a Puiseux series expansion

r(s_{2})=c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}+c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}+\cdots+c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}}+\cdots,

with leading term
c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}},
where the leading coefficient
c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}
may be real, or complex.
By having chosen
M
to be sufficiently large, we are of course assuming that all real coefficients
c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}
will be contained in the interval
I.
Denote by
\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}
the set of those
real
coefficients
c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}.

If
c\notin\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
we choose
{\varepsilon}>0
at least so small that
{\varepsilon}\ll\text{\rm dist\,}(c,\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}).

Figure 5.
Comparison between
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
and
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi})

Now, for any
c\in I,
let us introduce the new variable
\tilde{z}:=z_{1}-cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}},
and accordingly define

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2}):=\Phi(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}},s_{2}).

Note that
\tilde{\Phi}
is still analytic in
\tilde{z},
but may be only fractionally analytic in
s_{2}.
The region
D_{l_{1}}^{c}
then corresponds to

\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}:=\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):|\tilde{z}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\}.

In Step 2 of our resolution algorithm, which will be discussed in the next section, we shall base our analysis on the Newton polyhedron of
\tilde{\Phi}
in place of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi).
The corresponding vertices and compact edges of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi})
will be denoted by
(\tilde{B}_{l},\tilde{A}_{l}),l=0,\dots,\tilde{L}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{l}:=(\tilde{B}_{l},\tilde{A}_{l}),(\tilde{B}_{l-1},\tilde{A}_{l-1})],l=1,\dots,\tilde{L}.

The line supporting the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{l}
will be denoted by
\tilde{L}_{l},
the associated weight by

\tilde{\kappa}^{l},
and the corresponding modulus of slope by
\tilde{a}_{l}=\tilde{\kappa}_{1}^{l}/\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{l}.
The corresponding homogeneous domains and transition domains into which we shall decompose the upper half-plane where
\tilde{z}\in{\mathbb{R}},s_{2}>0,
will be denoted by
\tilde{D}_{l},l=1,\dots,\tilde{L},
and
\tilde{E}_{l},l=0,\dots,\tilde{L}.

Coming back to
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c},
note that by (
6.9
),

(7.25)

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=\tilde{U}_{0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}})^{\nu_{1}}\prod_{l=1}^{L}\Phi\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right](\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}},s_{2}),

where

\Phi\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right](\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}},s_{2})=\prod_{r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}-r(s_{2}))=\prod_{r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{r}(s_{2})),

if we define adjusted roots
\tilde{r}
by

\tilde{r}(s_{2}):=r(s_{2})-cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}.
