If now
l<l_{1}
and

r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right],

then
a_{l}<a_{l_{1}},
so the roots
\tilde{r}(s_{2})
and
r(s_{2})
do have the same leading term and the same multiplicity.

Thus the identities (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) show that we indeed have

(\tilde{B}_{l},\tilde{A}_{l})=(B_{l},A_{l}),\quad 0\leq l<l_{1}.

In particular,

\tilde{\gamma}_{l}={\gamma}_{l}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{a}_{l}=a_{l},\ l<l_{1}

(compare Figure
5
).

Case 1:

c\notin\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}.
Then, if
l=l_{1}
and

r\in\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right],

we can factor

\tilde{z}-\tilde{r}(s_{2})=s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\Big(c-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}+\frac{\tilde{z}}{s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}}\Big)+\text{higher degree terms},

where
|{\tilde{z}}/{s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}}|<{\varepsilon}.
Moreover, if
c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}
is real, then
|c-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}|\gg{\varepsilon},
and otherwise

|c-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}|\geq|\text{\rm Im\,}c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}|>0,
so that, by choosing
{\varepsilon}
small enough, we can assume that in both cases

|c-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}|\gg{\varepsilon}.
Thus we may assume that

|c-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}+{\tilde{z}}/{s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}}|\sim 1.

Arguing in a similar way also for
l\neq l_{1}
(compare our discussion in Subsection
7.2
), we find that the edge

\tilde{\gamma}_{l_{1}}
has the same slope and right endpoint as
{\gamma}_{l_{1}},
but as left endpoint a point of the form

(0,\tilde{A}_{l_{1}}),
and that we can factor

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=V(\tilde{z},s_{2})\,s_{2}^{\tilde{A}_{l_{1}}},\qquad(\tilde{z},s_{2})\in\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c},

where the function
V(\tilde{z},s_{2})
behaves in a similar way is in Subsection
7.2
. In particular, here
\tilde{L}=l_{1}.

Remark 7.5
.

This shows that, by changing from
z_{1}
to
\tilde{z},
we can regard the region
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}
now like a transition domain
\tilde{E}_{\tilde{L}},
in the coordinates
(\tilde{z},s_{2}).
With view to Lemma
7.4
, we also remark that here
\tilde{A}_{l_{1}}\geq A_{l_{1}}\geq A_{1}=1.

These kind of domains will be handled in Step 2 of our resolution algorithm.

Case 2:

c\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}.

Then
c
is of the form
c=c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}\in{\mathbb{R}}.
This will fix
{\alpha}_{1}
in the sequel. We then recall from (
6.12
) that, up to a constant factor, the
{\kappa}^{l_{1}}
-principal part of
\Phi
is given by

\Phi_{{\kappa}^{l_{1}}}(z_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{A_{l_{1}-1}}z_{1}^{B_{l_{1}}}\prod_{\alpha}\Big(z_{1}-c^{\alpha}_{l_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\Big)^{N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]}.

Consequently, the
{\kappa}^{l_{1}}
-principal part of
\tilde{\Phi}
is given by

\tilde{\Phi}_{{\kappa}^{l_{1}}}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=s_{2}^{A_{l_{1}-1}}(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}})^{B_{l_{1}}}\prod_{\alpha}\Big(\tilde{z}-(c^{\alpha}_{l_{1}}-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}})\ s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\Big)^{N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]}.

We must therefore distinguish two sub-cases:

Subcase (i):

B_{l_{1}}>0,
or

\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]\supsetneqq\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right].

Then
\tilde{\Phi}_{{\kappa}^{l_{1}}}
is not a monomial, and thus the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{l_{1}}
lies on the same line as
{\gamma}_{l_{1}}.
It thus will have the same slope
\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}=a_{l_{1}}
and right endpoint
(B_{l_{1}-1},A_{l_{1}-1})
as
{\gamma}_{l_{1}},
but the left endpoints of these two edges may be different.

Here

\tilde{E}_{l_{1}}=\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l_{1}+1}}<|\tilde{z}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\}.

Thus, if we choose
{\varepsilon}<2^{-M},
and decompose

\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}=\tilde{D}_{l_{1},1}^{c}\cup\tilde{D}_{l_{1},2}^{c},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{l_{1},1}^{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l_{1}+1}}\leq|\tilde{z}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\},

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{l_{1},2}^{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):|\tilde{z}|<2^{M}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}+1}}\},

then
\tilde{D}_{l_{1},1}^{c}\subset\tilde{E}_{l_{1}}.
Note that for
l_{1}=\tilde{L},
we even have
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}\subset\tilde{E}_{l_{1}}.

Moreover, clearly
\tilde{D}_{l_{1},2}^{c}
is contained in the union of the
\tilde{E}_{l}
s and
\tilde{D}_{l}
s with
l\geq l_{1}+1,
and therefore

\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}\subset\bigcup\limits_{l=l_{1}}^{\tilde{L}}\tilde{E}_{l}\,\cup\bigcup\limits_{l=l_{1}+1}^{\tilde{L}}\tilde{D}_{l}.

Remarks 7.6
.

(a) We thus see that in Subcase (i) the contribution by
D_{l_{1}}^{c}
to our maximal operator can be covered by the contributions by the

\tilde{E}_{l}
s with
l\geq l_{1}
and the
\tilde{D}_{l}
s with
l\geq l_{1}+1.
Those by the
\tilde{E}_{l}
s will be completely covered by Step 2 of our resolution algorithm in the next section, and to the
\tilde{D}_{l}
s we shall apply a similar kind of reasoning as in the present section, which will lead to Step 3 of our resolution algorithm, and so forth. Note also that
l_{1}+1\geq 2,
so that the first edge with right endpoint
(n-2,0)
will be the same for all steps
\geq 2,
namely
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}.
Thus, if
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1,
then we can apply Lemma
7.4
in Step 2, and as well in any higher step.

(b) Note also that if
\tilde{a}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}+1
is the leading exponent of a root
\tilde{r}(s_{2})
of
\tilde{\Phi},
then
\tilde{a}_{l}
is of the form
a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}},
and the leading term of
\tilde{r}(s_{2})
is of the form

c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}.

Subcase (ii):

B_{l_{1}}=0
and

\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right],

i.e., all roots in
\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]
have the same leading term
cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}.
Then

\tilde{\Phi}_{{\kappa}^{l_{1}}}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=s_{2}^{A_{l_{1}-1}}\tilde{z}^{N\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]}=s_{2}^{A_{l_{1}-1}}\tilde{z}^{B_{l_{1}-1}}

is a monomial, so that the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{l_{1}}
has “shrunk” to the vertex
(B_{l_{1}-1},A_{l_{1}-1})
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi})
by the change of coordinates from
z_{1}
to
\tilde{z}.
Here, the edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{l_{1}}
will thus have a slope
\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}>a_{l_{1}}.

Here

\tilde{E}_{l_{1}-1}=\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}}<|\tilde{z}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}-1}}\},

since
\tilde{a}_{l_{1}-1}=a_{l_{1}-1}.
And, we claim that we may assume that

2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}}<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\leq 2^{-M}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}-1}}.

Indeed, the first inequality is equivalent to
s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}-a_{l_{1}}}<{\varepsilon}2^{-M}.
But,
\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}-a_{l_{1}}>0,
so that the latter inequality can be assumed to be true because we may assume that
s_{2}\ll 1
is sufficiently small. The second inequality follows in a similar way. Let us here decompose

\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}=\tilde{D}_{l_{1},1}^{c}\cup\tilde{D}_{l_{1},2}^{c},

where now

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{l_{1},1}^{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}}\leq|\tilde{z}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\},

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{l_{1},2}^{c}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):|\tilde{z}|<2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}}\}.

Then
\tilde{D}_{l_{1},1}^{c}\subset\tilde{E}_{l_{1}-1},
in a similar way as in the previous subcase we see that here

(7.26)

\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}\subset\bigcup\limits_{l=l_{1}-1}^{\tilde{L}}\tilde{E}_{l}\,\cup\bigcup\limits_{l=l_{1}}^{\tilde{L}}\tilde{D}_{l}.

Remarks 7.7
.

(a) If
l_{1}=1,
then for our phase
\Phi
in (
6.8
), by (
6.2
) we have

(7.27)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\kappa}^{1}&=\big(\frac{1}{n-2},\frac{n-m}{n-2}\big),\quad a_{1}=\frac{1}{n-m},\\
\Phi_{{\kappa}^{1}}(z_{1},s_{2})&=z_{1}^{n-2}n(n-1)\beta(0)+s_{2}z_{1}^{m-2}m(m-1)\omega(0).\end{split}

Note that here
B_{l_{1}}=m-2.
Moreover, since we are assuming that
n\geq 2m,
we have
n-m\geq m\geq 2,
so that the polynomial
z_{1}^{n-m}n(n-1)\beta(0)+s_{2}m(m-1)\omega(0)
has at least 2 different (real or complex) roots, each of multiplicity 1, hence

\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
1\end{matrix}\right]\supsetneqq\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
1\end{matrix}\right].

This shows that here only Subcase (i) can arise.

In particular, in Step 2, Subcase (ii) can only arise when
l_{1}\geq 2,
and the same applies then to all higher steps, so that
l_{1}-1\geq 1
in (
7.26
) – thus no transition domains
\tilde{E}_{0}
will appear in (
7.26
).

(b) By (
7.26
) we see that in Subcase (ii) the contribution by
D_{l_{1}}^{c}
to our maximal operator can be covered by the contributions by the

\tilde{E}_{l}
s with
l\geq l_{1}-1\geq 1
and the
\tilde{D}_{l}
s with
l\geq l_{1}\geq 2.
Those by the
\tilde{E}_{l}
s will be completely covered by Step 2 of our resolution algorithm in the next section, and to the
\tilde{D}_{l}
s we shall apply a similar kind of reasoning as in the present section, which will lead to Step 3 of our resolution algorithm, and so forth. Note again that since here
l_{1}\geq 2,
the first edge with right endpoint
(n-2,0)
will be the same for all steps
\geq 2,
namely
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}.
Thus, if
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1,
then we can apply Lemma
7.4
in Step 2, and as well in any higher step.

(c) Note also that if
\tilde{a}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}
is the leading exponent of a root
\tilde{r}(s_{2})
of
\tilde{\Phi},
then
\tilde{a}_{l}
is of the form
a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}},
and the leading term of
\tilde{r}(s_{2})
is of the form

c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}.

We finally remark that, by compactness of the interval
I,
we can cover the homogeneous domain
D_{l_{1}}
by the sub-domains
D_{l_{1}}^{c},c\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
and a finite number of subdomains
D_{l_{1}}^{c^{j}},
with
c^{j}\notin\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}.

Thus we may reduce our studies to considering such sub-domains
D_{l_{1}}^{c},
for which we shall then, as explained before, pass to the coordinate
\tilde{z}
in place of
z_{1}.
The study of the contributions of the corresponding homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}
will be carried out in Step 2 (and higher) of our algorithm.

7.9.
Stopping domains
D^{c}_{l_{1}},c\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
for the resolution algorithm at Step 1

We shall stop on the domain
D^{c}_{l_{1}},c=c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
when the multiplicity

N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]

of the cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]

agrees with the multiplicity of every sub-cluster of it.

Observe that this is equivalent to the condition that the cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]

contains only one, real root
r
of multiplicity

N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right].

The fact that this root must be real stems from the observation that with any root in

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]
,
its complex conjugate must also be in the same cluster, since
\Phi
is real.

The contribution by such a domain
D^{c}_{l_{1}}
will be covered in a complete way in Subsection
8.8
of the next section.

Remark 7.8

(Stopping when
l_{1}=1
)
.

If
l_{1}=1,
we have seen in Remark
7.7
(a) that any real root in

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
1\end{matrix}\right]

has multiplicity
1,
so that by (
6.12
) we do have

N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
1\end{matrix}\right]=1.

This shows that for
l_{1}=1
we stop our resolution algorithm in Step 1.

8.
Analysis in Step 2 of the resolution algorithm

In this section, we shall proceed in a similar way as in the previous Section
7
, but our analysis will now be based on the coordinates

\tilde{z}
and the function
\tilde{\Phi}
in place of
z_{1}
and the function
\Phi.
Adapting the notation from the previous subsection, recall that

z_{1}=\tilde{z}+w(s_{2}),

where here
w(s_{2})=cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}.
The non-trivial extra term
w(s_{2}),
however, will create additional challenges compared to the analysis in the previous section.

Of course, we shall have to apply the same change of coordinates also to our original phase functions
\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})
and
\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime}):=\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1}
in (
6.7
), and therefore put

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{0,0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\breve{\phi}_{1}(\tilde{z}+w(s_{2}),s_{2}),

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{0}(\tilde{z},s^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(\tilde{z}+w(s_{2}),s_{2})=\tilde{\Phi}_{0,0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})+s_{1}\tilde{z}+s_{1}w(s_{2}).

Note that then

(8.1)

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2}):={\partial}_{\tilde{z}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{0}(\tilde{z},s^{\prime})={\partial}_{\tilde{z}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{0,0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})

(compare with (
6.8
)).

A crucial tool will again be Lemma
7.4
. Its application will here require that
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1,
so let us begin by discussing this condition.

8.1.
On the condition
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1

By Remark
7.5
, we see that
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1
in Case 1, i.e., when
c\notin\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}.
Assume next that we are in Case 2 where
c=c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}.
Then, if
l_{1}\geq 2,
we have that
\tilde{A}_{1}=A_{1}\geq 1.

But, in Remark
7.8
we have seen that for
l_{1}=1
we can stop our resolution algorithm already at Step 1, so that henceforth we may and shall assume that
l_{1}\geq 2,
hence
\tilde{A}_{1}=A_{1}\geq 1.

For the sake of completeness, let us finally observe that when
l_{1}=1,
then by Remark
7.7
(a) we must be in Subcase (i), and by (
7.27
) we have

\Phi_{{\kappa}^{1}}(z_{1},s_{2})=C_{1}z_{1}^{m-2}\big(z_{1}^{n-m}+C_{2}s_{2}\big)=C_{1}z_{1}^{m-2}\prod_{\alpha}\Big(z_{1}-c^{\alpha}_{l_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{1}}\Big),

with nontrivial real constants
C_{1},C_{2}.
This implies that

\tilde{\Phi}_{{\kappa}^{1}}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=C_{1}\tilde{z}\,(\tilde{z}+c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{1}})^{m-2}\prod_{{\alpha}\neq{\alpha}_{1}}\Big(\tilde{z}-(c^{\alpha}_{l_{1}}-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}})\ s_{2}^{a_{1}}\Big),

where the product over the
{\alpha}
s such that
{\alpha}\neq{\alpha}_{1}
will consist of
n-m-1\geq 1
different factors, since all non-trivial roots of

\Phi_{{\kappa}^{1}}
have multiplicity 1.
By (
6.10
) this implies that

\tilde{A}_{1}=(n-3)a_{1}=(n-3)/(n-m),
and
\tilde{B}_{1}=1,
so that
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}=[(1,(n-3)/(n-m)),(n-2,0)].

Thus,
\tilde{A}_{1}<1
if and only if
m=2.

Remarks 8.1
.

(a) Notice that by Remark
7.8
and our discussion in Subsection
8.8
we see that we shall have dealt in a complete way with the contribution by the homogeneous domain
D_{1}
and therefore will be allowed to assume that
l_{1}\geq 2
in Step 3 and higher.

(b) We shall see later (see Section
9
) that our resolution algorithm will stop whenever we have arrived at a narrow subdomain containing only one sub-cluster of roots consisting of just one root (possibly with multiplicity) whose leading jet
w(s_{2})
is real, i.e., when no further “fine splitting” of roots can occur. In such a case
we shall be able to argue in a very similar way as we are doing here.

Recall also that since the edges
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
and
{\gamma}_{1}
are lying on the same line
L_{1},
we have
\tilde{a}_{1}=a_{1},
and

(8.2)

\frac{\tilde{A}_{1}-1}{\tilde{a}_{1}}+\tilde{B}_{1}+2=m.

For later use, note that this identity remains valid even when
\tilde{A}_{1}<1.
And, if
m\geq 3,
so that
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1,
then by Lemma
7.4
we also have

(8.3)

\frac{\tilde{A}_{l}-1}{\tilde{a}_{l}}+\tilde{B}_{l}+2\leq m,\quad l=1,\dots,\tilde{L}.

Clearly, this holds true also for
l_{1}\geq 2.

8.2.
Domain decomposition and dyadic localization in
\tilde{z}
and
s_{2}

After having fixed
l_{1}\in\{1,\dots,L\},
according to our discussion in the previous section we can cover the domain
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}
by
\tilde{\kappa}^{l}
- homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l}
of the form

\tilde{D}_{l}:=\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}\leq|\tilde{z}|\leq 2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}\},\quad l\leq\tilde{L},

and transition domains of the form

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{l}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l+1}}<|\tilde{z}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}\},\quad l\leq\tilde{L}-1,

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\tilde{L}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):|\tilde{z}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{\tilde{L}}}\}.

More precisely, in Case 1 where
c\notin\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
according to Remark
7.5
we just need the transition domain
\tilde{E}_{\tilde{L}},
where
\tilde{L}=l_{1}.

In Case 2, where
c\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
we need to distinguish two subcases: if we are in Subcase (i), then
according to Remark
7.6
(a) we shall need the transition domains
\tilde{E}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}\geq 1
and the homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}+1\geq 2.
Moreover, according to Remark
8.1
(a), we shall treat the case
l_{1}=1

separately (in Subsection
8.8
), so that we shall here even assume that
l_{1}\geq 2.

In Subcase (ii) of Case 2, according to Remark
7.7
we may assume that
l_{1}\geq 2
and shall then need the transition domains
\tilde{E}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}-1\geq 1
and the homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}\geq 2.

Moreover, our discussion in Subsection
8.1
shows that under these assumptions we always have that
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1.

Recall also that in all cases that had appeared we had seen that the edges
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
and
{\gamma}_{1}
were supported on the same line
\tilde{L}_{1}=L_{1}
of “slope”
\tilde{a}_{1}=a_{1},
so that the identity (
8.2
) and the inequalities (
8.3
) will hold true.

Having fixed
l_{1}
and
l
according to these assumptions, we shall put

a:=a_{l_{1}},\quad\tilde{a}:=\tilde{a}_{l}.

Note that if we are in Case 1, or Subcase (i) of Case 2, then
\tilde{a}\geq\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}=a,
but in Subcase (ii) it may also happen that
\tilde{a}<a
(for instance if
l=l_{1}-1,
where
\tilde{a}=a_{l_{1}-1}<a_{l_{1}}=a
).

Note also that, in analogy to (
8.3
), by Lemma
7.4
we also have

(8.4)

\frac{\tilde{A}_{l}-1}{\tilde{a}_{l}}+\tilde{B}_{l}+2\leq m,\quad l=1,\dots,\tilde{L},

since
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1.

As in Section
7
, we shall again localize to these domains
\tilde{D}_{l}
and
\tilde{E}_{l}
in a smooth way by means of the corresponding functions
\tilde{\rho}_{l}
respectively
\tilde{\tau}_{l}
given by

\tilde{\rho}_{l}(\tilde{z},s_{2}):=\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{\tilde{z}}{2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}}\Big)-\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{\tilde{z}}{2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}}\Big),

respectively

\tilde{\tau}_{l}(\tilde{z},s_{2}):=\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{\tilde{z}}{2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}}\Big)\,-\chi_{0}\Big(\frac{\tilde{z}}{2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l+1}}}\Big).

The corresponding functions such as
\mu^{\lambda},J({\lambda},s)
and
F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}),
will be designated by means of an extra upper index
\tilde{\rho}_{l}
respectively
\tilde{\tau}_{l},
such as

\mu^{\tilde{\rho}_{l},{\lambda}},J^{\tilde{\rho}}({\lambda},s),
or
F^{\tilde{\tau}_{l}}({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1}).

Similarly as before, let us here localize to

|\tilde{z}|\sim 2^{-j},\ s_{2}\sim 2^{-k},

where we may assume that
j,k
are sufficiently large integers
\gg 1.
To this end, we define scaled coordinates
z,{\sigma}_{2}
by writing

\tilde{z}=2^{-j}z,\ s_{2}=2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},
so that

|z|\sim 1,\ \sigma_{2}\sim 1.

Accordingly, let us here put

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\chi_{1}(2^{k}s_{2})\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\tilde{\Phi}_{0}(\tilde{z},s^{\prime})}\chi_{1}(2^{j}\tilde{z})\,\eta d\tilde{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,2^{-j}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})}\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dz,

where we have put

\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2}):=\tilde{\Phi}_{0}(2^{-j}z,s_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),

and

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle 2^{-j}\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1})}\chi_{0}(s_{1})\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dzds_{1}.

The corresponding contribution to
\mu^{\lambda}
is here given by

(8.5)

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-k}\times

\displaystyle\iint\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})d{\sigma}_{2}\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3}.

Of course, we are following here the same conventions as in Subsection
7.1
. Applying the same reasoning as in that subsection, in analogy to (
7.1
), (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) we see here that
for the contribution by the transition domain
\tilde{E}_{l},
i.e., for
J^{\tilde{\tau}_{l}},
we may assume that
J^{\tilde{\tau}_{l}}_{j,k}\equiv 0,
unless

(8.6)

j\geq k\tilde{a}_{l}+M/2,\qquad\text{if}\quad l=1,\dots\tilde{L}.

Similarly, for the contribution by the homogeneous domain
\tilde{D}_{l},2\leq l\leq\tilde{L},
i.e., for
J^{\tilde{\rho}_{l}},
we may assume that
J^{\tilde{\rho}_{l}}_{j,k}\equiv 0,
unless

(8.7)

|j-k\tilde{a}_{l}|\leq M/2.

For the range of
j
s and
k
s described by (
8.6
), respectively (
8.7
), we are then also allowed to assume for simplicity that

\displaystyle J^{\tilde{\tau}_{l}}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J^{\tilde{\rho}_{l}}_{j,k}({\lambda},s)=\tilde{J}_{j,k}({\lambda},s).

The corresponding maximal operators will be designated by means of an extra lower index
j,k,
such as

{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda},
or
{\mathcal{M}}^{\rho_{l}}_{j,k}.

8.3.
Resolution of singularity on the transition domain
\tilde{E}_{l}

Fix
\tilde{E}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1},
respectively
l\geq l_{1}-1\geq 1
if we are in the Subcase (ii) of Case 2.
Arguing as in Subsection
7.2
, we may write

(8.8)

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=\tilde{V}(\tilde{z},s_{2})\,s_{2}^{\tilde{A}_{l}}\tilde{z}^{\tilde{B}_{l}},\qquad(\tilde{z},s_{2})\in\tilde{E}_{l},

where
\tilde{V}
is analytic in
\tilde{z}
and fractional analytic in
s_{2}
away from the coordinate axes and
|\tilde{V}|\sim 1.

Moreover, by means of Lemma
7.1
we see again that there is a function
\tilde{U}(\tilde{z},s_{2})
having similar properties as
\tilde{V}(\tilde{z},s_{2})
such that

{\partial}_{\tilde{z}}^{2}\big(\tilde{U}(\tilde{z},s_{2})s_{2}^{\tilde{A}_{l}}\tilde{z}^{\tilde{B}_{l}+2}\big)=\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2})={\partial}_{\tilde{z}}^{2}\tilde{\Phi}_{0,0}(\tilde{z},s_{2}).

In particular,
|\tilde{U}|\sim 1.

Again the function
\tilde{U}(\tilde{z},s_{2})s_{2}^{\tilde{A}_{l}}\tilde{z}^{\tilde{B}_{l}+2}
is analytic in
\tilde{z}
and vanishes of second order at
\tilde{z}=0.
Consequently, we see that (compare also (
8.1
))

\tilde{\Phi}_{0,0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=\tilde{U}(\tilde{z},s_{2})s_{2}^{\tilde{A}_{l}}\tilde{z}^{\tilde{B}_{l}+2}+\tilde{z}\tilde{g}(s_{2})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}),

with fractional analytic functions
\tilde{g}(s_{2}),\tilde{h}(s_{2}).
Since
\tilde{z}=0
iff
z_{1}=w(s_{2})
(recall that

\tilde{z}=z_{1}-w(s_{2})
), these are explicitly given by

(8.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{h}(s_{2})&=\breve{\phi}_{1}(w(s_{2}),s_{2}),\\
\tilde{g}(s_{2})&={\partial}_{z_{1}}\breve{\phi}_{1}(w(s_{2}),s_{2}).\end{split}

Consequently,

\tilde{\Phi}_{0}(\tilde{z},s^{\prime})=\tilde{U}(\tilde{z},s_{2})s_{2}^{\tilde{A}_{l}}\tilde{z}^{\tilde{B}_{l}+2}+s_{1}(\tilde{z}+w(s_{2}))+\tilde{z}\tilde{g}(s_{2})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}).

Let us finally again apply our changes of coordinates
\tilde{z}=2^{-j}z,\ s_{2}=2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}.
Then, if we put
A:=\tilde{A}_{l},B:=\tilde{B}_{l}
and

d_{j,k}:=2^{-kA-j(B+2)},

assuming that (
8.6
) holds true, we obtain

\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}=d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+s_{1}(2^{-j}z+w(s_{2})-y_{1})+2^{-j}z\tilde{g}(s_{2})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}),

where
U
has similar properties as in (
7.7
).

Remark 8.2
.

Note that we have picked up in particular the new term
s_{1}w(s_{2}),
where

|w(s_{2})|\sim s_{2}^{a}\sim 2^{-ka}

(recall that
a=a_{l_{1}}
). The same will in fact apply in all subsequent steps of our resolution algorithm, where in Step
p
we will have to choose for
w(s_{2})
the leading jet

w(s_{2}):=c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}+c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}+\cdots+c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}}.

Let us then write

w(s_{2})=2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),

so that
|\tilde{w}|\sim 1,
whereas all derivatives of
\tilde{w}
can be assumed to be very small. We shall use the following abbreviations:

\displaystyle Y_{1}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{ka}y_{1},

\displaystyle\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2})-Y_{1},

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle 2^{j-ka}.

These allow us to re-write

(8.10)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+2^{-j}s_{1}\big(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z\big)+2^{-j}z\tilde{g}(s_{2})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}),

where
z\sim 1\sim{\sigma}_{2}.
We can then re-write

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=2^{-j}\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1})}\chi_{0}(s_{1})\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dzds_{1}

(8.11)

\displaystyle=2^{-j}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\tilde{h}(s_{2})}\,\int{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+2^{-j}z\tilde{g}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})\big)}\chi_{1}(z)\eta dz,

where
{\varphi}:=\widehat{\chi_{0}}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
is rapidly decaying.

8.4.
L^{2}
- estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
\tilde{E}_{l}

In the same way as we had argued in Subsection
8.4
, we obtain the same kind of
L^{2}
-estimate:

(8.12)

\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{j,k}\|_{2\to 2}\lesssim 2^{-j}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

8.5.
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
\tilde{E}_{l}
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

8.5.1.
Reduction to
K|\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\lesssim 1

a) Let us first consider the
{\sigma}_{2}
-region where

(8.13)

K|\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\gg 1.

Then
K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z\sim K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}).

Case 1:
{\lambda}d_{j,k}\geq 1
.
Note that this implies that

{\lambda}\geq 2^{kA+j(B+2)}\geq 2^{2j},\quad\text{hence}\quad 2^{j}\leq{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Given any
0<{\delta}\ll 1,
we may then assume that
K|\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\geq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{j}.

For, if

1\ll K|\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|<{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{j},

then we would have
1\ll{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1/2},
contradicting our assumption that
{\lambda}\gg 1,
if we choose
{\delta}<1/2.

But, if
K|\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\geq\max\{1,{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{j}\},
then

|{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]|\geq{\lambda}^{{\delta}},

so that by (
8.11
) and (
8.5
) we see that the contribution to
\mu_{j,k}
by this
{\sigma}_{2}
-region is a small error term of order
O({\lambda}^{-N})
for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Case 2:
{\lambda}d_{j,k}<1
.
Here, we may ignore the term
d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}
of the phase in (
8.11
), since we can include the factor
e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2})}
in the amplitude. Let us thus assume that

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-j}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\tilde{h}(s_{2})}\,\int{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(2^{-j}z\tilde{g}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})+\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})\big)}\chi_{1}(z)\eta dz,

where we have put

\tilde{g}^{s}({\sigma}_{2}):=\tilde{g}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),\quad\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2}):=\tilde{h}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}).

This case will require a more careful analysis. Observe first that by (
8.9
) and (
6.2
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w(s_{2})^{n}\beta(w(s_{2}))+s_{2}w(s_{2})^{m}\omega(w(s_{2}))+s_{2}^{2}w(s_{2})q(w(s_{2}),s_{2}),

\displaystyle\tilde{g}(s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w(s_{2})^{n-1}\tilde{\beta}(w(s_{2}))+s_{2}w(s_{2})^{m-1}\tilde{\omega}(w(s_{2}))+s_{2}^{2}\tilde{q}(s_{2})=:\tilde{g}_{0}(s_{2})+s_{2}^{2}\tilde{q}(s_{2}).

Note that the term
s_{2}^{2}w(s_{2})q(w(s_{2}),s_{2})
can be absorbed by the term
s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})
in (
8.5
), by slightly modifying the definition of
B(s_{2}),
still keeping the condition
|B(s_{2})|\sim 1.
Let us therefore henceforth simply assume that

\tilde{h}(s_{2})=w(s_{2})^{n}\beta(w(s_{2}))+s_{2}w(s_{2})^{m}\omega(w(s_{2})).

Lemma 8.3
.

For every
{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{N}},
the following estimate holds true:

|{\partial}_{{\sigma}_{2}}^{\alpha}\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})|\lesssim 2^{-k(1+ma)},\qquad|{\partial}_{{\sigma}_{2}}^{\alpha}\tilde{g}_{0}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})|\lesssim 2^{-k(1+(m-1)a)},\quad\text{if}\quad{\sigma}_{2}\sim 1.

Proof.

Note that

na-(1+ma)=(n-m)a-1\geq(n-m)a_{1}-1=0,

so that
na\geq 1+ma
and also
(n-1)a\geq 1+(m-1)a.

Since
|w(s_{2})|\sim s_{2}^{a}\sim 2^{-ka},
the claimed estimates now follow easily.

Q.E.D.

Subcase 2.a:
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\lesssim 1.
Here, by Lemma
8.3
,
{\lambda}\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})=O(2^{-k})
and

{\lambda}\tilde{g}_{0}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})=O(2^{-k}),
since
m\geq 2.
We may thus ignore the terms
\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})
and
2^{-j}z\tilde{g}_{0}({\sigma}_{2})
in the amplitude of
\tilde{F}_{j,k}.

Moreover, since we shall here not use any oscillation from the
z
-integration, we may also assume that the term
2^{-j}zs_{2}^{2}\tilde{q}(s_{2})
stemming from our decomposition of
\tilde{g}(s_{2})
is absorbed by the term
s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})
in (
8.5
), so that

\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=2^{-j}\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}2^{-j}s_{1}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]}\chi_{0}(s_{1})\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dzds_{1}.

Note also that

{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]={\lambda}2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2})-{\lambda}2^{-ka}Y_{1}+{\lambda}2^{-j}z.

The first term
{\lambda}2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2})
can later on be included into the amplitude in (
8.5
), since
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\lesssim 1,
so that we can ignore this term.

Assume first that
|Y_{1}|\gg 1.
Then

1\ll|K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\sim K|Y_{1}|,
hence

(8.14)

{\lambda}2^{-j}|K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z|\sim{\lambda}2^{-ka}|Y_{1}|.

We can then proceed in a similar way as we did in the first part of Subsection
7.4
.

If
|Y_{1}|\gg 1
and
|Y_{1}|\geq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{ka},
integrations by parts in
s_{1}
show that

F_{j,k}=O({\lambda}^{-N})
for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}},
and thus also
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})=O({\lambda}^{-N})
for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}},
which shows that this leads just to a small error term.

So, assume next that

1\ll|Y_{1}|\lesssim{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{ka}.

We then decompose this region into the regions

\Delta_{\iota_{1}}:=\{y:|Y_{1}|\sim 2^{\iota_{1}}\},

where clearly we may assume that
1\ll\iota_{1}\lesssim k.
We further decompose
\Delta_{\iota_{1}}
into the subregions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\sim 2^{\iota_{1}},|Y_{2}|\sim 2^{\iota_{2}}\},\quad 1\ll\iota_{2}\leq k,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{1},0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\sim 2^{\iota_{1}},|Y_{2}|\lesssim 1\}.

Then

|\Delta_{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}}|\lesssim 2^{\iota_{1}+\iota_{2}-ka-k}.

For
y\in\Delta_{\iota_{1}},
then we may integrate by parts in
s_{1}.

Moreover, if
\iota_{2}\gg 1,
and if
{\lambda}2^{-2k+\iota_{2}}\geq 1,
then our preceding discussion and (
7.11
) show that we can also integrate by parts in
{\sigma}_{2},
and altogether this leads to the following estimate for
\mu_{k,j,\iota}^{\lambda}:

|\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-ka}2^{\iota_{1}})^{-1}({\lambda}2^{-2k}2^{\iota_{2}})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{-j+k+ka-\iota_{1}-\iota_{2}}.

Thus, Lemma
5.4
shows that, for every
{\varepsilon}>0,

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{-j}.

And, if
\iota_{2}=0,
we can again apply van der Corput’s estimate of order 2 in
{\sigma}_{2},
which leads to

|\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-ka}2^{\iota_{1}})^{-1}({\lambda}2^{-2k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}={\lambda}2^{-j+ka-\iota_{1}},

hence

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,(\iota_{1},0)}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}.

These estimate have to be summed over the
\iota_{2}
s, which will produce an extra factor of order
O(k).
However, later on we have to sum over the
j
s and
k
s such that
k\lesssim j/\tilde{a},
so effectively by summing we shall only pick up a factor of order
O(2^{{\varepsilon}j}),
where we may choose
{\varepsilon}>0
as small as we need; this will cause no harm.

Assume next that
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1.
Here we decompose into the regions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,|Y_{2}|\sim 2^{\iota_{2}}\},\quad 1\ll\iota_{2}\leq k,

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|,|Y_{2}|\lesssim 1\}.

Then

|\Delta_{\iota_{2}}|\lesssim 2^{\iota_{2}-ka-k}.

We can now only gain from integration by parts (respectively an application of van der Corput’s estimate) in
{\sigma}_{2},
and thus obtain

|\mu_{j,k,\iota_{2}}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-2k}2^{\iota_{2}})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-j+k-\iota_{2}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{-j+k+ka-\iota_{2}},

where we have used our assumption
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\lesssim 1.

Similarly,

|\mu_{j,k,0}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-2k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}={\lambda}^{2}2^{-j}\lesssim{\lambda}2^{-j+ka}.

This shows that we arrive at the same estimates as before, if we choose
\iota_{1}=0
therein.

Subcase 2.b:
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\gg 1.

Assume again first that
|Y_{1}|\gg 1,
so that (
8.14
) holds true.

If
|Y_{1}|\geq{\lambda}^{\delta}
for some
{\delta}
such that
0<{\delta}\ll 1,
then
{\lambda}2^{-j}|K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z|\gtrsim{\lambda}^{{\delta}},
hence

(8.15)

\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+\Gamma)=O({\lambda}^{-N})\qquad\text{for every}\quad N\in{\mathbb{N}},

since
{\varphi}
is rapidly decaying, so this case contributes again only a small error term.

So, assume next w.l.o.g. that
1\ll|Y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{\delta}.
Then

(8.16)

|y_{1}|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\delta}2^{-ka},

and, by (
8.14
),

(8.17)

|{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)|\lesssim({\lambda}2^{-ka}|Y_{1}|)^{-N}\leq({\lambda}2^{-ka})^{-N}

for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Again, if
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\geq{\lambda}^{\delta},
this implies (
8.15
), so let us assume that
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\leq{\lambda}^{\delta}.

Then, by Lemma
8.3
, since
m\geq 2,

|{\lambda}\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})|\lesssim{\lambda}2^{-k(1+ma)}={\lambda}^{1-m}({\lambda}2^{-ka})^{m}2^{-k}\ll{\lambda}^{1-m}{\lambda}^{{\delta}m}\leq{\lambda}^{{\delta}m-1}\ll 1,

if we choose
{\delta}>0
sufficiently small. Similarly,

|{\lambda}2^{-j}z\tilde{g}_{0}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})|\lesssim 2^{-j}{\lambda}2^{-k(1+(m-1)a)}\leq 2^{-j}({\lambda}2^{-ka})2^{-k}.

Let us next write
\tilde{a}=da,
where
d>0.
Since we are on
\tilde{E}_{l},
by (
8.6
) we then have that
j\geq k\tilde{a}=kda.
Thus, for every
{\varepsilon}>0,

\displaystyle|{\lambda}2^{-j}z\tilde{g}_{0}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 2^{-kda}({\lambda}2^{-ka})2^{-k}={\lambda}^{-{\varepsilon}}({\lambda}2^{-ka})^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k(1+da-a{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{-{\varepsilon}+{\delta}(1+{\varepsilon})}2^{-k(1+da-a{\varepsilon})}.

Choosing first
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small, and then
{\delta}>0
sufficiently small, we see that also
|{\lambda}2^{-j}z\tilde{g}_{0}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})|\ll 1.

We may thus again ignore the terms
\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})
and
2^{-j}z\tilde{g}_{0}({\sigma}_{2})
in the amplitude of
\tilde{F}_{j,k},
so that we may assume that

(8.18)

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}\iint{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)\,e^{i{\lambda}s_{3}y_{3}}(1-\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})))

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\times e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}2^{-2k}\Big({\sigma}_{2}^{2}\big(B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+2^{-j}z\tilde{q}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})\big)-{\sigma}_{2}Y_{2}\Big)}\chi_{1}(z)\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\eta dzd{\sigma}_{2}ds_{3}.

Here, we assume that
\chi_{0}\equiv 1
on a sufficiently large neighborhood of the origin, and we have put

Y_{2}:=2^{k}y_{2}.

Reduction to
|Y_{2}|\lesssim 1.
Note that here

\displaystyle\Big|{\partial}_{{\sigma}_{2}}\big({\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle{\lambda}2^{-ka}\Big|{\varphi}^{\prime}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\Big|\tilde{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)\Big|,

where
\tilde{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
, and where we have used (
8.14
) and
1\leq|Y_{1}|
for the last inequality. Note that also the
{\sigma}_{2}
-derivative of
\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))
can be controlled in a similar way, since
K=2^{j}2^{-ka}\leq{\lambda}2^{-ka}.

Thus, the
{\sigma}_{2}
-derivative of the amplitude in (
8.18
) behaves in the same way as the factor itself.
In a similar way as in Subsection
7.4
, let us consider now the subregions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:1\ll|Y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{\delta},|Y_{2}|\sim 2^{\iota}\},\quad 1\ll\iota_{2}\leq k.

Since for
y\in\Delta_{\iota}

\Big|{\partial}_{{\sigma}_{2}}\Big({\sigma}_{2}^{2}\big(B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+2^{-j}z\tilde{q}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})\big)-{\sigma}_{2}Y_{2}\Big)\Big|\sim 2^{\iota},

an integration by parts in
{\sigma}_{2},
using also (
8.17
) with
N=1,
shows that

|\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|={\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-ka})^{-1}\,({\lambda}2^{-2k}2^{\iota})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{-j}2^{k(a+1)}2^{-\iota}.

And, by (
8.16
), we find that

|\Delta_{\iota}|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\delta}2^{-ka}2^{-k}2^{\iota},

so that we conclude that

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j},

provided we choose
{\delta}>0
sufficiently small.
Here,
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}
denotes the contribution by
\Delta_{\iota}
to
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}.
Thus, we obtain the following analogue of
(
7.12
):

\sum\limits_{1\ll\iota\leq k}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j}.

We have thus reduced ourselves to the region

\Delta_{0}:=\{y:1\ll|Y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{\delta},|Y_{2}|\lesssim 1\}.

Here, we apply van der Corput’s estimate of order
2
(Lemma
5.1
) in
{\sigma}_{2}
and obtain

|\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|={\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-ka})^{-1}\,({\lambda}2^{-2k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}={\lambda}2^{-j}2^{ka},

whereas
|\Delta_{0}|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\delta}2^{-ka}2^{-k},
so that we shall here obtain the estimate

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,0}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}

as in (
7.13
) of Section
7
, and altogether we arrive also here at the estimate (
7.14
) for the contribution by the region where
|Y_{1}|\gg 1.

We have thus reduced ourselves to estimating
the contribution by the region where
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1.

Recall also that we are still assuming (
8.13
) and
{\lambda}2^{-j}\geq 1.

We next observe that we can further reduce to the case where
1\ll|K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\lesssim 2^{j}{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1},
again with
0<{\delta}\ll 1,
for if
|K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\gg 2^{j}{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1},
we see from (
8.18
) that the contribution to
\mu_{j,k}
by this
{\sigma}_{2}
-region is a small error term of order
O({\lambda}^{-N})
for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}.

So, let us assume that

{\sigma}_{2}\in\Sigma:=\{{\sigma}_{2}\sim 1:|{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{ka}\}.

Then
|\Sigma|\lesssim{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{ka},
and thus

\iint\limits_{\Sigma}\big|{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)\big|\,\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})|\eta|dzd{\sigma}_{2}\lesssim|\Sigma|({\lambda}2^{-j})^{-1}={\lambda}^{{\delta}-2}2^{j+ka},

hence, by (
8.18
),

|\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|={\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}{\lambda}^{{\delta}-2}2^{j+ka}={\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\delta}}2^{k(a-1)}.

Moreover, since here
y\in\Delta:=\{y:1\ll|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1\},
where
|\Delta|\lesssim 2^{-ka},
by choosing
{\delta}>0
sufficiently small we may conclude that

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k}.

Note that this type of estimate had not yet appeared in Section
7
.

b) We have thus reduced ourselves to estimating the contribution by the
{\sigma}_{2}
-region where

(8.19)

K|\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\lesssim 1.

A look at the phase
\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}
in (
8.10
) then shows that we can now again apply Lemma
5.2
in the variables
(s_{1},z)
in place of
(\eta,x)
to the oscillatory integral
\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})
in (
8.11
), with
N:={\lambda}2^{-j}\geq 1.

Moreover, observe that
s_{1}
and
z
result from our original coordinates
s_{1}
and
z_{1}=x_{1}
by means of a smooth change of coordinates. Passing back to our original coordinate, according to Remarks
5.3
we therefore must evaluate the original phase
\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}x_{1}-s_{1}y_{1}
in (
6.6
) at
x_{1}=y_{1},
and thus get

\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=({\lambda}2^{-j})^{-1}2^{-j}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},s_{2})}\eta(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}).

This implies that

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3}\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))\,\eta d{\sigma}_{2}ds_{3}

for the contribution by the region where
K|{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\lesssim 1.

By (
6.2
), we have

\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}):=y_{1}^{n}\beta(y_{1})+2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})+2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}y_{1}q(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}).

The last term can be absorbed by the term
2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),
by slightly modifying
B(s_{2})
to
B(s_{2},y_{1}),
which allows us to assume for simplicity that
q\equiv 0.
Then we may re-write

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}\int e^{-is_{3}{\lambda}\big(2^{-k}\Phi_{1}({\sigma}_{2},y)+y_{1}^{n}\beta(y_{1})-y_{3}\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))\,\eta d{\sigma}_{2}ds_{3},

where the phase
\Phi_{1}({\sigma}_{2},y)
is given by

\Phi_{1}({\sigma}_{2},y):=-{\sigma}_{2}[y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})]+2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},y_{1}).

We shall now distinguish two subcases:

The subcase where
K\lesssim 1.
We claim that then our assumption (
8.19
) implies that
|y_{1}|\lesssim 2^{-j}.

To this end, note first that the condition
K\lesssim 1
is equivalent to
2^{-ka}\lesssim 2^{-j}.
Thus, if
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,
then
|y_{1}|\lesssim 2^{-ka}\lesssim 2^{-j}.
And, if
|Y_{1}|\gg 1,
then

1\gtrsim K|{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))|\sim K|Y_{1}|=2^{j}|y_{1}|,
so that again
|y_{1}|\lesssim 2^{-j}.

Observe next that here all
{\sigma}_{2}
-derivatives of
\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))
are uniformly bounded. This shows that we can now just follow the arguments from the last part of Subsection
7.4
(after identity (
7.15
)), by either integrating by parts in
{\sigma}_{2},
or applying van der Corput’s estimates, in order to arrive again at estimates of the form (
7.16
) for the corresponding contributions to
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
(notice that in Subsection
7.4
the quantity
Y_{1}
had been defined in a different way, so that there

|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1
had been equivalent to
|y_{1}|\lesssim 2^{-j}
).

The subcase where
K\gg 1.
Here we see that necessarily
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1.
We shall again try to follow our reasoning from Subsection
7.4
, but shall see that the case
\iota_{2}=0
will still require a more refined reasoning.

Let us again decompose the region where
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1
into the subregions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota_{2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,2^{k}|y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})|\sim 2^{\iota_{2}}\},1\ll\iota_{2}\leq k,

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1,2^{k}|y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})|\lesssim 1\}.

Then

|\Delta_{\iota_{2}}|\lesssim 2^{-ka}2^{\iota_{2}-k}.

Assume first that
\iota_{2}\gg 1.
Observe first that

\int\Big|{\partial}_{{\sigma}_{2}}\big(\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\eta\big)\Big|\,d{\sigma}_{2}\lesssim K\int\big|\chi^{\prime}_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))\big|\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\,d{\sigma}_{2}\lesssim 1,

since here
{\sigma}_{2}\sim 1,
so that we may use
\tau_{2}:={\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2})
as a new variable of integration in place of
{\sigma}_{2}.

Thus, we may again integrate by parts in
{\sigma}_{2}
and obtain that

|\mu_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1,\iota_{2}}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}({\lambda}2^{-2k+\iota_{2}})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{k-\iota_{2}},

hence

(8.20)

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1,\iota_{2}}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-ka}.

Note that this type of estimate had not yet appeared in Section
7
.

Assume finally that
\iota_{2}=0.
Then a direct application of van der Corput’s estimate of order 2 in
{\sigma}_{2}
would lead in a similar way to the estimate

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1,0}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}2^{-k}2^{-ka},

which turns out to be insufficient. Indeed, we shall need to gain a factor
K^{-1}=2^{ka}2^{-j}
over this estimate, which will require a more refined argument.

Note first that

|{\partial}_{{\sigma}_{2}}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\sim 1.
This allows us to write, for
{\sigma}_{2}\sim 1,

{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})=(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{2}^{c})W(\sigma_{2},Y_{1}),

where
{\sigma}_{2}^{c}={\sigma}_{2}(Y_{1})
and
W
are analytic functions with
|{\sigma}_{2}^{c}|\sim 1\sim|W|.

It is then natural to change coordinates from
{\sigma}_{2}
to

\tau_{2}:=K({\sigma}_{2}-{\sigma}_{2}^{c}),\quad\text{i.e.,}\quad{\sigma}_{2}={\sigma}_{2}^{c}+K^{-1}\tau_{2}.

Then, for
y\in\Delta_{0},
we may assume that, with respect to our new variables of integration,

(8.21)

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1,0}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}K^{-1}\int e^{-is_{3}{\lambda}\big(2^{-k}\Phi_{2}(\tau_{2},y)+y_{1}^{n}\beta(y_{1})-y_{3}\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2}^{c}+K^{-1}\tau_{2})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,\chi_{0}(\tau_{2})\,\eta d\tau_{2}ds_{3},

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(\tau_{2},y):=\Phi_{1}({\sigma}_{2}^{c}+K^{-1}\tau_{2},y)=-\big({\sigma}_{2}[y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})]+2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},y_{1})\big)\Big|_{{\sigma}_{2}:={\sigma}_{2}^{c}+K^{-1}\tau_{2}}.

Since
y\in\Delta_{0},
we may further write

y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}\omega(y_{1})=:2^{-k}Y_{2},\quad\text{where}\quad|Y_{2}|\lesssim 1,

so that

\Phi_{2}(\tau_{2},y)=\Phi_{1}({\sigma}_{2}^{c}+K^{-1}\tau_{2},y)=2^{-k}\big(-{\sigma}_{2}Y_{2}+{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},y_{1})\big)\Big|_{{\sigma}_{2}:={\sigma}_{2}^{c}(Y_{1})+K^{-1}\tau_{2}}.

By performing a Taylor expansion of the phase function
\Phi_{1}({\sigma}_{2}^{c}+K^{-1}\tau_{2},y)
with respect to
K^{-1}\tau_{2},
we see that we may write

{\lambda}2^{-k}\Phi_{2}(\tau_{2},y)={\lambda}2^{-2k}\Big(H_{0}(Y)+K^{-1}\tau_{2}\,H_{1}(Y)+K^{-2}\tau_{2}^{2}\,H_{2}(Y,K^{-1}\tau_{2})\Big),

where the
H_{j}
are analytic functions of their very variables, and where

(8.22)

|H_{2}|\sim 1,\qquad H_{1}(Y)=-(Y_{2}-A(y_{1}))=-2^{k}\big(y_{2}-y_{1}^{m}{\omega}(y_{1})-2^{-k}A(y_{1})\big).

Here,

A(y_{1})=2{\sigma}_{2}^{c}(Y_{1})B(0,y_{1})+D(y_{1}),
with an error term
D(y_{1})
of order
O(2^{-k}).
Note also that

\big|{\partial}_{\tau_{2}}^{2}\big[\tau_{2}^{2}H_{2}(Y,K^{-1}\tau_{2})\big]\big|\sim 1,\qquad\big|{\partial}_{\tau_{2}}\big[\tau_{2}^{2}H_{2}(Y,K^{-1}\tau_{2})\big]\big|\lesssim 1,

since
K\gg 1.

We are thus led to decomposing the
y
-region
\Delta_{0}
into the following subsets:

\displaystyle\Delta_{0,\nu}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y\in\Delta_{0}:|H_{1}(Y)|\sim 2^{\nu}K^{-1}\},\qquad 1\ll\nu\lesssim\log K\leq\log{\lambda};

\displaystyle\Delta_{0,0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y\in\Delta_{0}:|H_{1}(Y)|\lesssim K^{-1}\}.

As usually, the corresponding measures will be denoted by
\mu_{j,k,\nu}^{\lambda},
and so forth.
Since
2^{ka}|y_{1}|=|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1
on
\Delta_{0},
in combination with (
8.22
) we see that

(8.23)

|\Delta_{0,\nu}|\lesssim 2^{-ka}2^{-k}2^{\nu}K^{-1}.

Assume first that
\nu\gg 1.
Then an integration by parts with respect to
\tau_{2}
in (
8.21
) leads to

|\mu_{j,k,\nu}^{\lambda}(y+\Gamma)|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}K^{-1}\big[{\lambda}2^{-2k}\,2^{\nu}K^{-2}\big]^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}2^{k-\nu}K,

hence, in combination with (
8.23
),

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\nu}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-ka},

i.e., the same type of estimate as in (
8.20
).

Assume next that
\nu=0.
Here, we can again apply van der Corput’s estimate of order 2 in
\tau_{2}
and obtain that

|\mu_{j,k,0}^{\lambda}(y+\Gamma)|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}K^{-1}\big[{\lambda}2^{-2k}\,K^{-2}\big]^{-\frac{1}{2}}={\lambda},

hence

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,0}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}2^{-ka}2^{-k}K^{-1}={\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k},

which is again of the form (
7.13
).

Combining all the estimates from this subsection, we see that in analogy with the estimate (
7.16
) which we had established for Step 1, for Step 2 we obtain that for every
{\varepsilon}>0
we have the following estimate:

(8.24)

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-ka}.

8.6.
L^{p}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l}
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

Interpolation between the
L^{2}
- estimate (
8.12
) and the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimate (
8.24
) leads to

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle({\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}(2-\frac{2}{p})}2^{-j}+({\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}(2-\frac{2}{p})}2^{-j}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle({\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}\big(2^{-j}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{2-\frac{2}{p}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle({\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-ka})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}\big(2^{-j}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)^{2-\frac{2}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\,(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}\,2^{-j}

\displaystyle+\,{\lambda}^{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\,(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}2^{-j}

\displaystyle+{\lambda}^{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\,(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle+{\lambda}^{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}({\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\lambda}d_{j,k})^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\,(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\,2^{-ka(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})},

if
1<p<2.

Arguing as in Subsection
7.5
, we see that we can sum over all dyadic
{\lambda}
s and obtain that, for every
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0,

(8.25)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{\lambda}\gg 1}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}+(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-ka(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}.

Recall next that
A=\tilde{A}_{l},B=\tilde{B}_{l},
so that by (
8.4
)

\frac{A-1}{\tilde{a}}+B+2\leq m,\quad\text{and}\quad d_{j,k}^{-1}=2^{kA+j(B+2)}.

Moreover, since we are on
\tilde{E}_{l},
by (
8.6
) we have
j\geq k\tilde{a}.

This shows that we can argue in exactly the same way as in Subsection
7.6
in order to show that we can sum the first two terms on the right-hand side of (
8.25
) over all
k
s and
j
s such that
j\geq k\tilde{a}.

This leaves us with showing that

(8.26)

\sum\limits_{j,k\gg 1:j\geq k\tilde{a}}\Big((d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}+(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-ka(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}\Big)<\infty,

provided
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
is sufficiently small.

But, if
j\geq k\tilde{a},
since
A\geq 1,
we see that

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2^{kA+j(B+2)})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2^{k(A-1)+j(B+2)})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}-{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{j\big(\frac{A-1}{\tilde{a}}+B+2\big)(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\,2^{-k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}-{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{jm(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\,2^{-k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}-{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{j\big[m(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})+\frac{2}{p}-2\big]}\,2^{-k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}-{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}.

But,

m(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})+\frac{2}{p}-2<m\frac{1}{2m}+\frac{2}{p}-2=\frac{2}{p}-\frac{3}{2}<0,

since
p>\max\{3/2,h\}.
Thus we see that if we choose
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
sufficiently small, then both the exponent of
2^{j}
as well as that of
2^{k}
in the last estimate are negative, so that we can sum these estimates over all
j,k\geq 1.

We are thus left with the second sum in (
8.26
). But, since
j\geq k\tilde{a},

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-ka(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2^{kA+j(B+2)})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-ka(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2^{k(A-1)+j(B+2)})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-ka(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{j\big(\frac{A-1}{\tilde{a}}+B+2\big)(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\,2^{k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-\frac{2a}{p}+a)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{j\big[m(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})+\frac{2}{p}-2\big]}\,2^{k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-\frac{2a}{p}+a)}.

Again, we may assume that the exponent of
2^{j}
is strictly negative.

Assume first that
\tilde{a}\geq a.

Then
j\geq ka,
hence after summing first in
j\geq ka,
we are left with the sum

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}2^{ka\big[m(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})+\frac{2}{p}-2\big]}\,2^{k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-\frac{2a}{p}+a)}.

But, the exponent of
2^{k}
can be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle a\big[m(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})+\frac{2}{p}-2\big]+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2a}{p}+a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(am+1)(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})-a

\displaystyle<(am+1)\frac{1}{n}-a=\frac{1-(n-m)a}{n}\leq 0,

since
p>h
and
a\geq a_{1}=1/(n-m).

By choosing
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
sufficiently small, we can thus finally also sum over all
k\geq 1.

Assume finally that
\tilde{a}<a.

Then
-2a/p+a=-2a(1/p-1/2)<-2\tilde{a}(1/p-1/2)=-2\tilde{a}/p+\tilde{a},
so that we can here estimate

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-ka(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{j\big[m(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})+\frac{2}{p}-2\big]}\,2^{k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-\frac{2\tilde{a}}{p}+\tilde{a})}.

Thus, after summing first in
j\geq k\tilde{a},
we are left with the sum

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}2^{k\tilde{a}\big[m(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})+\frac{2}{p}-2\big]}\,2^{k(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-\frac{2\tilde{a}}{p}+\tilde{a})}.

From here on, we can argue as before, only with
a
replaced by
\tilde{a};
note here that also
\tilde{a}\geq a_{1}.

This concludes the proof of estimate (
8.26
).

8.7.
L^{p}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l}
and
\tilde{E}_{l}
when
{\lambda}<2^{j}

We shall study here both the situations in Step 1 as well as in Step 2.
Let us begin by observing that the condition
{\lambda}<2^{j}
implies that

(8.27)

{\lambda}d_{j,k}<2^{-j}\ll 1.

This implies by (
7.9
), respectively (
8.12
), that here we have the
L^{2}
-estimate

(8.28)

\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}_{j,k}\|_{2\to 2}\lesssim 2^{-j}.

As for the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
-estimates, let us first look at the
Step 1-situation and the contribution by
E_{l}
:

By (
7.10
) and (
8.27
), we may here assume that

\Phi_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}=-s_{1}y_{1};

the exponentials of all other terms of the phase can be included into the amplitude. Thus,

\displaystyle F_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-j}\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\Phi_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1})}\chi_{0}(s_{1})\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dzds_{1}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle 2^{-j}{\varphi}(-{\lambda}s_{3}y_{1}),

where
{\varphi}:=\widehat{\chi_{0}}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
is rapidly decaying.
This shows that, up to a small error term, we can reduce to the region where

|y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1},

where again we may later on choose
{\delta}>0
as small as we like. But then we obtain

(8.29)

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}

\displaystyle\times\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})d{\sigma}_{2}\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,{\varphi}(-{\lambda}s_{3}y_{1})ds_{3}.

Let us first assume that
{\lambda}\geq 2^{k}.

In a similar way as in Subsection
7.4
we may then decompose the remaining
y
-region into the subregions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1},|y_{2}|\sim 2^{\iota-k}\},\quad 1\ll\iota\leq k,

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1},|y_{2}|\lesssim 2^{-k}\}.

Then

|\Delta_{\iota}|\lesssim{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{\iota-k}.

And, as before, if
\iota\gg 1,
an integration by parts in
{\sigma}_{2}
shows that

|\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{\iota-2k})^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-j}2^{k-\iota}.

Hence, if we choose
{\delta}
sufficiently small,

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}/2}2^{-j}2^{k-\iota}{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1}2^{\iota-k}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j},

which leads to the following analogue of (
7.12
):

\sum\limits_{\iota\leq k}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j}.

If
\iota=0,
we can again apply van der Corput’s estimate and obtain in a similar way that

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,0}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}.

Altogether, we thus obtain the following analogue of (
7.16
):

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j}.

Interpolating the estimates (
8.28
) and (
7.16
), we find that for
1<p<2,

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}\lesssim({\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}2^{-j}+({\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}2^{-j}.

As for the first term, summing over all
k\geq 0
and all dyadic
{\lambda}
s such that
{\lambda}<2^{j},
we are left with the sum

\sum\limits_{j\geq 0}2^{j\big((1+{\varepsilon})(\frac{2}{p}-1)-1\big)},

which is easily seen to be convergent if we choose
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small.

As for the second term, summing first over all all
k\leq\log{\lambda}
and then all dyadic
{\lambda}
s that
{\lambda}<2^{j},
we are left with the sum

\sum\limits_{j\geq 0}2^{j\big((\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon})(\frac{2}{p}-1)-1\big)},

with an
{\varepsilon}>0
which may have to be chosen slightly bigger than before. Again, it is easily seen that the latter series is convergent if we choose
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small, since
p>3/2.

Let us finally assume that
{\lambda}<2^{k}.
Then the “oscillatory integral”
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})
has indeed essentially no oscillation, so that we only get the estimates

|\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}\quad\text{and}\quad|\Delta|\lesssim{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1},

where
\Delta:=\{y:|y_{1}|\leq{\lambda}^{{\delta}-1},|y_{2}|\ll 1\}.
Thus,

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k},

hence

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}\lesssim({\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}2^{-j}.

Summing first over all
j
s such that
2^{j}>{\lambda}
and all
k
s such that
2^{k}>{\lambda},
we are left with the sum

\sum\limits_{{\lambda}\gg 1}{\lambda}^{\big((\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}-1)(\frac{2}{p}-1)-1\big)}.

But,
(3/2-1)(2/p-1)-1=1/p-3/2<-5/6,
since
p>3/2,
so that the sum is again convergent if we choose
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small.

Let us next look at the
Step 2-situation and the contribution by
\tilde{E}_{l}
:

By (
8.10
) and (
8.27
), we may here assume that

\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}=s_{1}2^{-ka}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}),

where
z\sim 1\sim{\sigma}_{2}.
Putting again
\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2}):=\tilde{h}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),
we find that

\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=2^{-j}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})}\,\int{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-ka}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})\big)\chi_{1}(z)\eta dz,

where
{\varphi}:=\widehat{\chi_{0}}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
is rapidly decaying and, by (
8.5
),

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-k}\times

\displaystyle\iint\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})d{\sigma}_{2}\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3}.

Assume first that
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\geq 1.
We then decompose in
y
into the regions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\iota}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\sim 2^{\iota}\},\quad 1\ll\iota\leq ka,

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{y:|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1\},

and denote the corresponding contributions by
\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}.

Then

|\Delta_{\iota}|\lesssim 2^{\iota}2^{-ka}.

And, if
\iota\gg 1,
then clearly

|\tilde{F}_{j,k,\iota}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})|\lesssim 2^{-j}({\lambda}2^{-ka}2^{\iota})^{-1},

hence

|\mu_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-ka}2^{\iota})^{-1}.

This implies that

(8.30)

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}.

Finally, if
\iota=0,
we can estimate

\displaystyle|\mu_{j,k,0}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}\int\big|{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-ka}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})\big)\big|\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})d{\sigma}_{2}\,\chi_{1}(s_{3})\chi_{1}(z)dzds_{3}.

But,

\int\big|{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-ka}({\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2})-Y_{1})\big)\big|\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})d{\sigma}_{2}\lesssim({\lambda}2^{-ka})^{-1},

since
{\sigma}_{2}\sim 1,
so that we may use
\tau_{2}:={\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2})
as a new variable of integration in place of
{\sigma}_{2}.
Thus,

|\mu_{j,k,0}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})|\lesssim{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}({\lambda}2^{-ka})^{-1},

so that (
8.30
) remains valid also for
\iota=0.
Interpolation with (
8.28
) leads to

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k,\iota}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}\lesssim\big({\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k}\big)^{\frac{2}{p}-1}2^{-j}.

Summing the estimate in first in
\iota\leq ka,
then in
k\geq 0
and next over all
j
s such that
2^{j}>{\lambda},
we are left with the sum

\sum\limits_{{\lambda}\gg 1}{\lambda}^{(\frac{3}{2}+{\varepsilon})(\frac{2}{p}-1)}{\lambda}^{-1}={\lambda}^{\frac{3}{p}-\frac{5}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}},

where
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
can be chosen as small as we like. Since
p>3/2,
we have that
3/p-5/2<2-5/2<0,
so that the remaining series in
{\lambda}
is convergent.

Assume next that
{\lambda}2^{-ka}<1.
Here, we must again invoke Lemma
8.3
. According to this lemma,
we have that for
{\sigma}_{2}\sim 1,

|{\partial}_{{\sigma}_{2}}^{\alpha}\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})|\lesssim 2^{-k(1+ma)}.

This implies that
{\lambda}2^{-k(1+ma)}\ll{\lambda}2^{-ka}<1,
which shows that we can now include the factor
e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})}
of
\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})
in the oscillatory integral defining
\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})
into the amplitude. I.e., we may simply assume that

\tilde{h}^{s}\equiv 0,
and thus write

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}\times

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2}){\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-ka}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})\big)\eta d{\sigma}_{2}\,\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3}.

But note that
{\lambda}2^{-ka}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})={\lambda}2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2})-{\lambda}y_{1},
where the first term is harmless, since
{\lambda}2^{-ka}\leq 1.

A comparison with the identity (
8.29
) thus shows that we may now argue exactly as we did for the Step 1- situation.

This concludes our discussion of the case where
{\lambda}<2^{j}.

8.8.
L^{p}
- estimates for the contributions by domains
D_{l_{1}}^{c},c\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
when the algorithm stops after step 1

Assume now that
c=c^{{\alpha}_{1}}_{l_{1}}\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}
is such that the cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]

contains only one, real root
r_{0}
of multiplicity

N_{0}:=N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right].

The leading term of
r_{0}(s_{2})
is then given by
c^{{\alpha}_{1}}_{l_{1}}s_{2}^{a},
where again
a:=a_{l_{1}}.

We can then
modify our change of coordinates by re-defining

\tilde{z}:=z_{1}-r_{0}(s_{2}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2}):=\Phi(\tilde{z}+r_{0}(s_{2}),s_{2}).

The domain corresponding to
D_{l_{1}}^{c}
is then essentially again of the form

\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}=\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):|\tilde{z}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}\},

with
0<{\varepsilon}\ll 1.
We claim that then also on
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}
we have a good resolution of singularities of
\tilde{\Phi},
namely

(8.31)

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=\tilde{V}(\tilde{z},s_{2})\,s_{2}^{\tilde{A}}\tilde{z}^{N_{0}},\qquad(\tilde{z},s_{2})\in\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c},

for some
\tilde{A}\geq 1,
where
\tilde{V}
has similar properties as in (
8.8
).

Indeed, in analogy to (
7.25
), we have that

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=\tilde{U}_{0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})(\tilde{z}+r_{0}(s_{2}))^{\nu_{1}}\prod_{l=1}^{L}\Phi\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right](\tilde{z}+r_{0}(s_{2}),s_{2}).

Since
|\tilde{z}|\ll|r_{0}(s_{2})|,
by modifying
\tilde{U}_{0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})
appropriately, we may replace here

(\tilde{z}+r_{0}(s_{2}))^{\nu_{1}}
by
s_{2}^{a\nu_{1}},
as well as

\Phi\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right](\tilde{z}+r_{0}(s_{2}),s_{2})

by the following:

– if
l<l_{1},
then by

s_{2}^{a_{l}N\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]};

– if
l=l_{1},
then by

\prod\limits_{{\alpha}\neq{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{aN\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]}\,\tilde{z}^{N_{0}};

– if
l>l_{1},
then by

s_{2}^{aN\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}.

This shows that indeed (
8.31
) holds true. Moreover, a quite similar reasoning shows that the
{\kappa}^{l_{1}}
-principal part of

\tilde{\Phi}
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{{\kappa}^{l_{1}}}={\rm constant}\,(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a})^{\nu_{1}}\prod\limits_{l<l_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l}N\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}\prod\limits_{{\alpha}\neq{\alpha}_{1}}\big(\tilde{z}-(c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}}-c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}})s_{2}^{a}\big)^{N\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}\\
l_{1}\end{matrix}\right]}\,\tilde{z}^{N_{0}}\prod\limits_{l>l_{1}}(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a})^{N\left[\begin{matrix}\cdot\\
l\end{matrix}\right]}.

The term with the smallest power of
\tilde{z}
in this
{\kappa}^{l_{1}}
- homogenous function agrees with a constant multiple of
s_{2}^{\tilde{A}}\tilde{z}^{N_{0}},
which shows that the Newton-Puiseux polyhedron of
\tilde{\Phi}
must be of the type sketched in Figure
6
.

Figure 6.
Stopping: comparison between
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
and
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi})

Consequently, we see that
\tilde{L}=l_{1},

(\tilde{B}_{\tilde{L}},\tilde{A}_{\tilde{L}})=(N_{0},\tilde{A})
and
\tilde{a}_{\tilde{L}}=a_{l_{1}}=a,
if
N_{0}<B_{l_{1}-1},

whereas
\tilde{L}=l_{1}-1,

(\tilde{B}_{\tilde{L}},\tilde{A}_{\tilde{L}})=(N_{0},\tilde{A})=(B_{l_{1}-1},A_{l_{1}-1})
and

\tilde{a}_{\tilde{L}}=a_{l_{1}-1},
if
N_{0}=B_{l_{1}-1}.

This shows that the region
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}
is contained in the transition domain

\tilde{E}_{\tilde{L}}:=\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):|\tilde{z}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{\tilde{L}}}\}

defined as in Subsection
8.2
, provided we choose
{\varepsilon}\ll 2^{-M},
so that the desired
L^{p}
-estimates for the contribution by
\tilde{D}_{l_{1}}^{c}
follow immediately from our discussions in the previous subsections.

8.9.
On the contributions by the homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l}

Recall that we are here in Case 2, i.e.,
c\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
and that more precisely in Subcase (i) we shall assume that
l\geq l_{1}+1\geq 2,
where
l_{1}\geq 1,
and in Subcase (ii) we assume that
l\geq l_{1}\geq 2.

The following notation, which lends for a generalisation to higher steps of the resolution algorithm, will be come useful:
We shall write
\Phi=:\Phi^{[1]},D_{l}=:D_{l}^{[1]},
and so forth, and

\tilde{\Phi}=:\Phi^{[2]},\quad\tilde{z}=:z_{2}=z_{1}-c^{{\alpha}_{1}}_{l_{1}}s_{2}^{a},\quad\tilde{D}_{l}=:D_{l}^{[2]},

and so forth, where as before
a=a_{l_{1}}.

Recall next from Remark
7.7
(c) that if we are on
D_{l}^{[2]}
and
if
\tilde{a}_{l}
is the leading exponent of a root
\tilde{r}(s_{2})
of
\Phi^{[2]}
lying in
D_{l}^{[2]},
then
\tilde{a}_{l}
is of the form
a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}},
and the leading term of
\tilde{r}(s_{2})
is of the form

c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}.

Fixing from here on
l_{2},
by replacing
\Phi=:\Phi^{[1]}
by
\Phi^{[2]},
and the set
\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}}
by the set

\mathcal{C}^{{\alpha}_{1}}_{l_{1},l_{2}}

of all
real
coefficients
c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}},

we can thus from here on proceed as in Subsection
7.8
, changing first
from the variable
z_{2}
to

\tilde{z}_{2}=z_{3}:=z_{2}-cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}=z_{1}-\big(c^{{\alpha}_{1}}_{l_{1}}s_{2}^{a}+cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}\big),

in order to pass from homogeneous regions

D_{l}^{[2],c}:=\{(z_{2},s_{2}):|z_{2}-cs_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}\},

to

\tilde{D}^{[2],c}:=\{(z_{3},s_{2}):|z_{3}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}\},

and from
\Phi^{[2]}
to
\tilde{\Phi}^{[2]}=:\Phi^{[3]},

and so forth.
Note also that if
c=c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}\in\mathcal{C}^{{\alpha}_{1}}_{l_{1},l_{2}},
then we can write

z_{3}=z_{1}-w(s_{2}),

if we choose for
w(s_{2})
here

w(s_{2}):=c^{{\alpha}_{1}}_{l_{1}}s_{2}^{a}+c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}},

i.e., the leading jet of the sub-cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
l_{1}&l_{2}\end{matrix}\right].

Moreover, we clearly still have that

|w(s_{2})|\sim s_{2}^{a}

(compare with Remark
8.2
).

We are thus led to Step 3 of the resolution of singularities algorithm, in which we can apply the same arguments as in Step 2, now applied to
\Phi^{[2]}
in place of
\Phi,
in order to derive the desired
L^{p}
-estimates for the associated maximal operators.

9.
Higher steps of the resolution algorithm and conclusion of the proof of Theorem
1.2

In the same way as we had passed from Step 1 to Step 2, and from Step 2 to Step 3, we can now proceed recursively.
Suppose we have already introduced the functions
\Phi^{[1]},\dots,\Phi^{[p]},p\geq 2,
where

\Phi^{[j]}(z_{j},s_{2})=\Phi(z_{j}+w_{j}(s_{2}),s_{2})\qquad j=2,\dots,p,

and where
w_{j}(s_{2})
is the real, leading jet of a sub-cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{j-1}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{j-1}\end{matrix}\right].

Note that then
z_{j}=z_{1}-w_{j}(s_{2}).

The transition domains associated to
\Phi^{[p]}
can be handled as before in Step 2.
And, by narrowing down in a similar way as in the previous steps to a homogeneous subdomain
D^{[p+1],c},
which corresponds to a narrow subdomain in the original coordinates
z_{1}
containing only roots from a sub-cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right]

of order
p
of the previous sub-cluster

\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p-1}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p-1}\end{matrix}\right]

of order
p-1

whose associated leading jet

w_{p+1}(s_{2}):=c_{l_{1}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}}}+c_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}{\alpha}_{2}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}l_{2}}^{{\alpha}_{1}}}+\cdots+c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}}

is real, we can introduce the next function

\Phi^{[p+1]}(z_{p+1},s_{2}):=\Phi(z_{p+1}+w_{p+1}(s_{2}),s_{2}).

Note that then

z_{p+1}=z_{p}-c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}},

and

\Phi^{[p+1]}(z_{p+1},s_{2})=\Phi^{[p]}(z_{p+1}+c_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p}}s_{2}^{a_{l_{1}\cdots l_{p}}^{{\alpha}_{1}\cdots{\alpha}_{p-1}}},s_{2}).

We have thus arrived at Step
p+1
and can bootstrap.

In this way, we shall construct nested sequences of subclusters

\Big\{\left[\begin{matrix}{\alpha}_{1}&\cdots&{\alpha}_{p}\\
l_{1}&\dots&l_{p}\end{matrix}\right]\Big\}_{p}.

The corresponding sequences of multiplicities of these sub-clusters are then decreasing and must therefore eventually become constant, say after step
p=P,
and we are reduced to a narrow homogeneous domain. But, arguing as in Subsection
7.9
, then the sub-cluster of order
P
can contain only one, real root, of some multiplicity
N_{0},
and we can estimate the contribution by this narrow homogeneous domain in the same way as we did in Subsection
8.8
.

This shows that our resolution algorithm will always stop after a finite number of steps; this completes the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

10.
First steps in the proof of Theorem
1.9

In this section, we assume that
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e},
with
n\geq 4.
Since the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
in the normal form (
1.3
) are in this case already adapted to

\phi
(because
n<2m),
we shall here not really make use of this normal form, but instead use Property
({\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+})
in these linearly adapted coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2}).

10.1.
Stationary phase in
x_{2}

We again first apply the method of stationary phase to the
x_{2}
-integration in (
5.2
), which leads to

(10.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-is_{3}{\lambda}}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\big[{\lambda}^{-1/2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{1}x_{1}+\phi(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})))}\tilde{\eta}(x_{1},s_{2})\,dx_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt+r({\lambda},s)\big],

with a slightly modified cut-off function
\chi_{1},

where
\tilde{\eta}
is another smooth bump function supported in a
sufficiently small neighborhood of the origin,
r({\lambda},s)
is a
remainder term of order

r({\lambda},s)=O({\lambda}^{-\frac{3}{2}})\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\lambda}\to+\infty,

and
x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2})
denotes the unique (non-degenerate)
critical point of the phase
\phi
with respect to
x_{2}.
Then, similarly as before,
\phi(x_{1},x_{2}^{c}(x_{1},s_{2}))=\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})
is the Legendre transform of
\phi.

Following the proof of Theorem
4.1
, let us choose line-adapted coordinates
(y_{1},y_{2})=(x_{1}-\alpha(x_{2}),x_{2}),
in which
\phi(x_{1},x_{2})
is given by
\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2}),
and decompose

\tilde{\phi}(y_{1},y_{2})=\tilde{\phi}(0,y_{2})+\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2}).

We then denote by
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
the first non-vertical edge of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}),
which has
(n,0)
as its left endpoint, and choose the weight
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}=(\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1},\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})=(1/n,\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2})
so that
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is contained in the line
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1.

Assume first that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0.
Then, as we have seen,
we may decompose

\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}(y_{1},y_{2})=p(y_{1},y_{2})+\tilde{\phi}_{\rm err}(y_{1},y_{2}),

where
p=p_{\tilde{e}_{1}}
is a
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
