-homogeneous polynomial of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
1
with Taylor support on the first compact edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\phi}_{\rm red}),
consisting of at least two monomial terms, one of them being of the form
cy_{1}^{n},
and where
\tilde{\phi}_{\rm err}
consists of terms of higher
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree.

If we put
z_{1}:=x_{1}-\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}),
with
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}):={\alpha}(s_{2}w_{0}(s_{2})),
then we had seen in the proofs of Lemma
4.2
and Theorem
4.1
that the new coordinates
(z_{1},s_{2})
are line-adapted to
\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2}).
Moreover, if we assume without loss of generality (for simplicity) that
w_{0}(0)=1,
then if
\breve{\phi}(x_{1},s_{2})
is represented by the function
\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}(z_{1},s_{2})
in these line-adapted coordinates, then by (
4.10
) we have the following analogue of (
6.1
):

\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}(z_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})

where

(10.2)

\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})=p(z_{1},s_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(z_{1},s_{2}),

with the same polynomial
p
as in (
1.9
) and an error term
\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}
consisting of terms of
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-degree
>1,
and where
p(0,s_{2})\equiv 0\equiv\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(0,s_{2}).

Note that this
defines
here the function
\breve{\phi}_{1}
for the present setting.

On the other hand, if
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
then we have seen in the proof of Theorem
4.1
that
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}_{\rm red})
is contained in the region where
t_{1}\geq n.
Since
\phi
is analytic, this implies that

(10.3)

\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}(z_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})=s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+z_{1}^{n}\big(G(s_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(z_{1},s_{2})\big),

where
G
and
\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}
are analytic and
G(0)\neq 0,
whereas
\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(0,s_{2})\equiv 0.

Remark 10.1
.

The identity (
10.3
) shows that the function
\breve{\phi}^{\rm la}
has already a good resolution of singularity when
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0,
and that here
n_{e}=n.
We shall therefore assume that
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}>0
in the subsequent sections, and only briefly comment on the case
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0
in Subsection
12.6
.

The contribution of the error term
r({\lambda},s)
to
\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}
in (
10.1
)
and the corresponding maximal operator are again easily estimated, and we shall henceforth ignore this error term.

After changing from the coordinate
x_{1}
to the coordinate
z_{1}
in the integral in (
10.1
), we then we may finally assume that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+s_{1}\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})+1\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{1}z_{1}+\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2}))}\eta\,dz_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{1}(s_{3})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})+s_{1}\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})+1\big)}J({\lambda},s),

where again

J({\lambda},s):=\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}z_{1})}\eta\,dz_{1}.

In order to defray the notation, we have simply dropped the tilde from
\eta.

Introducing also

(10.4)

\displaystyle F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int J({\lambda},s)e^{i{\lambda}s_{3}s_{1}(y_{1}-\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}))}\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{0}(s_{1})\,ds_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}(z_{1}+\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})-y_{1})\big)}\eta(z_{1},s_{2})\chi_{0}(s_{3}s^{\prime})\chi_{0}(s_{1})\,\eta dz_{1}ds_{1},

by Fourier inversion and a change from the coordinates
\xi
to
s,
we then have that (with slightly modified functions
\chi_{0},\chi_{1}
)

\displaystyle\mu^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}\iint F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-s_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\chi_{0}(s_{2})\,\eta ds_{2}\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3},

where we have again put
\Gamma:=(0,0,1).

Observe that (
10.2
) again allows to write
\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})
in a similar way as we did this in (
6.2
):

\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})=z_{1}^{n}\beta(z_{1})+s_{2}z_{1}^{m^{\prime}}\omega(z_{1})+s_{2}^{2}z_{1}q(z_{1},s_{2}),

where
x_{1}^{m^{\prime}}\omega(z_{1})
stands for a finite type function, if
m^{\prime}
is finite, and a flat function, if
m^{\prime}=\infty.
Note also that here
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}+m^{\prime}/n\geq 1.

Remark 10.2

(On the notion of effective multiplicity for
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{n-1}
-singularities)
.

A comparison between the identities (
6.2
) and (
10.2
) for
\breve{\phi}_{1}
suggests that at this stage we could as well have
introduced the notion of effective multiplicity
n_{e}
for functions
\phi
of type
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{n-1},
namely by means of the identity (
6.2
) for
\breve{\phi}_{1}.
Indeed, for such
\phi,
the edge
\breve{\gamma}_{1}
of the Newton polyhedron
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}_{1})
with right vertex
(n,0)
lies on the line
{\kappa}^{e}_{1}t_{1}+{\kappa}^{e}_{2}t_{2}=1,
with the weight

{\kappa}^{e}:=\big(\frac{1}{n},\frac{n-m}{n}\big),

and if we define the number
n_{e}
in same way by means of the edge
{\gamma}_{1}
as we did this for functions of type

{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}
in Subsection
1.2
, we find that here
n_{e}=m,
since the point
(m,1)
lies on the edge
e_{1}.
The according exponent
p_{e}
would then be given by
p_{e}=2m/(m+1).
But, note that here
p_{e}\leq h=2n/(n+2),
since
2m\leq n,
so
p_{e}
is not really relevant for
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{n-1}
-type singularities. This was the reason why we had introduced the notion of effective multiplicity only for
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{n-1}
-singularities, for which in addition a direct definition of
n_{e}
in terms of Newton polyhedra associated to
\phi
is available.

Recall, however, that in contrast to Section
6
, we now have

(10.5)

\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}<1/2.

Recall also that since
\phi
is of type
{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{+}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e},

p
does satisfy the following two assumptions:

(A1)

{\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}p(z_{1},s_{2})
consists of at least two distinct monomial terms, one of them of course being
n(n-1)z_{1}^{n-2}.

(A2)

If
{\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}p(z_{1},s_{2})
does not vanish of maximal possible order
n-2
along a real, non-trivial root of
{\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}p(z_{1},s_{2});

more precisely,
{\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}p(z_{1},s_{2})
is not of the form

(10.6)

{\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}p(z_{1},s_{2})=n(n-1)(z_{1}-cs_{2}^{d})^{n-2},

with
c\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and an integer exponent
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}.

Let us also recall from Remark
1.3
that

(10.7)

n<2n_{e},\quad\text{in particular}\quad n_{e}>2.

With our new function
\breve{\phi}_{1}
as defined in (
10.2
), we can now essentially follow the proof of Theorem
1.2
, however, with some important modifications and changes, and we shall therefore mostly concentrate on those.

To this end, we shall adopt the same notation, putting for instance again (compare (
6.7
),
(
6.8
))

\Phi_{0}(z_{1},s^{\prime}):=\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}z_{1},\qquad\Phi(z_{1},s_{2}):={\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}\Phi_{0}(z_{1},s^{\prime})={\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2}),

and shall use the same notation for the associated oscillatory integrals
J
and
F,
Newton polyhedra, and so forth.

It is important to observe that condition (A1) ensures that the Newton polyhedra of
\breve{\phi}_{1}
and of
\Phi
are closely related:

{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
is obtained by translating
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}_{1})
by
-2
in horizontal direction and then considering only the part with
t_{1}\geq 0
(compare Figure
7
).

Figure 7.
Comparison between
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}_{1})
and
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)

In particular, since
n_{e}>2,
the first edge
{\gamma}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
, whose endpoints are
(B_{0},A_{0})=(n-2,0)
and
(B_{1},A_{1}),
where
A_{1}\geq 1,
and the first edge
{\gamma}^{e}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}_{1}),
whose right endpoint is
(n,0),
are having the same modulus of slope

(10.8)

a_{1}=\frac{{\kappa}_{1}^{1}}{{\kappa}^{1}_{2}}=\frac{1}{n-n_{e}}.

Actually, a similar result holds true also for the other edges of
{\mathcal{N}}(\phi).
To this end, let us denote by
\breve{\kappa}^{l}
the weight associated to the
l
th edge
{\gamma}^{e}_{l}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\breve{\phi}_{1}).

Then

a_{l}=\frac{{\kappa}_{1}^{l}}{{\kappa}^{l}_{2}}=\frac{\breve{\kappa}_{1}^{l}}{\breve{\kappa}^{l}_{2}}\geq a_{1},\qquad l=1,\dots,L.

In particular, in a similar way as in Subsection
7.6
, by Lemma
7.4
this will now imply (for Step 1) that

(10.9)

\frac{A_{l}-1}{a_{l}}+B_{l}+2\leq n_{e}

for
l=1,\dots,L.

This is the analogue of the estimate (
7.21
), which had been crucial for the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

One should therefore basically think of simply replacing
m
by
n_{e}
in our proof of Theorem
1.2
in order to get a proof of Theorem
1.9
. However, this is, of course, an oversimplification, and there are also some important difference to be noticed, on which we shall concentrate subsequently.

11.
Analysis in Step 1 of the resolution algorithm

11.1.
L^{2}
- estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l}

Since our arguments in Subsection
7.3
did not make use of the special form of
\breve{\phi}_{1},
the
L^{2}
-estimates
(
7.9
) remain valid also here.

11.2.
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l},l=1,\dots,L,
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

11.2.1.
The case where
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})\not\equiv 0

This case behaves more like a simplified version of the Step 2 - situation. We shall therefore defer its discussion to the end of Section
12
.

11.2.2.
The case where
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})\equiv 0

Here, we can basically follow our discussion of the Step 1 - situation in Section
7
.
Observe first that all estimates up to (
7.14
) will again remain valid. However, after having applied Lemma
5.2
, the identity (
7.15
) for
F_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1}({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})
makes explicit use of the form of the phase
\breve{\phi}_{1},
as well as the oscillatory integral giving
\mu_{j,k,|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma}).
We shall see that this part will indeed require a different discussion here. The complete phase is now given by

\displaystyle 2^{-2k}\big[{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}\big]+\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-y_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-2k}\big[{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}\big]+p(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-y_{3}.

Recall also that here
Y_{1}:=2^{j}y_{1},
so that the condition
|Y_{1}|\lesssim 1
is equivalent to
|y_{1}|\lesssim 2^{-j}.

But, since
p
is
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous of degree
1,
we have

(11.1)

p(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})=2^{-k/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}p(2^{ka_{1}}y_{1},{\sigma}_{2}).

And, by (
10.5
), we may write
2^{-k/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}=2^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}k}2^{-2k},
for some
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0.
Moreover,

|2^{ka_{1}}y_{1}|=|2^{-j+ka_{1}}Y_{1}|\lesssim 2^{-j+ka_{1}}\leq 2^{-(j-ka_{l})},

and since we are on
E_{l},
by (
7.1
) we have that
j-ka_{l}\gtrsim 1.

Thus,
|2^{ka_{1}}y_{1}|\lesssim 1.

These estimates show that the term
p(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+\breve{\phi}_{\rm err}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
can be absorbed into the term

2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),
i.e., we may assume that the complete phase is of the form

2^{-2k}\big[{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})-2^{k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}\big]-y_{3},

where
B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})
has similar properties as
B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),
in particular
|B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\sim 1.

From here on, we can thus again follow the arguments from Subsection
7.4
, if we assume there that
{\omega}(y_{1})\equiv 0,
and arrive at the same
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
-estimates as in that subsection.

11.3.
L^{p}
- estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau_{l}}
for the contribution by
E_{l},l=1,\dots,L,
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

The previous discussions show that also the
L^{p}
-estimates (
7.17
) remain valid. Since, for the summation in
{\lambda}
of these estimates, only the condition
p>3/2
was needed, and since the subsequent summation over all
k\leq j/a,
with
a:=a_{l}\geq a_{1}
works as well,
we can thus eventually reduce to proving that the estimate

(11.2)

\sum\limits_{j\gg 1}\Big(2^{j\big[(\frac{A-1}{a}+B+2)(\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-1\big]}+2^{j\big[\frac{A}{a}+B+2)(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-1\big]}\Big)<\infty

still holds true.

But, by (
10.9
),

\frac{A-1}{a}+B+2\leq n_{e}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2}{p}-1<\frac{1}{n_{e}},

since
1/p<1/p_{e}=1/2+1/(2n_{e}),
so that we may assume that the exponent of the first summand in (
11.2
) is strictly negative, and thus we can sum in
j.

Similarly, by (
10.8
) and (
10.7
),

\frac{A}{a}+B+2\leq n_{e}+\frac{1}{a}\leq n_{e}+\frac{1}{a_{1}}=n_{e}+(n-n_{e})=n\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{2n_{e}}<\frac{1}{n},

so that we may assume that the exponent of the second summand in (
11.2
) is strictly negative too and we can again sum in
j.

11.4.
L^{p}
-estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau_{0}}
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

Here we need to argue in a different way in order to see that the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimate (
7.16
) still remains true.

Since
p
is a
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial function, we can write it in the form

p(y_{1},s_{2})=y_{1}^{n}\beta(y_{1})+s_{2}\tilde{\rho}(y_{1})+s_{2}^{2}\tilde{q}(y_{1},s_{2}),

where
\tilde{\rho}
and
\tilde{q}
are
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous polynomial functions. Suppose we had
\tilde{q}(0,0)\neq 0,
so that
\tilde{q}
would be a constant function. Then
s_{2}^{2}\tilde{q}(y_{1},s_{2})
would be
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous of degree
2\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=1,
which would contradict (
10.5
).
Hence we must have
\tilde{q}(0,0)=0.

Essentially in the same way we can show that

\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},s_{2})=y_{1}^{n}\beta(y_{1})+s_{2}\rho(y_{1})+s_{2}^{2}q(y_{1},s_{2}),

with analytic functions
\rho
and
q
such that
q(0,0)=0.
Thus, the complete phase can be written as

2^{-2k}\big[{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+{\sigma}_{2}^{2}q(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{k}{\sigma}_{2}(y_{2}-\rho(y_{1})\big]+y_{1}^{n}\beta(y_{1})-y_{3},

and since we may assume that
|q|\ll 1,
the term
{\sigma}_{2}^{2}q(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
can again be absorbed into the first term. Thus, we can indeed now
argue as in Subsection
7.4
, simply replacing the term
y_{1}^{m}{\omega}(y_{1})
in this argument by
\rho(y_{1}).

As in Subsection
7.7
, what then remains to show is that

\sum\limits_{j,k\gg 1:k\geq j/a_{1}}\Big((2^{jn-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}+(2^{jn})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}\Big)<\infty.

But, we can now essentially follow the arguments from Subsection
7.7
.
For instance, in the first summand, we can first sum over all
k\geq j/a_{1}
and arrive at the sum

\sum\limits_{j\gg 1}2^{j\big[(n-\frac{1}{a_{1}})(\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime})-1\big]}.

But,

n-\frac{1}{a_{1}}=n_{e},\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2}{p}-1<\frac{1}{n_{e}},

so that by choosing
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
sufficiently small we can indeed sum in
j.
The remaining arguments from Subsection
7.7
can easily be adapted in a similar way, since for
p>p_{e}
we still have

n(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})<n\frac{1}{2n_{e}}<n\frac{1}{n}=1.

Before we come to discuss the contributions by the homogeneous domains
D_{l},
we need to prove a crucial lemma on multiplicities of roots.

11.5.
On the condition
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1:
a multiplicity lemma

Lemma 11.1

(Multiplicity Lemma)
.

Let
P(y_{1},y_{2})
be a polynomial function which is
{\kappa}^{1}
-homogeneous of degree 1 and satisfies
P(y_{1},0)=y_{1}^{n-2},
where
0<{\kappa}^{1}_{2}\leq 1.
Define

n_{e}
as in Figure
8
, i.e.,

n_{e}-2=\frac{1-{\kappa}^{1}_{2}}{{\kappa}^{1}_{1}}.

Note that then
a:={\kappa}^{1}_{1}/{\kappa}^{1}_{2}
is the modulus of the slope of the line
L_{1}.
Assume that
P
has a non-trivial real root of multiplicity strictly greater then
n_{e}-2.

Then, either
P
is of the form

(11.3)

P(y_{1},y_{2})=(y_{1}-{\lambda}y_{2}^{p})^{n-2},\quad\text{where}\quad p\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0},{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\},

or all non-trivial real roots of
P
have multiplicity 1, and, more precisely,
P(y_{1},y_{2})
is of the form

P(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}^{\nu_{1}}(y_{1}^{q}-{\lambda}y_{2}^{p}),\quad\text{where}\quad p,q\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0},q\geq 2,\frac{p}{q}=a,{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}.

Figure 8.

Proof.

According to
[
IM11a
, Proposition 2.2]
,

P
can be written in the form

(11.4)

P(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\prod_{k}(y_{1}^{q}-{\lambda}_{k}y_{2}^{p})^{n_{k}},

with distinct
{\lambda}_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
and multiplicities
n_{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{0\},
with
\nu_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Let us put
N:=\sum\limits_{k}n_{k},
so that
n-2=\nu_{1}+Nq,
and note that
a=p/q.
Note also that the monomial of lowest degree in
y_{1}
in
P
is then of the form
cy_{1}^{\nu_{1}}y_{2}^{Np},
which implies that
(B_{1},A_{1})=(\nu_{1},Np).
But then

{\kappa}^{1}_{1}\nu_{1}+{\kappa}^{1}_{2}Np=1,\quad{\kappa}^{1}_{1}=a{\kappa}^{1}_{2},

hence

{\kappa}^{1}_{2}=1/(a\nu_{1}+Np).

This implies that

(11.5)

n_{e}-2=\frac{1-{\kappa}^{1}_{2}}{{\kappa}^{1}_{1}}=\frac{1/{\kappa}^{1}_{2}-1}{a}=\frac{a\nu_{1}+Np-1}{a}.

Now, by our assumption, there is some
k^{\prime}
such that
{\lambda}_{k^{\prime}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and
n_{k^{\prime}}>n_{e}-2.
In particular, (
11.5
) implies that

(11.6)

N\geq n_{k^{\prime}}>\nu_{1}+\frac{Np-1}{a},

hence

(11.7)

(p-a)N<1-a\nu_{1}.

(i) If
q=1,
then
a=p,
so that by (
11.7
) we have
p\nu_{1}<1,
hence
\nu_{1}=0.
Thus, by (
11.4
)

P(y_{1},y_{2})=\prod_{k}(y_{1}-{\lambda}_{k}y_{2}^{p})^{n_{k}}.

But, by (
11.6
),
n_{k^{\prime}}>N-1/p,
hence
n_{k^{\prime}}=N=n-2.
This implies

P(y_{1},y_{2})=(y_{1}-{\lambda}_{k^{\prime}}y_{2}^{p})^{n-2}.

(i) If
q\geq 2,
then by (
11.7
),

N<\frac{1-a\nu_{1}}{p-a}\leq\frac{1}{p-a}=\frac{1}{p(1-1/q)}\leq\frac{1}{p/2}=\frac{2}{p}\leq 2.

Thus,
N=1,
and without loss of generality we may assume that
N=n_{1}=1.
This implies that

P(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}^{\nu_{1}}(y_{1}^{q}-{\lambda}_{1}y_{2}^{p}),

hence all non-trivial real roots of
P
are simple.

Q.E.D.

11.6.
On the contributions by the homogeneous domains
D_{l},l=1,\dots,L

As in Subsection
11.6
, the case
l=1
requires special considerations. To this end,
recall that the
{\kappa}^{e}
-principal part of
\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2})
is the polynomial
p(z_{1},s_{2}),
and accordingly

P(z_{1},s_{2}):={\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}p(z_{1},s_{2})

is the
{\kappa}^{1}
-principal part of
\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})={\partial}_{z_{1}}^{2}\breve{\phi}_{1}(z_{1},s_{2}),
which is associated to the first edge
{\gamma}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi).

Moreover, if
a=a_{1},
then every non-trivial real root of
P
will be of the form
cs_{2}^{a},
with
c\neq 0.

Now, suppose again that we change in a narrow homogeneous neighborhood
D_{1}^{c}
of this root to the coordinates
\tilde{z}:=z_{1}-cs_{2}^{a},
so that we pass from
\Phi
to
\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2}):=\Phi(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a},s_{2}),
and from
P
to
\tilde{P}(\tilde{z},s_{2}):=P(\tilde{z}+cs_{2}^{a},s_{2}).

Observe first that Subcase (ii) of Case 2 from Subsection
11.6
will appear here if and only if
\tilde{P}
will be of the form
c_{0}z_{1}^{n-2},
i.e., if the edge
{\gamma}_{1}
will “shrink” to its right vertex
(\tilde{B}_{0},\tilde{A}_{0})=(B_{0},A_{0})=(n-2,0)
under the change of coordinates. But, this would mean that

P(z_{1},s_{2})=n(n-1)(z_{1}-cs_{2}^{a})^{n-2}.

However, this is excluded by our assumption (A2) (cf. (
10.6
)).

Thus, again, only Subcase (i) of Case 2 can appear (compare Remark
7.7
(a)), so that the first compact edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi})

lies on the same line
L_{1}
as
{\gamma}_{1}.
Denote again by
(\tilde{B}_{0},\tilde{A}_{0})=(B_{0},A_{0})=(n-2,0)
its right endpoint, and by
(\tilde{B}_{1},\tilde{A}_{1})
its left endpoint. Then
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is associated to
\tilde{P}.

Assume now that we had
\tilde{\nu}_{1}=\tilde{B}_{1}>n^{e}-2,
where
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
denotes the power of
y_{1}
of the first factor in (
11.4
), if we look at

\tilde{P}
in place of
P.
But that would mean that
P(z_{1},s_{2})
would have a non-trivial real root of order strictly greater than
n^{e}-2,
so that we could apply Lemma
11.1
to
P(z_{1},s_{2})
and conclude that either

P(z_{1},s_{2})=(z_{1}-cs_{2}^{p})^{n-2},

(compare (
11.3
)), or all non-trivial real roots of
P
have multiplicity 1. The first case is, however, again excluded by assumption (A1).

In conclusion, we see that either all of the non-trivial real roots of
P
have multiplicity 1 (as it had been in Section
7
), or we must have

\tilde{B}_{1}\leq n^{e}-2.
But, this is equivalent to having
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1
(compare Figure
8
).

From here on, all of the remaining arguments from Section
7
carry over to our present situation.

12.
Analysis in Step 2 of the resolution algorithm

Fixing again
l_{1}\in\{1,\dots,L\}
as in Section
8
, putting accordingly

a:=a_{l_{1}}\geq a_{1},

and proceeding in the same way as before, we are again led to considering changes of coordinates of the form

\tilde{z}:=z_{1}-w(s_{2}),

where
w(s_{2})=cs_{2}^{a}
(or a full root
r_{0}
with this leading term). The function
\Phi(z_{1})
will then lead to the function
\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z}).
Note, however, one difference in the effect on the function
F:

Due to the presence of the extra term
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})
in the phase for
F({\lambda},s_{2},s_{3},y_{1})
in (
10.4
), we should here indeed better define

\Phi_{0}(x_{1},s^{\prime}):=\breve{\phi}_{1}(x_{1},s_{2})+s_{1}(x_{1}+\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}))
, and therefore

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{0,0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\breve{\phi}_{1}(\tilde{z}+w(s_{2}),s_{2}),

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{0}(\tilde{z},s^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(\tilde{z}+w(s_{2}),s_{2})=\tilde{\Phi}_{0,0}(\tilde{z},s_{2})+s_{1}\tilde{z}+s_{1}\big(w(s_{2})+\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})\big).

We shall therefore also introduce the function

W(s_{2}):=w(s_{2})+\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}),

besides the function
w(s_{2}),
in Step 2, and similarly also in every higher order step.

Now, either
W(s_{2})\equiv 0,
but then we can proceed in Step 2 (or higher) essentially in the same way as in Step 1, so that we shall ignore this case in the sequel.

Or,
W(s_{2})\not\equiv 0,
and then

|W(s_{2})|\sim s_{2}^{a^{\prime}}

for some rational
a^{\prime}>0,
since
W(s_{2})
is fractionally analytic.

We remark that the value of
a^{\prime}
can depend on the step of the resolution algorithm, since in higher steps of our algorithm we shall have to replace the function
w(s_{2})
by the leading jet of some higher-order sub-cluster of roots. Observe also: since
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})
is analytic, we have that

|\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})|\sim s_{2}^{a_{0}}
for some integer
a_{0}\geq 1.
The following lemma is then immediate:

Lemma 12.1
.

Either
a^{\prime}\geq 1,
or
a^{\prime}<1,
and then
a<1
and hence
1>a^{\prime}=a\geq a_{1}.

Next, as in Subsection
8.2
, we can reduce to estimating the contributions by the
\tilde{\kappa}^{l}
- homogeneous domains

\tilde{D}_{l}
of the form

\tilde{D}_{l}:=\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}\leq|\tilde{z}|\leq 2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}\},\quad l\leq\tilde{L},

and the transition domains of the form

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{l}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):2^{M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l+1}}<|\tilde{z}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{l}}\},\quad l\leq\tilde{L}-1,

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\tilde{L}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\tilde{z},s_{2}):|\tilde{z}|<2^{-M}s_{2}^{\tilde{a}_{\tilde{L}}}\}.

More precisely, in Case 1 where
c\notin\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
according to Remark
7.5
we just need the transition domain
\tilde{E}_{\tilde{L}},
where
\tilde{L}=l_{1}.

In Case 2, where
c\in\mathcal{C}_{l_{1}},
we need to distinguish two subcases: if we are in Subcase (i), then
according to Remark
7.6
(a) we shall need the transition domains
\tilde{E}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}\geq 1
and the homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}+1\geq 2.
Moreover, in analogy with Remark
8.1
(a), we may and shall treat the case where
l_{1}=1
and where all non-trivial real roots of
P
have multiplicity 1 separartely,
so that we shall here even assume that
l_{1}\geq 2.

In Subcase (ii) of Case 2, according to Remark
7.7
we may assume that
l_{1}\geq 2
and shall then need the transition domains
\tilde{E}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}-1\geq 1
and the homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1}\geq 2.

Moreover, our discussion in Subsection
11.6
shows that under these assumptions we may assume that
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1,

and that the edges
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
and
{\gamma}_{1}
are supported on the same line
\tilde{L}_{1}=L_{1}
of “slope”
\tilde{a}_{1}=a_{1},
so that, by Lemma
7.4
, we now have (in analogy with the identities (
8.2
) and (
8.3
)) that

(12.1)

\frac{\tilde{A}_{1}-1}{\tilde{a}_{1}}+\tilde{B}_{1}+2=n_{e},

(even when
\tilde{A}_{1}<1
), and

(12.2)

\frac{\tilde{A}_{l}-1}{\tilde{a}_{l}}+\tilde{B}_{l}+2\leq n_{e},\quad l=1,\dots,\tilde{L}

(compare with (
10.9
)).

Having fixed
l_{1}
and
l
according to these assumptions, we shall put

\tilde{a}:=\tilde{a}_{l}.

Note that if we are in Case 1, or Subcase (i) of Case 2, then
\tilde{a}\geq\tilde{a}_{l_{1}}=a,
but in Subcase (ii) it may also happen that
\tilde{a}<a
(for instance if
l=l_{1}-1,
where
\tilde{a}=a_{l_{1}-1}<a_{l_{1}}=a
).

12.1.
Resolution of singularity on the transition domain
\tilde{E}_{l}

Fix
\tilde{E}_{l}
with
l\geq l_{1},
respectively
l\geq l_{1}-1\geq 1
if we are in the Subcase (ii) of Case 2. Arguing as in Subsection
8.3
, we here find that

(12.3)

\tilde{\Phi}_{0}(\tilde{z},s^{\prime})=\tilde{U}(\tilde{z},s_{2})s_{2}^{\tilde{A}_{l}}\tilde{z}^{\tilde{B}_{l}+2}+s_{1}(\tilde{z}+W(s_{2}))+\tilde{z}\tilde{g}(s_{2})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}).

And, after applying the change of coordinates
\tilde{z}=2^{-j}z,\ s_{2}=2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}
putting
A:=\tilde{A}_{l},B:=\tilde{B}_{l}
and

d_{j,k}:=2^{-kA-j(B+2)},

assuming that (
8.6
) holds true, we obtain

\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}=d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+s_{1}(2^{-j}z+W(s_{2})-y_{1})+2^{-j}z\tilde{g}(s_{2})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}),

where
U
has similar properties as in (
7.7
). Let us then write

W(s_{2})=2^{-ka^{\prime}}{\sigma}_{2}^{a^{\prime}}\tilde{W}({\sigma}_{2}),

so that
|\tilde{W}|\sim 1,
whereas all derivatives of
\tilde{W}
can be assumed to be very small. We shall use the following abbreviations:

\displaystyle Y_{1}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{ka^{\prime}}y_{1},

(12.4)

\displaystyle\gamma({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{2}^{a^{\prime}}\tilde{W}({\sigma}_{2})-Y_{1},

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle 2^{j-ka^{\prime}}.

These allow us to re-write again as in (
8.10
) and (
8.11
)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+2^{-j}s_{1}\big(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z\big)+2^{-j}z\tilde{g}(s_{2})+\tilde{h}(s_{2})

and

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=2^{-j}\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1})}\chi_{0}(s_{1})\chi_{1}(z)\,\eta dzds_{1}

\displaystyle=2^{-j}e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\tilde{h}(s_{2})}\,\int{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-j}[K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+z]\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(d_{j,k}U^{s}(z,{\sigma}_{2}){\sigma}_{2}^{A}z^{B+2}+2^{-j}z\tilde{g}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})\big)}\chi_{1}(z)\eta dz,

where
z\sim 1\sim{\sigma}_{2},
and where
{\varphi}:=\widehat{\chi_{0}}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
is rapidly decaying.

12.2.
L^{2}
- estimation of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
\tilde{E}_{l}

The
L^{2}
-estimates (
8.12
) again remain valid here.

12.3.
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
\tilde{E}_{l}
when
{\lambda}\geq 2^{j}

Again, we can essentially follow the discussions from Subsection
8.3
, and shall only highlight places where the arguments require modifications. Recall from (
8.9
) that

\tilde{h}(s_{2})=\breve{\phi}_{1}(w(s_{2}),s_{2}),\quad\tilde{g}(s_{2})={\partial}_{z_{1}}\breve{\phi}_{1}(w(s_{2}),s_{2}).

Thus, if we put again

\tilde{g}^{s}({\sigma}_{2}):=\tilde{g}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),\quad\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2}):=\tilde{h}(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),

we see that

\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})=\breve{\phi}_{1}(2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}),\quad\tilde{g}({\sigma}_{2})={\partial}_{z_{1}}\breve{\phi}_{1}(2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}).

But, (
11.1
) implies that here

p(2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})=2^{-k/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}p(2^{-k(a-a_{1})}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),{\sigma}_{2}),

where
a-a_{1}\geq 0,
and, by (
10.5
),
2^{-k/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}=2^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}k}2^{-2k},
for some
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0.

Similarly, since
{\partial}_{y_{1}}p
is
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}
-homogeneous of degree
1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1},
we see that

{\partial}_{y_{1}}p(2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})=2^{-k\frac{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}}{\partial}_{y_{1}}p(2^{-k(a-a_{1})}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),{\sigma}_{2}).

And, if we again assume that
j\gtrsim k\tilde{a},
recalling that
\tilde{a}\geq a_{1},
we see that also

2^{-j}2^{-k\frac{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}}\lesssim 2^{-k/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}.

These estimates imply that

\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})=O(2^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}k}2^{-2k}),\quad 2^{-j}\tilde{g}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})=O(2^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}k}2^{-2k})\text{ for }j\gtrsim k\tilde{a},

which means that
we can absorb these terms (as functions of
{\sigma}_{2}
) into the term
s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})=2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
of the amplitude of the oscillatory integral (
8.5
).

From here on, we can simply follow the arguments for the reduction to the case
K|{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})|\lesssim 1
in Subsection

8.5.1
, distinguishing now between the
Subcase 2.a:
{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}\lesssim 1
, and

Subcase 2.b:
{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}\gg 1,
and replacing accordingly
a
by
a^{\prime}
in these steps of proof.

Note that the main difference compared to the previous discussion lies in how we get rid of the terms
\tilde{h}^{s}({\sigma}_{2})
and

2^{-j}\tilde{g}^{s}({\sigma}_{2}),
which turns out to be even a bit easier here than in Subsection
8.5.1
.

Next, we can also easily adapt the arguments which led to (
7.15
) and the identity

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})+2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3}\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))\,\eta d{\sigma}_{2}ds_{3}.

But, due to (
11.1
), we may absorb the term
\breve{\phi}_{1}(y_{1},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
into
2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})
within the amplitude, so that without loss of generality we may assume that

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k,K|{\gamma}|\lesssim 1}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{3}{2}}2^{-k}\int e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}\big(2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3}\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})\chi_{1}(s_{3})\,\chi_{0}(K{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1}))\,\eta d{\sigma}_{2}ds_{3}.

This is essentially as in Subsection
8.5.1
, if we assume there that
{\omega}(y_{1})\equiv 0,
and replace
a
by
a^{\prime}
in these arguments.

In this way, combining all these estimates, we see that we here obtain the following analogue to estimate (
8.24
):

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{1+{\varepsilon}\to 1+{\varepsilon}}\lesssim{\lambda}^{1+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j-k}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-j}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-k}+{\lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}}2^{-ka^{\prime}}.

By means of interpolation with our
L^{2}
- estimate (
8.12
) and summation in
{\lambda}
we arrive then at the following analogue to
(
8.25
):
Arguing as in Subsection
7.5
, we see that we can sum over all dyadic
{\lambda}
s and obtain that, for every
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0,

(12.5)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{\lambda}\gg 1}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}\|_{p\to p}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1}2^{-k})^{\frac{2}{p}-1+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}+(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-j}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-ka^{\prime}(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}.

Putting again
A:=\tilde{A}_{l},B:=\tilde{B}_{l},
we recall from (
12.1
) and (
12.2
) that

\frac{A-1}{\tilde{a}}+B+2\leq n_{e},\quad\text{and}\quad d_{j,k}^{-1}=2^{kA+j(B+2)}.

Moreover, since we are on
\tilde{E}_{l},
by (
8.6
) we have
j\geq k\tilde{a}.

This shows that we can argue in exactly the same way as in Subsection
7.6
in order to show that we can sum the first two terms on the right-hand side of (
12.5
) over all
k
s and
j
s such that
j\geq k\tilde{a}.

We are thus left with showing that

(12.6)

\sum\limits_{j,k\gg 1:j\geq k\tilde{a}}\Big((d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-k(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}+(d_{j,k}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\,2^{-ka^{\prime}(\frac{2}{p}-1)}2^{-j(2-\frac{2}{p})}\Big)<\infty,

provided
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
is sufficiently small.

The first sum in (
12.6
) can be estimated in the same way as in Subsection
7.6
- we just have to replace
m
be
n_{e}
in the argument.

As for the second sum, note that the terms in the second sum can be estimated by the terms of the first sum, provided
a^{\prime}\geq 1.

This leaves as with the case where
a^{\prime}<1.
Then, by Lemma
12.1
,
a^{\prime}=a\geq a_{1},
and as before we can distinguish between the cases
\tilde{a}\geq a,
and
\tilde{a}<a.

In the first case, we can follow the estimates from Subsection
7.6
, with
m
replaced by
n_{e},
and only need to verify that
(n-n_{e})a\geq 1.
But,
a\geq a_{1}=1/(n-n_{e})
by (
10.8
), so that the desired estimate holds true.

In the second case where
\tilde{a}<a,
we can again argue as in Subsection
7.6
, since we also have that
\tilde{a}\geq a_{1}.

This completes the proof of (
12.6
).

12.4.
L^{p}
- estimates of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,k}^{\lambda}
for the contribution by
E_{l}
and
\tilde{E}_{l}
when
{\lambda}<2^{j}

Arguing in a similar way as in Subsection
8.7
, we again have the
L^{2}
-estimate (
8.28
).

As for the
L^{1+{\varepsilon}}
-estimates, let us first look at the
Step 1-situation and the contribution by
E_{l}
.
Again, we shall first assume that
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})\equiv 0.

Arguing in a similar way as in Subsection
8.7
, we may here again assume that

\Phi_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}=-s_{1}y_{1}.

We can therefore adopt our discussion from Subsection
8.7
verbatim, in which we only made use of the assumption
p>3/2.

Let us next look at the
Step 2-situation and the contribution by
\tilde{E}_{l}
:

By (
12.3
), we can here assume that

\tilde{\Phi}_{0}^{s}(z,s_{1},{\sigma}_{2})-s_{1}y_{1}=s_{1}2^{-ka^{\prime}}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})+\tilde{h}(s_{2}),

where
z\sim 1\sim{\sigma}_{2},
with
Y_{1},K
and
{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})
now defined by (
12.4
). But, the term
\tilde{h}(s_{2})
can again be absorbed into the term
s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})
and shall henceforth be dropped.

We then find that

\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})=2^{-j}\,\int{\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})\big)\chi_{1}(z)\eta dz,

where
{\varphi}:=\widehat{\chi_{0}}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
is rapidly decaying, and

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-k}\times

\displaystyle\iint\tilde{F}_{j,k}({\lambda},2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2},s_{3},y_{1})e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(s_{2}^{2}B(s_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2})d{\sigma}_{2}\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3}.

Assume first that
{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}\geq 1.
We can here argue in the same way as in Subsection
8.7
, only with
a
replaced by
a^{\prime},
since we only made use of the assumption
p>3/2.

Assume next that
{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}<1.
Since we had already seen that we may assume that
\tilde{h}^{s}\equiv 0,
we can again follow the reasoning from Subsection
8.7
, and write

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}^{\lambda}(y+{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\lambda}^{\frac{5}{2}}2^{-j-k}\times

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\iint e^{-i{\lambda}s_{3}(2^{-2k}{\sigma}_{2}^{2}B(2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})-2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2}y_{2}-y_{3})}\chi_{1}({\sigma}_{2}){\varphi}\big(s_{3}{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})\big)\eta d{\sigma}_{2}\,\chi_{1}(s_{3})ds_{3}.

But note that
{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}{\gamma}({\sigma}_{2},Y_{1})={\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}{\sigma}_{2}^{a^{\prime}}\tilde{W}({\sigma}_{2})-{\lambda}y_{1},
where the first term is harmless, since
{\lambda}2^{-ka^{\prime}}\leq 1.

Thus we may again argue as in the Step 1 situation discussed before.

This concludes our discussion of the case where
{\lambda}<2^{j}.

12.4.1.
Completion of Step 1: the case where
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2})\not\equiv 0

This case can be handled like a Step 2 case, where
formally
a=+\infty
and
W(s_{2}):=\tilde{\alpha}(s_{2}),
so that
a^{\prime}=a_{0}\geq 1,
since
a_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}.
We leave the details to the interested reader.

12.5.
On the contributions by the homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l}

These can easily be handled in analogy to our discussions in Subsection
12.5
, with the obvious modifications of the functions
W(s_{2})
as indicated at the beginning of Section
12
.

12.6.
The case where
\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}=0

In view of Remark
10.1
, this case behaves very much like the cases explained before where we would have stopped our resolution algorithm, after applying a change of coordinates by subtracting a complete real roots. It can therefore be handled in a similar way as we did in such situations. We leave the details to the interested reader.

13.
Higher steps of the resolution algorithm and conclusion of the proof of Theorem
1.9

Here, we can again just proceed as we did in the corresponding Section
9
for the proof of Theorem
1.2
; this completes the proof of Theorem
1.9
.

14.
Proof of the Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger conjecture for surfaces of class
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}_{n-1}

In this final section, we shall briefly sketch how the proof of Theorem
1.9
can be modified in order to prove the following

Proposition 14.1
.

Assume that
S
is the graph of
1+\phi,
and accordingly
x^{0}=(0,0,1),
where
\phi
is analytic and of type

{\mathcal{A}}_{n-1}^{e}
. Then, if the density
\rho
is supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{0},
the condition

p>\max\{3/2,h,{2n}/(n+1)\}=max\{3/2,{2n}/(n+1)\}
is sufficient for
{\mathcal{M}}
to be
L^{p}
-bounded.

Details are again left to the interested reader. Recall that this result will complete the proof of the Iosevich-Sawyer-Seeger conjecture for arbitrary analytic hypersurfaces in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.

Proof.

We again assume that
(y_{1},y_{2})
are line-adapted coordinates in which (
1.9
) and (
1.11
) hold true, so that

p(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}^{n}+c_{1}y_{1}y_{2}^{\beta+2},\qquad\text{with}\ \beta\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Since
{\partial}_{y_{1}}^{2}p(y_{1},y_{2})=n(n-1)y_{1}^{n-2},
we now find that the first edge
{\gamma}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\Phi)
has a modulus of slope
a_{1}^{\prime}
such that
+\infty\geq a_{1}^{\prime}>a_{1}=1/(n-n_{e}).
This first edge will intersect the line
s_{2}=1
at a point
(n^{\prime}_{e}-2,1),

so that
a^{\prime}_{1}=1/(n-n^{\prime}_{e}),
and

(14.7)

\frac{n}{2}<n_{e}<n^{\prime}_{e}\leq n.

In Step 1 of the resolution algorithm, we can then proceed as in the proof of Theorem
1.9
, only with
a_{1}
replaced by
a^{\prime}_{1},
and
n_{e}
replaced by
n^{\prime}_{e}.
Note that now
a\geq a^{\prime}_{1},
and
1/p-1/2<1/(2n)\leq 1/(2n^{\prime}_{e}),
so that the discussion in Subsection
11.3
carries over directly. Similarly, since now

n-\frac{1}{a^{\prime}_{1}}=n-(n-n^{\prime}_{e})=n^{\prime}_{e},\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2}{p}-1<\frac{1}{n^{\prime}_{e}},

also the discussion in Subsection
11.4
carries over, since now we can therein assume that
k\geq j/a^{\prime}_{1}.

In Step 2 (and higher later on), recall that we had put
z_{1}:=y_{1}.
We first note that here we apply changes of coordinates of the form

\tilde{z}:=z_{1}-w(s_{2}),

where now
|w(s_{2})|\sim s_{2}^{a},\quad\text{with}\quad a\geq a^{\prime}_{1}.
It is important that the control of the functions
\tilde{g}
and
\tilde{h}
that we had discussed in Subsection
12.3
still works here: recall that

p(2^{-ka}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),2^{-k}{\sigma}_{2})=2^{-k/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}p(2^{-k(a-a_{1})}{\sigma}_{2}^{a}\tilde{w}({\sigma}_{2}),{\sigma}_{2}),

and since
a-a_{1}\geq a^{\prime}_{1}-a_{1}>0,
we again can conclude that
2^{-k(a-a_{1})}\lesssim 1.
Similarly, since now
j\geq ka\geq ka^{\prime}_{1}\geq ka_{1},
we still have that

2^{-j}2^{-k\frac{1-\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{1}}{\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}}\lesssim 2^{-k/\tilde{\kappa}^{e}_{2}}.

Thus, we can basically proceed as before, but there is one
major difference:

In our application of the Multiplicity Lemma
11.1
for the study of the contribution by the domain
D_{1},
we can no longer exclude the first possibility (
11.3
), i.e., it may happen that

P(z_{1},s_{2})=(z_{1}-c_{1}s_{2}^{p})^{n-2},\qquad c_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\},p\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1},

vanishes of maximal order
n-2
along a nontrivial real root. Note that here we would perform a change of coordinates

\tilde{z}:=z_{1}-w(s_{2}),
with
w(s_{2}):=c_{1}s_{2}^{a^{\prime}_{1}},
where
a^{\prime}_{1}=p
is a natural number, in order to pass from
\Phi
to

\tilde{\Phi}.
This is now an analytic change of coordinates, so that
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi})
is still a Newton polyhedron (and not just Newton-Puiseux), which implies that automatically we will have
\tilde{A}_{1}\geq 1.

However, since in the new coordinates,
\tilde{P}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=\tilde{z}^{n-2},
the first edge
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{\Phi})
will have a (modulus of) slope
a^{\prime\prime}_{1}
such that
\infty\geq a^{\prime\prime}_{1}>a^{\prime}_{1}>a_{1},
and we shall be reduced to a region of the form

\tilde{D}_{1}^{c_{1}}:=\{|\tilde{z}|<{\varepsilon}s_{2}^{a^{\prime}_{1}}\},

in which we shall again have to devise transition domains
\tilde{E}_{l}
and homogeneous domains
\tilde{D}_{l},
including the case
l=1.

For all other cases in our application of Lemma
11.1
, the discussion at the end of Subsection
11.6
carries over.

Now, if we proceed to higher steps of our resolution algorithm, we shall either no longer encounter the first possibility (
11.3
) from a certain step on, so that we can directly adapt our discussion from the proof of Theorem
1.9
,
or the first possibility (
11.3
) will arise at
every
Step j, within the corresponding domain
D_{1}^{(j)}.

However, when
j
is sufficiently large, say when
j\geq j_{0},
then
D_{1}^{(j)}
will contain exactly one real root, of maximal multiplicity
n-2,
(compare the discussion in Section
9
), if we consider this domain in our original coordinates
(z_{1},s_{2}),
and we see that this root
r(s_{2})
will have a Taylor series expansion of the form

r(s_{2})=c_{1}s_{2}^{a^{\prime}_{1}}+c_{2}s_{2}^{a^{\prime}_{2}}+\cdots+c_{j}s_{2}^{a^{\prime}_{j}}+\cdots,

with natural numbers
a^{\prime}_{1}<a^{\prime}_{2}<\cdots<a^{\prime}_{j}<\cdots,
and real coefficients
c_{j}.
Moreover, we may then write

\Phi(z_{1},s_{2})=V(z_{1},s_{2})(z_{1}-r(s_{2}))^{n-2},
with
|V|\sim 1.
As what we always did in such a situation, we can now perform the analytic change of coordinates

\tilde{z}:=z_{1}-r(s_{2}),

and find that in these new coordinates
(\tilde{z},s_{2}),
we have

\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{z},s_{2})=\tilde{V}(\tilde{z},s_{2})\tilde{z}^{n-2}.

As in earlier situations where we had stopped the resolution of singularities algorithm, we now see that we have arrived at a good resolution of singularities and can apply our usual arguments to prove
L^{p}
- boundedness of
{\mathcal{M}}
for

p>\max\{3/2,2n/(n+1)\}
(note that here
n^{\prime}_{e}=n
if we define
n^{\prime}_{e}
with respect to these new coordinates).

Q.E.D.
